Punishment is most effective at high commitment thresholds and with strong punishment, while reward can sustain cooperation at any threshold if the reward is strong enough. Intermediate thresholds require less severe punishment to sustain cooperation. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to cooperation rates and strategy dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that punishment can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs with commitment, especially at high commitment thresholds and with strong punishment. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes. The results suggest that, in similar designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested or quantified in terms of payoff.

- source: Toriumi, F., Yamamoto, H., & Okada, I. (2016). Exploring an Effective Incentive System on a Groupware. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based simulations of public-goods-like meta-sanction games, systems with first-order punishment and appropriate second-order sanctions (such as punishing non-punishers or rewarding punishers) can achieve and robustly sustain high cooperation rates. In contrast, systems that allow first-order rewards without second-order incentives for rewarding behavior can actually impede the formation of cooperative regimes. The cooperation rate is highly sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio of sanctions and the discount factor for higher-order sanctions. The results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: it shows that the structure of the punishment and reward system (including the presence or absence of second-order incentives) strongly affects the cooperation rate in public-goods-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that enabling punishment (with appropriate cost/benefit ratios and possibly second-order sanctions) can increase cooperation rates, but the translation to efficiency is not quantified. The results also warn that adding rewards without second-order incentives can reduce cooperation, which may imply lower efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2025). Effects of punishment driven by inequity aversion on promoting cooperation in public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment driven by inequity aversion can promote the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when punishment costs are low and fines are high. Inequity-averse cooperators (IACs) can invade defectors and dominate the population under favorable parameter regimes, but their effectiveness is limited by internal conflict (punishing other cooperators or IACs) and the presence of non-punishing cooperators. Inequity-averse defectors (IADs) can also promote cooperation by forming protective barriers around cooperators, reducing the ability of defectors to invade. The effectiveness of punishment depends on the ratio of fine to cost, the synergy factor (MPCR), and the spatial structure. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency or total payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms—specifically, those based on inequity aversion—can increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games, particularly when punishment is not too costly and fines are substantial. The findings suggest that the design of punishment (who is targeted, how costs and fines scale) and the spatial structure of interactions are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. Instead, it supports the idea that punishment can change the behavioral composition of the group, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related models, but this link is not demonstrated here.

- source: Kaiping, G. A., Jacobs, G. S., Cox, S. J., & Sluckin, T. J. (2014). Nonequivalence of updating rules in evolutionary games under high mutation rates. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with punishment, the choice of updating rule (BP, DP, LD) can dramatically change the long-run frequencies of defectors, cooperators, and punishers. For the same parameter values, the birth-death process leads to a population dominated by defectors, while the death-birth process results in a majority of cooperative strategies (cooperators and punishers). Link dynamics yields intermediate results. These differences arise even in well-mixed populations and are especially pronounced when mutation rates are not negligible and when more than two strategies interact. The paper does not report group efficiency or payoff, but the shift from defector to cooperator/punisher dominance implies a likely increase in group welfare under some updating rules.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the updating rule (a feature of evolutionary dynamics, not typically manipulated in lab experiments) can be a major moderator of the effect of punishment on cooperation and, by implication, on efficiency. In public goods games with punishment, the same game design (including punishment parameters) can yield very different long-run outcomes depending on the updating rule: punishment may be ineffective (defector-dominated) under one rule and highly effective (cooperator/punisher-dominated) under another. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as indicating that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly sensitive to the evolutionary dynamics assumed, especially in theoretical or agent-based models. For empirical prediction, this suggests caution in generalizing across studies with different evolutionary assumptions.

- source: Quan, J., Guo, H. T., & Wang, X. J. (2022). Impact of reputation-based switching strategy between punishment and social exclusion on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial public goods game. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that conditional punishment based on reputation (switching between fines and exclusion) is more effective at promoting cooperation than traditional punishment alone. Higher reputation thresholds (stricter punishment), higher fines, and lower exclusion costs all lead to higher frequencies of cooperation. The enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) also strongly moderates the emergence and stability of cooperation. The model shows phase transitions between full defection and full cooperation, and regions where cooperation and defection coexist. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly, focusing instead on behavioral outcomes (strategy frequencies).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of punishment mechanisms (including conditionality based on reputation, the severity of fines, and the cost of exclusion) can substantially affect the frequency of cooperation in spatial PGGs. While the findings are highly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on cooperation rates, they are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency or group payoff, since these are not reported. The results suggest that, in similar spatial PGGs, enabling punishment (especially with flexible, reputation-based mechanisms) is likely to increase cooperation rates, which would generally be expected to improve efficiency, but the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred from this paper.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Li, B. F., Li, C., Li, H. B., & Zheng, Z. M. (2022). Two-layer network model of public goods games with intervention and corruption. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment (umpire intervention) always increases the fraction of cooperators compared to no intervention, even if all umpires are corrupt and only accept bribes. The fraction of fair umpires, the spatial distribution of umpires, the punishment fine, and the bribe size all have strong effects on cooperation rates. In particular, higher bribe costs and higher punishment fines both increase cooperation, but the effect of bribe cost is more pronounced. The model is robust to network structure (square lattice vs. scale-free). However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of a punishment mechanism (even if imperfect or corrupt) increases cooperation rates in PGG-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcome, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates as a function of punishment design, but not for predicting group welfare or surplus. The model's detailed exploration of punishment cost, effectiveness, and corruption is informative for mechanism design, but the lack of payoff reporting is a key limitation for efficiency prediction.

- source: Benjamin, C., & Sarkar, S. (2019). Triggers for cooperative behavior in the thermodynamic limit: A case study in Public goods game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in the thermodynamic limit of the two-player PGG, the prevalence of cooperation (as measured by game magnetization) is strongly affected by the reward for providing, the cost of the public good, and the presence and magnitude of punishment for defection. Specifically, increasing punishment shifts the critical point for cooperation to lower reward values, making cooperation more prevalent. Increasing cost shifts the critical point to higher reward values, making defection more prevalent. The model predicts phase transitions in the dominant strategy as these parameters vary. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that punishment increases the fraction of cooperators, which would generally be associated with higher efficiency in standard PGGs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in the thermodynamic limit of a two-player PGG, the presence and magnitude of punishment increases the prevalence of cooperation (i.e., more players choose to provide), while higher cost suppresses cooperation. Although the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the increase in cooperation due to punishment suggests that, in standard PGGs, enabling punishment would increase efficiency, especially when the cost of the public good is not too high. The findings are most directly applicable to settings with two players, continuous contribution choices, and no communication, and may be less directly transferable to finite or empirical settings. The results are useful for predicting the qualitative effect of punishment on cooperation and, by extension, on efficiency, but do not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Feng, S. A., Liu, X. S., & Dong, Y. D. (2022). Limited punishment pool may promote cooperation in the public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The introduction of a limited punishment pool in a public goods game can effectively suppress defectors and promote cooperation, especially when the pool's capacity is set within an optimal range. If the punishment pool is too small, defectors persist; if it is too large, punishers crowd out cooperators. The model shows that the right balance in punishment pool capacity maximizes the prevalence of cooperation. The paper does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but notes that the 'overall income' is higher in certain parameter ranges where both cooperators and punishers coexist and defectors are suppressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment can promote cooperation in public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on the capacity of the punishment pool: too little punishment is ineffective, but too much can reduce cooperation by crowding out cooperators. For prediction tasks, the results imply that the design of the punishment mechanism (especially its capacity and cost parameters) is crucial for maximizing cooperation, but the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Ling, T., Li, Z., Feng, M. Y., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Supervised cooperation on interdependent public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that both higher punishment severity (fine ratio) and higher bribe costs increase the fraction of cooperators and fair referees in a spatial PGG with coevolving referee behavior. There are rapid phase transitions from defection/corruption to cooperation/fairness as these parameters increase. The synergy factor (MPCR analog) is a key moderator: higher values make cooperation and fairness more likely. The supervision fee for referees also affects the prevalence of fairness. Payoff distributions are shown in spatial snapshots, indicating that average player payoffs increase with the synergy factor, but the paper does not systematically quantify efficiency or compare group payoffs between punishment-enabled and punishment-disabled conditions. The main findings are about behavioral outcomes (cooperation, fairness) rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment mechanisms (with coevolving referee behavior) can increase cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, especially when punishment is severe and bribes are costly. However, for the downstream prediction task—predicting efficiency (group payoff relative to full cooperation) as a function of game design and control efficiency—the paper is only indirectly informative. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a primary outcome, nor does it provide a direct baseline comparison with and without punishment. The qualitative discussion and payoff snapshots suggest that higher cooperation rates are associated with higher average payoffs, but the lack of explicit efficiency metrics limits direct applicability for quantitative prediction. The model structure and parameter dependencies (e.g., on player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are informative for mechanism understanding.

- source: Quan, J., Yu, J. Y., Li, X., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Conditional switching between social excluders and loners promotes cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a social exclusion strategy with dynamic (state-dependent) costs into a spatial public goods game can substantially increase the average level of cooperation in the population. Higher probability of exclusion success and lower exclusion cost parameters both expand the parameter space where cooperation dominates. The Moore neighborhood structure (more neighbors per player) is more conducive to cooperation than the traditional neighborhood. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that dynamic-cost social exclusion (a form of punishment) can increase cooperation rates in spatial public goods games, especially with high exclusion probability and low cost. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that, under similar game design dimensions, enabling effective punishment is likely to increase cooperation, which may correlate with higher efficiency, but this is not directly demonstrated. The effect of neighborhood size (player count per group) is also highlighted: larger groups (Moore neighborhood) facilitate higher cooperation. For efficiency prediction, this paper provides indirect support that punishment can be beneficial, but does not quantify efficiency outcomes.

- source: Goette, L., & Huffman, D. (2007). Affect and the motivational foundations of social capital. *Review of General Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, cooperation typically declines over time due to negative affect and conditional cooperation. However, when a punishment option is introduced, cooperation rates remain high, as punishment deters defection. The motivation to punish is linked to affective responses, such as anger at defectors and satisfaction from punishing. The paper notes that punishment can sometimes be misapplied (e.g., punishing cooperators), which can undermine cooperation. The findings are based on a synthesis of prior experimental studies, not new empirical data, and focus on behavioral and affective outcomes rather than direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong qualitative support for the idea that introducing punishment in public goods games can sustain high cooperation rates and prevent the typical decline seen in repeated games without punishment. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report new experimental results. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of a punishment mechanism is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, efficiency, provided punishment is targeted at defectors. The review also cautions that punishment can be counterproductive if misapplied. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms and moderators of punishment effects, but less so for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Varga, Z., Scarelli, A., Cressman, R., & Garay, J. (2010). Evolutionary game model for a marketing cooperative with penalty for unfaithfulness. *Nonlinear Analysis-real World Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper reviews how different sources of power—such as punishment, reward, asymmetric dependence, and information—are operationalized in economic games, including public goods games. It highlights that punishment power is often used to enforce cooperation, but can also provoke retaliation and cycles of revenge, potentially undermining group outcomes. Delegation of punishment power and the use of centralized or third-party punishment mechanisms can help sustain cooperation. The review also notes that reward can be as effective as punishment in some contexts, but is less well understood. The paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or group payoff, but it synthesizes behavioral mechanisms that are relevant to the prediction of such outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic context for how punishment and reward power affect cooperation in public goods games, but does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative estimates of efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the potential pathways through which punishment may increase or decrease efficiency, such as by deterring free-riding or provoking retaliation. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be moderated by factors such as the structure of punishment (individual vs. centralized), the possibility of retaliation, and the availability of reward. However, it does not provide data that can be directly used for quantitative prediction.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Friendly-rivalry solution to the iterated n-person public-goods game. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the CAPRI-n strategy achieves full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the iterated n-person public-goods game, even with implementation errors. When all players use CAPRI-n, the group achieves the cooperative payoff in the limit of small error probability. The strategy is defensible (cannot be exploited), efficient (achieves full cooperation), and distinguishable (can exploit unconditional cooperators). Evolutionary simulations show that CAPRI-n dominates the population across a wide range of benefit-to-cost ratios and population sizes, outperforming other memory-one strategies. The results are robust for n=2 and 3, and supported by Monte Carlo simulation for n=4.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in repeated public-goods games with error, a well-designed punishment/reciprocity strategy (CAPRI-n) can achieve near-maximal efficiency, even as group size increases. The findings suggest that, when the game allows for memory-based contingent punishment and recovery, enabling such mechanisms can dramatically increase efficiency relative to baseline. The results are most directly applicable to settings with continuous contributions, no explicit punishment cost, and no external punishment/reward mechanism, but where strategies can implement contingent retaliation. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated PGGs with endogenous, strategy-based punishment/reciprocity, especially as group size increases.

- source: Riehm, T., Fugger, N., Gillen, P., Gretschko, V., & Werner, P. (2022). Social norms, sanctions, and conditional entry in markets with externalities: Evidence from an artefactual field experiment. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that all-can-win intergroup competition (where multiple groups can be ranked first and avoid penalties) leads to large increases in cooperation and group output across all treatments. The effect is strongest when penalties for not being ranked first are sharp (T1, T2), supporting near-perfect cooperation and benefiting a majority of individuals, though not all. In these cases, average earnings are about the same with and without competition, as severe penalties for a few groups offset gains for the majority. In the weaker-penalty treatment (T3), cooperation increases less, and the effect on earnings is neutral. The results suggest that the design of the penalty function (i.e., the sharpness of incentives) is crucial for both cooperation and efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a rank-based, all-can-win intergroup competition mechanism in a standard linear public goods game can substantially increase cooperation and group output, especially when penalties for low-ranked groups are sharp. However, the effect on efficiency (average earnings) is not always positive: while most individuals benefit, severe penalties for a minority can offset group-level gains. For prediction, the sharpness of the penalty function (punishment tech) and the degree of coordination among groups are key moderators. If the control game (no competition) has low efficiency, introducing sharp all-can-win competition is likely to increase group output and may increase average earnings, but the risk of severe losses for some groups must be considered. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with intergroup competition as a sanctioning mechanism.

- source: Eichenseer, M. (2023). Leading-by-example in public goods experiments: What do we know?. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper synthesizes 62 experimental studies (mainly linear public goods games) and conducts meta-analyses and weighted least squares regressions. It finds that sequential play (leadership) increases group efficiency compared to simultaneous play. Centralized punishment and reward (where only the leader can punish or reward) further increase efficiency, with punishment having a stronger effect than reward. Peer punishment/reward (where all can punish/reward) have even larger effects. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. The marginal per capita return (MPCR) and group size also positively affect efficiency, but to a lesser extent. Leaders contribute more than followers, and followers contribute about 79% as much as leaders. Inequality within groups does not generally increase with leadership. Leaders' payoffs are similar to those of average players in simultaneous games. The paper provides quantitative estimates of effect sizes for each moderator.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment (and reward) on efficiency in public goods games, conditional on game design dimensions. The meta-regressions include variables for group size, number of rounds, MPCR, presence and type of punishment/reward, and leadership structure. The results show that adding centralized punishment (by a leader) increases efficiency by about 0.26 (on a 0-1 scale of contributions relative to optimum), and peer punishment increases it by about 0.47. The effect of reward is positive but smaller. Rotating leadership reduces efficiency. These findings can be used to adjust predicted efficiency when punishment is enabled, given the control efficiency and game design. The paper is highly relevant for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Ibrahimli, R., Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2024). Migration costs and rewarding schemes in spatial public goods games. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study simulates spatial public goods games where agents can migrate between groups if unsatisfied. Four setups are compared: baseline (migration only), migration cost (punishment), rewarding scheme (loyalty reward), and combined. Migration costs (punishment) robustly increase average contribution, especially at low enhancement factors, and are more effective than rewards. The combined mechanism can further improve cooperation in some cases. The main reported outcome is average contribution, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that institutional punishment (here, migration cost) is a stronger driver of cooperation than institutional rewards in this spatial setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, migration costs) increase cooperation (contribution) more than rewards in spatial PGGs, especially at low enhancement factors. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for understanding how migration costs and rewards affect contribution rates under different enhancement factors, group sizes, and migration probabilities. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper offers only adjacent, indirect support.

- source: Quan, J., Qin, Y., Zhou, Y. W., Wang, X. J., & Yang, J. B. (2020). How to evaluate one's behavior toward 'bad' individuals? Exploring good social norms in promoting cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with reputation-based social norms, rewarding cooperation and punishing defection toward 'bad' individuals (low-reputation) can promote cooperation, especially when the reputation threshold for being considered 'good' is high. However, increasing the reward intensity for cooperating with 'bad' individuals does not further improve cooperation, while increasing punishment intensity for defecting against 'bad' individuals only helps in high-threshold environments. The main outcome is the frequency of cooperators; average payoff per strategy is reported but not the main focus. The model is robust to both lattice and well-mixed population structures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the effect of punishment and reward mechanisms on cooperation in PGG-like environments, especially under varying reputation thresholds. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate and not efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency is limited. The reported average payoffs per strategy suggest that cooperators tend to have higher payoffs, but group-level efficiency (relative to full cooperation) is not analyzed. The model's manipulation of punishment and reward intensity, and the reporting of average payoffs, may inform qualitative expectations about the direction of efficiency effects, but quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. Y., Chen, W. M., Tang, C. X., & Wang, X. J. (2024). Reputation-dependent social learning on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a conditional strategy—where agents can switch between excluding defectors and opting out as loners—substantially increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game. Lower tolerance for defectors (i.e., agents become loners more readily), lower exclusion costs, and higher exclusion probability all lead to higher cooperation levels. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperative strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff. The study does not report efficiency as a fraction of the social optimum, nor does it provide direct evidence on how these mechanisms affect group payoff or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that conditional exclusion and voluntary opt-out mechanisms can increase the frequency of cooperation in spatial public goods games, especially when exclusion is cheap and effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment or exclusion on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that punishment-like mechanisms (social exclusion) are more effective when costs are low and effectiveness is high, but the translation to efficiency outcomes remains uncertain.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhang, J. L., Xie, G. M., & Wang, L. (2010). Group penalty on the evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of a group penalty mechanism—removing groups whose collective payoff falls below a threshold—substantially increases the stationary fraction of cooperators in spatial public goods games across a variety of network topologies. The effect is robust to initial conditions and is sensitive to the deletion threshold and the number of connections of newcomers. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so the findings are limited to cooperation rates rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that group-level punishment (group penalty) can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games, but it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may suggest higher efficiency, but this is not demonstrated or quantified. The model is theoretical/simulation-based and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions in a way that allows for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ozono, H., Kamijo, Y., & Shimizu, K. (2016). Institutionalize Reciprocity to Overcome the Public Goods Provision Problem. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: In a repeated four-person public goods game, embedding a reciprocal strategy as an institution (institutionalized reciprocity, IR) leads to significantly higher group efficiency (total profit) than a standard control game. The effect is strongest when the institution enforces a strict upper bound on contributions (S-IR), which enables reciprocators to coordinate against free riders and prevents excessive cooperation. In S-IR, group profits are significantly higher than in both the control and a less restrictive IR variant (NL-IR). The IR mechanism operates as a collective punishment system: if any player fails to meet the target, the group's future benefit is reduced for all. The study does not implement standard peer punishment, but the IR mechanism provides a collective sanction that is effective at increasing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutionalized, rule-based collective punishment (via reduction of future group benefit after a deviation) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated public goods games, especially when the institution strictly limits contributions to a target. While the study does not test standard peer punishment, it shows that collective, institutionally enforced sanctions can outperform standard repeated games without punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that introducing a credible, enforced institutional sanction (even if not peer punishment) can raise efficiency well above control, particularly when the rules are clear and binding. The results are most directly applicable to games with four players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions (in increments), and full feedback on others' contributions and payoffs.

- source: Schnell, E., & Muthukrishna, M. (2024). Indirect reciprocity undermines indirect reciprocity destabilizing large- scale cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a nested PGG environment with both local and global public goods, indirect reciprocity (reputation-based conditional aid) can stabilize cooperation at a single scale, but when multiple scales are present, local cooperation is more stable and can invade global cooperation. This occurs even when the global PGG has a higher theoretical maximum payoff. The model demonstrates that the presence of multiple, potentially conflicting reputations leads to a situation where local cooperation undermines global cooperation, reducing overall efficiency at the larger scale. The more fragmented the population (more local groups), the harder it is for global cooperation to be stable. Thus, indirect reciprocity, as typically modeled, cannot by itself sustain efficient, large-scale cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of group membership and the scale at which reputational incentives operate are critical moderators of the efficiency gains from indirect reciprocity-based 'punishment' (here, conditional aid/reward) in PGG-like environments. Specifically, if the game design allows for multiple, nested groups and reputations can be built at the local level, enabling local cooperation, then enabling indirect reciprocity (punishment/reward) may not increase, and can even decrease, efficiency at the global (large-group) level. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of multiple group scales and the ability to build local reputations are key negative moderators of the efficiency effect of punishment/reward mechanisms. The findings are directly relevant for settings where the control (no punishment/reward) game is inefficient, but the addition of indirect reciprocity does not guarantee higher efficiency at the largest scale.

- source: Zschache, J. (2012). Producing public goods in networks: Some effects of social comparison and endogenous network change. *Social Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games on networks, the ability of agents to change their social ties in response to social comparison (i.e., to seek out like-minded cooperators and avoid defectors) can sustain or increase cooperation and thus the production of public goods. Low network density and low costs of network change (low cohesion) allow cooperators to persist by forming supportive subgroups, leading to higher efficiency. High initial cooperation and low segregation also promote full cooperation. When network change is costly (high cohesion), cooperation collapses and efficiency is low. The model shows that both forward-looking and backward-looking (adaptive) agents can achieve high efficiency under favorable network conditions, but backward-looking agents require even more favorable starting conditions. Explicit costly punishment is not modeled; instead, social comparison provides a form of endogenous, non-costly social pressure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that endogenous network change and social comparison can substitute for explicit costly punishment in sustaining cooperation and efficiency in public goods games. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game designs allowing for dynamic partner selection or network rewiring (low cohesion/costs) will see higher efficiency, especially when initial cooperation is high and the network is not highly segregated. However, the absence of explicit costly punishment means the results are most applicable to settings where social comparison or endogenous social pressure is the main incentive, not where formal punishment is enabled. The results are directly informative for predicting efficiency in networked PGGs with endogenous tie change, but less so for classic PGGs with explicit punishment options.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation in costly institutions exhibits Red Queen and Black Queen dynamics in heterogeneous public goods. *Communications Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model demonstrates that introducing a costly institution (with a participation fee) alongside a free institution in a public goods game can increase cooperation and group efficiency. For low enhancement factors (low profitability), only the costly institution supports cooperation, and average payoffs remain close to the entrance cost (i.e., not much higher than the baseline). For higher enhancement factors, cooperation emerges in both institutions, and group payoffs increase above zero. The costly institution is more likely to be populated by cooperators, as defectors avoid the entrance cost unless there are enough cooperators to exploit. The model also shows that the effect of the entrance cost is context-dependent: it is beneficial for cooperation and efficiency at low profitability, but can be detrimental at high profitability if the cost is too high, as it reduces participation. The results hold in both well-mixed and structured populations, though spatial structure removes some bistability and further supports cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that introducing a costly institution (entrance fee) in a public goods game can increase efficiency and group payoff, especially when the baseline profitability (mpcr/enhancement factor) is low. The entrance cost acts as a filter, keeping defectors out and allowing cooperators to achieve higher payoffs. For prediction tasks, the presence of a costly institution (or, by analogy, a punishment mechanism with a cost to participate) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a control with no such cost, particularly when the marginal per-capita return is low. However, if the cost is too high and the game is already highly profitable, the effect may reverse. The model does not include explicit punishment, but the costly institution's effect is adjacent to punishment in that it deters free-riding through a cost barrier. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where institutional participation costs or costly precommitment mechanisms are present, rather than direct peer punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Wang, T. (2023). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods games under a risk-transfer mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a risk-transfer (coalition) mechanism into a spatial public goods game can enhance or reduce cooperation and group efficiency, depending on the synergy coefficient (r) and the coalition closeness (alpha). In two-strategy systems, coalitional cooperators are more competitive than traditional cooperators, and coalitional defectors are less competitive than traditional defectors. In four-strategy systems, increasing coalition closeness (alpha) generally increases cooperation, but can also lead to non-monotonic effects where too strong coalitions reduce cooperation due to the breakdown of indirect territorial competition. The mechanism acts as a wealth redistribution system, strengthening cooperators at the boundary of clusters but sometimes making them vulnerable to invasion by other types. The model does not include punishment or reward, but the coalition mechanism is compared to these in the discussion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in PGGs, as it does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it provides informative theoretical evidence about how payoff-redistribution mechanisms (such as coalitions or risk-sharing) can affect group efficiency and cooperation in spatial PGGs. The findings suggest that mechanisms which redistribute payoffs among players can have complex, sometimes non-monotonic effects on efficiency, depending on the structure of the game (e.g., synergy coefficient, spatial structure, coalition closeness). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of any payoff-redistribution mechanism (including punishment, if it were present) may depend strongly on the details of the mechanism and the spatial or network structure. However, direct quantitative prediction of punishment effects is not possible from this paper.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Evolution of cooperation and consistent personalities in public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in two-round public goods games, both reward-based and assortative mechanisms can promote the evolution of cooperation and the emergence of consistent cooperative or defective personalities. In the 'reward dilemma' scenario, only first-round cooperators are allowed to play a second PGG, which can incentivize cooperation if the second game's enhancement factor is high enough. In the 'assortative' scenario, both cooperators and defectors play a second PGG in separate groups, and competition between these groups can further promote cooperation and personality consistency. The model shows that cooperation is maximized at intermediate values of the second-round enhancement factor, and that consistent personalities (cooperating or defecting in both rounds) naturally evolve. However, the paper does not model punishment or sanctions, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how reward and assortment mechanisms (and the possibility of repeated or conditional interaction) can promote cooperation and personality consistency, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or their efficiency effects. The findings may be indirectly informative for prediction tasks involving reward or assortment, but not for punishment. The model's outcomes are primarily behavioral (cooperation rates, personality consistency), not efficiency or group payoff, so it should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment.

- source: Kang, H. W., Li, Z. K., Shen, Y., Huang, Z. Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). From eligibility to suitability: Regulation and restriction of reputation-based access system on free-riding behavior in spatial public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that reputation-based exclusion mechanisms, which filter out low-reputation (likely free-riding) individuals before the PGG round, can significantly increase cooperation and total system payoff in spatial public goods games. The homogeneous exclusion model requires higher exclusion probability to match the performance of traditional (post-game) exclusion, but both converge to high cooperation and payoff at high exclusion probability. The heterogeneous exclusion model, which tailors exclusion probability to the reputation gap, outperforms both the homogeneous and traditional models in promoting cooperation and system payoff at high exclusion intensity. Both reputation-based models are especially effective at suppressing second-order free-riding, a limitation of traditional exclusion. The models are simulated on a 100x100 lattice with three strategies (C, D, EC), and results are robust across a range of synergy factors and exclusion costs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that pre-game, reputation-based exclusion mechanisms (a form of access control) can increase group payoff and cooperation in spatial PGGs, especially when exclusion is sufficiently strict. The findings suggest that, for games with spatial structure and reputation tracking, enabling such exclusion mechanisms is likely to increase efficiency relative to control (no exclusion or punishment). The effect is stronger for the heterogeneous (adaptive) exclusion model. The results are most directly applicable to spatial PGGs with continuous contribution, reputation tracking, and exclusion as the main sanctioning mechanism. The paper does not study classic costly punishment (where players pay to reduce others' payoffs post-game), so direct transfer to standard punishment-enabled PGGs should be cautious. However, the results are highly informative for predicting the effect of exclusion-based access control on efficiency, especially in spatial/reputation contexts.

- source: Xiao, J. F., Liu, L. J., Chen, X. J., & Szolnoki, A. (2023). Evolution of cooperation driven by sampling reward. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the sampling reward mechanism can effectively increase the average cooperation level in both PGG and CRD models. Higher reward thresholds and higher reward intensities promote cooperation, but also increase the cost to the institution. The effect of reward intensity is non-linear: above a critical threshold, further increases yield little additional cooperation. Sample size has a non-monotonic effect on cost, with intermediate sizes minimizing cost. The findings are robust across both game types. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that reward mechanisms (specifically, sampling reward) can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, with effects moderated by reward threshold, intensity, and sample size. However, it does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The paper is most useful for understanding how reward design parameters affect cooperation rates and institutional costs, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. It does not provide empirical or theoretical results on punishment, nor does it analyze efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation.

- source: Wang, M. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2020). The interplay of behaviors and attitudes in public goods game considering environmental investment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The introduction of an alliance strategy, where players pay an aid fee to help exploited allies, leads to a dramatic increase in the prevalence of cooperative behavior (C + A) in spatial PGGs. Alliance clusters are robust to defector invasion and can expand to dominate the population, especially for intermediate values of the synergy factor (r) and aid intensity (beta). The cooperation level is a non-monotonic function of both r and beta, with optimal intervals for each. Average payoffs per strategy are reported, showing that alliance players eventually outperform defectors and cooperators. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total welfare as a function of the alliance mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that mechanisms analogous to mutual aid or alliance (distinct from punishment) can promote cooperation and increase the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial PGGs. However, because the model does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare, it is only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency effects of punishment. The findings suggest that payoff redistribution among cooperators can be highly effective in suppressing defection, but the results cannot be directly transferred to settings with explicit punishment. The paper is most useful for understanding the effects of mutual aid or alliance-type mechanisms, not punishment.

- source: Stoop, J., van Soest, D., & Vyrastekova, J. (2018). Rewards and cooperation in social dilemma games. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that net-positive rewards (where the recipient gains more than the sender loses) dramatically increase cooperation and efficiency in standard public goods games (VCM), with most groups reaching high contribution levels when rewards are available. In contrast, in the Claim game—where players can undo others' contributions by taking from the public pool—rewards have only a marginal effect on cooperation, and average net contributions are not significantly different from zero. In the Claim game, rewards are often exchanged bilaterally and are not strongly linked to cooperative behavior in the social dilemma. The results suggest that the effectiveness of rewards in promoting efficiency depends critically on the structure of the social dilemma, particularly whether others can undo cooperative actions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effectiveness of reward mechanisms in increasing efficiency in public goods games depends on whether the game allows for negative actions (i.e., taking from the public pool) and whether the MPCR is certain. In standard VCMs (linear PGGs), rewards can substantially increase efficiency, but in environments where contributions can be undone (as in the Claim game or CPR games), rewards are much less effective. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'take' option or uncertain MPCR is a critical moderator: control efficiency is not a sufficient predictor of treatment efficiency with rewards if the game allows for undoing others' contributions. The findings do not inform about punishment effects, as punishment is not studied.

- source: Chen, Q., Chen, T., & Yang, R. (2019). Using rewards reasonably: The effects of stratified-rewards in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that stratified-rewards can promote, maintain, or even inhibit efficiency (measured as available funds for public goods) depending on the scope and distribution of rewards. If the number of people who receive rewards is small and the number who get high-level rewards is controlled, available funds can increase. If too many people get high rewards, available funds can fall below the baseline, even if all players cooperate. The sum of contributions and the standard deviation of contributions are also affected by reward parameters. The fraction of cooperators is always high with rewards, but this does not guarantee high efficiency. The optimal reward design balances the number of recipients and the cost of rewards to maximize available funds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of reward mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like environments is highly sensitive to the design of the reward scheme. It shows that simply enabling rewards does not guarantee higher efficiency; the number of rewarded players, the proportion of high-level rewards, and the cost of rewards all interact to determine the net group payoff. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of a reward mechanism can have positive, neutral, or negative effects on efficiency, depending on these design details. Since the paper does not study punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment, but it does highlight the importance of cost structures and incentive distribution in predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Chung, D., Yun, K., Kim, J. H., Jang, B., & Jeong, J. (2011). Different Gain/Loss Sensitivity and Social Adaptation Ability in Gifted Adolescents during a Public Goods Game. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that gifted adolescents are more cooperative and earn higher total payoffs than average adolescents in a repeated binary public goods game with five players and no punishment or reward mechanisms. Three incentive conditions are tested: (1) standard PGG, (2) no-loss guarantee for cooperators, and (3) equalized bonus for all in successful rounds. Gifted adolescents show higher gain sensitivity and lower loss sensitivity, and are more strategic in maximizing payoffs. Average adolescents are more loss-averse, less strategic, and more influenced by social adaptation behaviors. The study provides detailed evidence on how group composition (gifted vs. average) and incentive structure affect efficiency and cooperation, but does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctioning.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on efficiency and payoff outcomes in repeated binary PGGs without punishment or reward. It is directly informative for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions with known game design parameters (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.). However, it does not inform the effect of enabling punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or discussed in the experimental design. Thus, it is useful for establishing baseline efficiency but not for predicting the effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., & Chen, Z. Q. (2016). The public goods game with a new form of shared reward. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a shared reward, proportional to the number of cooperators, can enable cooperation to invade and become stable in a population of defectors if the reward parameter exceeds a critical threshold. This threshold depends negatively on the population size and the public goods multiplier, and positively on the group size. Larger populations and higher public goods multipliers make it easier for cooperation to be favored, while larger group sizes make it harder. The analysis is entirely theoretical and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, and thus does not directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. However, it does provide theoretical insight into how reward mechanisms (distinct from punishment) can affect the evolution of cooperation and group payoff. For the downstream prediction task focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant, but may be useful for understanding the comparative role of reward-based interventions.

- source: Wang, C. Q., Lin, Z. Z., & Rothman, D. S. (2022). Public goods game on coevolving networks driven by the similarity and difference of payoff. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the way agents update strategies—locally, globally, or a mix—has complex effects on cooperation and payoffs in spatial PGGs. When both cooperators and defectors have the same tendency for global updating, local updating is more beneficial for cooperation at low synergy factors (r), while both local and global updating can support cooperation at high r, but their combination can be detrimental. When cooperators and defectors differ in their global updating propensity, the system's cooperation and payoff outcomes depend sensitively on these propensities. Moderate global updating by defectors and low global updating by cooperators most favors cooperation and higher payoffs. The paper does not study punishment or its effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGGs. It is useful for understanding how information structure (local vs global updating) and network effects influence cooperation and payoffs, but does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment-enabled versus punishment-disabled efficiency. The absence of punishment mechanisms means it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., He, J. L., & Xu, W. S. (2022). Wealth-based rule favors cooperation in costly public goods games when individual selection is inevitable. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in both independent and interdependent PGGs with two cooperative strategies, the coexistence and prevalence of each strategy depend on the synergy factor and the difference in input costs. In the interdependent model, effective input and economic benefit are maximized when the two strategies are present in similar proportions. The interdependence mechanism (In-PGG) can sometimes lead to more quasi-cooperation than the independent mechanism (An-PGG), especially when the synergy factor and the difference in input costs are small. However, the paper does not directly report or quantify group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the stable proportions of strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or sanctions. It is useful for understanding how the structure of cooperation (independent vs interdependent strategies) and parameter settings affect the prevalence of cooperative behaviors, but it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in a way that can be directly mapped to the prediction task. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how structural features of the game (such as interdependence of contributions) affect the emergence of cooperation, but not for the effect of punishment.

- source: Fosgaard, T. R., & Piovesan, M. (2015). Nudge for (the Public) Good: How Defaults Can Affect Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that while most subjects do not simply accept the default contribution values, the presence of a default (either free rider or perfect conditional cooperator) significantly influences their stated cooperation strategies and actual contributions in a subsequent repeated public goods game. Specifically, exposure to a cooperative default increases later contributions, while a free rider default decreases them. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in any treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning is present. It does, however, show that non-binding defaults can shift contribution behavior, which may indirectly inform models of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Wang, G. R., Li, J. B., Wang, W. H., Niu, X. F., Wang, Y., & Selten, R. (2024). Confusion cannot explain cooperative behavior in public goods games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.

  findings: The study finds that, after ensuring participants understand the public goods game, most still do not behave as pure free riders, especially when playing with humans. Social preferences and social norms, rather than confusion, explain much of the observed cooperation. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.

- source: van den Berg, P., Dewitte, P., Aertgeerts, I., & Wenseleers, T. (2020). How the incentive to contribute affects contributions in the one-shot public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that average contributions in a one-shot public goods game increase with the marginal per capita return (MPCR), with most of the increase occurring at lower MPCR values (below 0.58–0.64). At higher MPCR, contributions plateau well below full cooperation. The experiment uses groups of three, no punishment or reward, and no communication. The findings provide a high-resolution mapping of how MPCR shapes contribution behavior, but do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting baseline (control) efficiency in public goods games as a function of MPCR, group size, and the absence of punishment or reward. It does not provide any evidence about the effect of punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is useful for estimating control efficiency (without punishment) given MPCR and group size, but does not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in a public goods game with environmental investment is promoted when social attention (driven by individual attitudes and environmental feedback) is high and when the investment budget exceeds a threshold. The model identifies three types of public response mechanisms to environmental degradation, with the 'precautionary' mechanism (rapid response to early degradation) being most beneficial for cooperation. The level of cooperation is sensitive to the parameters governing attention and forgetfulness, but the level of social attention itself is robust to these parameters within certain intervals. No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not analyzed.

Its findings are limited to the dynamics of cooperation and social attention in a PGG with environmental investment and evolving attitudes. It may provide indirect context for how non-punitive mechanisms (like attention or investment) affect cooperation, but is not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The study finds that arranging participants into homogeneous or heterogeneous subgroups of efficacy does not significantly affect the number or proportion of contributions to the public good. This is because two mediating mechanisms—perceived efficacy and expectation of subgroup contribution—operate in opposite directions and cancel each other out. The experiment does not include any punishment, sanction, or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are limited to contribution behavior and psychological mediators.

- source: Battu, B., & Srinivasan, N. (2020). Evolution of conditional cooperation in public good games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that high and stable cooperation (measured by donation rates) can be achieved in repeated public good games when agents' social learning is sufficiently biased toward prestige (social value) or when both prestige and payoff are weighted but with some noise. When imitation is driven mainly by payoff, cooperation collapses. The model does not include punishment or reward, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding how social learning mechanisms (prestige vs. payoff) affect the evolution of cooperation, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public good games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that cooperation can be sustained by prestige-biased social learning even in the absence of punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not provide usable quantitative or qualitative evidence about the efficiency impact of punishment or about moderators of punishment effects.

- source: Tomassini, M., & Antonioni, A. (2021). Computational behavioral models in public goods games with migration between groups. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that allowing unsatisfied players to migrate between groups increases average contribution in public goods games, especially for lower enhancement factors (r). The effect of group size and update timing (synchronous vs asynchronous) is minor. Heterogeneity in migration propensity has little effect on average contribution compared to a homogeneous population. The amount of information available to players (own payoff/contribution vs also knowing the number of contributors in the group) does not substantially change the results. However, the paper does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on migration as an alternative to punishment may be contextually informative for understanding other forms of exit or mobility, but are not directly transferable to the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The reported outcomes are average contribution rates, not efficiency or payoff, and thus are only adjacent to the prediction task.

  findings: The paper finds that longer memory (up to a threshold) and slower memory decay promote higher levels of cooperation in both scale-free and small-world networks. Heterogeneity in memory length (asynchronous model) further enhances cooperation, especially in scale-free networks. The synergy factor (analogous to MPCR) also increases cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperator density, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or reward mechanisms are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is useful for understanding how memory and network structure affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or the impact of punishment. The absence of punishment mechanisms and efficiency outcomes limits its direct relevance to the prediction task.
