  findings: The study finds that allowing cooperators to preferentially invest in groups with more cooperators (higher alpha) and to use longer memory of past group composition increases the fraction of cooperators in both well-mixed and structured populations. The mechanism leads to the formation of larger and more robust cooperation clusters, which can dominate the population under favorable conditions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, as they show that mechanisms which allow for adaptive investment based on group composition can promote cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing only contextual or adjacent evidence, not direct input.

- source: Salahshour, M. (2021). Freedom to choose between public resources promotes cooperation. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that allowing individuals to choose between multiple public goods games (resources) with different enhancement factors can promote cooperation. Defectors tend to cluster in the highest quality resource, while cooperators can 'shelter' in lower quality resources, avoiding exploitation. Cooperation is maximized when the two highest quality resources are similar in quality. The model is analyzed both analytically (replicator-mutator dynamics) and via simulation, showing robust results across parameter ranges. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The main outcome is the density of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is highly relevant for understanding how choice between public resources affects cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding the impact of resource choice and group structure on cooperation.

- source: Inaba, M., & Akiyama, E. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in multiplex networks through asymmetry between interaction and replacement. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in multiplex network models of public goods games, the symmetry or asymmetry between the scope of interaction and the scope of strategy replacement can have non-monotonic effects on the evolution of cooperation. Specifically, in some cases, increasing asymmetry (e.g., local interaction with global replacement) can partially promote cooperation, contrary to previous findings that symmetry always promotes cooperation. The main outcome is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as punishment is not modeled. However, it is relevant for understanding how network structure and the symmetry of interaction and learning/replacement can affect cooperation rates. The findings may be useful for contextualizing the role of network and update rules in cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment or sanctions.

- source: Battu, B., & Rahwan, T. (2023). Cooperation without punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated public goods games, cooperation cannot be sustained by conditional cooperators alone, nor by simply injecting altruists if there is no social noise. However, when a moderate level of noise is present, a small but critical fraction of altruists can trigger and sustain high levels of cooperation among conditional cooperators. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and instead focuses on the role of positive reciprocity and social learning. The main outcome is the level of cooperation (contribution rate), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism in its model. Its findings are relevant for understanding alternative mechanisms to punishment for sustaining cooperation, but do not inform the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. The paper does, however, argue that punishment can reduce social welfare, but this is a theoretical and literature-based claim, not a result of the presented model. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should be considered as providing context for non-punishment mechanisms, but not as a source of quantitative or comparative evidence on punishment's effect on efficiency.

- source: Xie, K., & Szolnoki, A. (2025). Reputation in public goods cooperation under double Q-learning protocol. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that integrating reputation into both investment decisions and payoff calculations, especially when using double Q-learning, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The effect is strongest when the weight of reputation in payoff (η), the synergy factor (r), and the reputation sensitivity parameter (δ) are all sufficiently high. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even in challenging environments, but only when these parameters are jointly favorable. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rate, reputation distribution).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It is highly relevant for understanding how reputation mechanisms and reinforcement learning protocols affect cooperation rates, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency changes due to punishment. The absence of any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and the lack of payoff-based outcome reporting, limit its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Wang, X. Y., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2024). Conformity poses a double-edged sword effect on the evolution of cooperation within heterogeneous endowment populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the existence and maintenance of pure cooperator groups in a multi-group public goods game can promote the rise of cooperation in the population, even without mechanisms like punishment or reward. However, mutation and migration allow defectors to invade cooperator groups, undermining cooperation. Group division (splitting of large groups) can counteract the negative effects of mutation by generating new cooperator groups, but only if mutation rates are not too high and group division is sufficiently frequent. The synergy factor (r) and contribution level (c) also moderate these dynamics. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, so its findings are not directly transferable to payoff-based prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it provides indirect context on how group structure, mutation, and migration affect cooperation rates. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, except as a reminder that mechanisms other than punishment (e.g., group division, migration) can strongly affect cooperation, and that the presence or absence of such mechanisms should be considered when interpreting or predicting the effects of punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Li, H. Z., Zhang, M., & Wang, X. J. (2024). Cooperation dynamics in nonlinear spatial public goods games with endogenous synergy and discounting feedback. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that introducing endogenous feedback—where the payoff structure depends on the current and historical level of cooperation—promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation in spatial public goods games. Both the length and intensity of the feedback mechanism (how much history is considered and how strongly it affects payoffs) increase the fraction of cooperators in the population. The model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. The results are based on simulations of cooperation density and strategy dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on endogenous feedback and nonlinear payoffs may be contextually relevant for understanding mechanisms that promote cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Li, Y. P., Hu, X. Y., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., Yang, J. J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Environmental information perception enhances cooperation in stochastic public goods games via Q-learning. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a stochastic PGG with environmental feedback, the ability of individuals to perceive environmental state information significantly promotes the emergence and stability of cooperation. Specifically, the critical synergy factor (r) required for cooperation to emerge is lower when individuals can perceive the environment, compared to both a static environment and a dynamic environment that is not perceived. The study uses Q-learning agents on spatial networks and explores a variety of model extensions (update rules, neighborhood size, network topology, etc.), consistently finding that environmental information perception lowers the cooperation threshold. However, the paper does not analyze or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings are adjacent to the prediction task, as they show that environmental information perception (not punishment) can promote cooperation (not efficiency) in stochastic PGGs. The results may be useful for understanding how information and feedback mechanisms affect cooperation thresholds, but they do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the prediction task, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Wang, X. W., Jiang, L. L., Nie, S., & Wang, B. H. (2015). Uncovering cooperative behaviors with sparse historical behavior data in the spatial games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper uses agent-based simulations to study how conformity-driven behavior affects cooperation in public goods games with binary heterogeneous endowments (rich and poor). The main finding is a 'double-edged sword' effect: when the multiplication factor (synergy of cooperation) is high, conformity enhances cooperation by strengthening network reciprocity; when the multiplication factor is low, conformity inhibits cooperation, especially among the poor, by allowing defection to infiltrate cooperative clusters. These effects are robust to variations in conformity strength, the proportion of rich, and the extension to multiplayer PGGs. However, the study only reports on cooperation rates, not on efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on conformity and cooperation rates in heterogeneous endowment settings may be contextually informative for understanding behavioral dynamics in PGGs, but are not directly transferable to efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor to the effects of punishment. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Flores, L. S., Vainstein, M. H., Fernandes, H. C. M., & Amaral, M. A. (2023). Heterogeneous contributions can jeopardize cooperation in the public goods game. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study demonstrates that in spatial PGGs with imitation dynamics, there is an optimal homogeneous contribution value that maximizes cooperation (measured as cooperator density) for a given lattice structure. Introducing heterogeneity in contributions (i.e., allowing cooperators to contribute different amounts) can reduce the overall level of cooperation compared to this optimum, due to parasitic interactions where higher-contribution cooperators are exploited by lower-contribution ones. The effect of heterogeneity depends on the lattice structure: on square lattices, heterogeneity is detrimental to cooperation at the optimum, while on triangular lattices (with clustering), the effect is less negative. The main dependent variable is the equilibrium density of cooperators; efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in contributions (a form of player heterogeneity) affects cooperation rates, but not efficiency or payoff outcomes. The results may be useful for contextualizing the impact of contribution heterogeneity as a moderator, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment. The absence of punishment means this paper should not be used as primary evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Gao, B., Li, X., Hou, S. X., Jia, D. Y., & Du, M. J. (2019). Resolving public goods dilemma by giving the poor more support. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that narrowing the income gap among group members (negative alpha, i.e., redistributive policies favoring the poor) increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial public goods game, while increasing income inequality (positive alpha) reduces cooperation. The model is simulated on a lattice, and the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators under different redistribution regimes. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are present, and no efficiency or group payoff outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about redistribution and cooperation rates may be of indirect interest for understanding how inequality and redistribution affect cooperation, but it is not relevant for the core prediction task regarding punishment and efficiency.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper finds that experimental economic games, including public goods games, show substantial variability in behavioral outcomes (such as cooperation and prosociality) across populations, subject pools, settings, and contextual frames. There is no reliable generalizability of results, and evidence for parallelism between game and real-world behavior is mixed. The review highlights the importance of context and framing in interpreting game results and cautions against assuming that game behavior reflects real-world preferences or norms. The paper does not address punishment or efficiency outcomes directly.

However, it does not discuss punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and thus offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is as a caution about external validity and the importance of context, not as a source of quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or payoff effects.

- source: Bravo, G., & Squazzoni, F. (2013). Exit, Punishment and Rewards in Commons Dilemmas: An Experimental Study. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a modified public goods game with six players per group and ten rounds per treatment, the introduction of punishment (a 5 MU fine for non-cooperation) significantly increased cooperation and efficiency compared to the control (no incentives). However, the highest efficiency (profit as a proportion of the theoretical optimum) was achieved when both voluntary participation (exit option) and positive incentives (5 MU reward for cooperation) were present. Punishment alone (No-Neg) led to 67% of the optimum, while reward plus exit (Ex-Pos) led to 69%. The control (No-Null) and punishment plus exit (Ex-Neg) both yielded 58%. Thus, while punishment increases efficiency over the control, it is less effective than the reward-plus-exit combination. The paper also finds that exit alone has little effect, and that the interaction between exit and incentives is important for maximizing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with six players, ten rounds, no communication, and fixed institutional punishment (5 MU fine per defection), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), but the efficiency gain is smaller than that achieved by combining rewards and voluntary participation. The efficiency effect of punishment is positive but not maximal. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, no chat, continuous contribution, fixed MPCR, fixed punishment cost/tech, no reward in punishment-only treatments, no identification of punishers, and full information about group outcomes) are all specified, making this paper highly informative for predicting efficiency under similar conditions.

- source: Wegmann, J., & Musshoff, O. (2019). Groundwater management institutions in the face of rapid urbanization - Results of a framed field experiment in Bengaluru, India. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both externally imposed punishment and reward rules, implemented via random monitoring and sanctions/rewards for exceeding/following optimal extraction, significantly reduce over-extraction of groundwater in a framed field experiment with 600 real-world users. Social efficiency (group payoff relative to the social optimum) increased from 86% in the control to 97% under punishment and 95% under reward, with welfare gains of 8% and 6% over control, respectively (both statistically significant at 1%). Communication alone had a much smaller effect (90% efficiency, 1% gain, not significant). The effect of punishment and reward was not significantly different between rural and urban areas. User types (myopic, rational, social optimal) persisted across treatments, but all treatments crowded out the most pro-social (social optimal) users to some extent. The main outcome is that externally imposed monitoring and sanctioning (punishment) is highly effective at increasing efficiency in this CPR context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence that, in a dynamic CPR game with real-world users, adding an externally imposed punishment mechanism (random monitoring, monetary penalty for over-extraction) increases group efficiency (payoff/welfare) by a substantial and statistically significant margin compared to a control with no punishment. The effect is robust to user type and urbanization. The game is not a standard linear PGG but is a close CPR variant with continuous extraction, group interdependence, and profit-based payoffs. The punishment cost and technology are specified, and the efficiency gains are quantified. This evidence should increase predicted efficiency for similar games when punishment is enabled, especially when the control efficiency is below the social optimum.

- source: van Miltenburg, N., Przepiorka, W., & Buskens, V. (2017). Consensual punishment does not promote cooperation in the six-person prisoner's dilemma game with noisy public monitoring. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three punishment institutions (individual decision rule, CDR2, CDR3) in a six-person prisoner's dilemma, with and without 20% noise in public monitoring of contributions. In the absence of noise, all punishment institutions increase cooperation and earnings relative to the baseline, with collective decision rules (especially CDR3) performing best. With noise, however, all punishment institutions result in lower earnings than the baseline (no-punishment) condition. The individual decision rule (IDR) produces slightly higher cooperation than collective rules under noise, but still does not improve earnings. The main reason is that noise leads to both mistaken punishment of cooperators and missed punishment of defectors, so resources are wasted and cooperation is not effectively enforced. Thus, punishment does not promote efficiency in noisy environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a six-person all-or-nothing prisoner's dilemma with 20% noise in public monitoring, enabling punishment (regardless of whether it is individual or requires consensus) reduces group efficiency compared to the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is robust across different punishment decision rules. The findings suggest that, when noise is present, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than the control efficiency. The results are directly relevant for settings with similar group size, binary contribution, and noisy monitoring, and less so for linear PGGs or settings with perfect information.

- source: Koike, S., Nakamaru, M., Otaka, T., Shimao, H., Shimomura, K. I., & Yamato, T. (2018). Reciprocity and exclusion in informal financial institutions: An experimental study of rotating savings and credit associations. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment compared four treatments in a fixed-order ROSCA game: baseline (no sanctions), punishment rule (forfeiture), voting (exclusion), and both punishment and voting. Exclusion by voting led to significantly higher contribution rates and mean payoffs than the other treatments. The punishment rule, which prevented non-contributors from receiving payouts, did not increase contributions or payoffs relative to the baseline. The combination of voting and punishment was less effective than voting alone. The results suggest that social exclusion based on reputation is a more effective mechanism for sustaining cooperation and improving group efficiency than the explicit punishment rule tested here.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong evidence that a costless, rule-based punishment (forfeiture of payout for non-contributors) does not improve efficiency or mean payoff compared to a baseline with no punishment. In contrast, exclusion by peer voting (reputation-based exclusion) substantially increases both contributions and efficiency. The game structure is a close variant of a public goods game (ROSCA), with fixed group size, known rounds, and full information about others' actions. The findings suggest that not all punishment mechanisms are equally effective: exclusion based on group voting is effective, but a non-costly, rule-based punishment is not. This distinction is important for predicting the effect of punishment in similar environments.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). The paradigm of tax-reward and tax-punishment strategies in the advancement of public resource management dynamics. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that both tax-funded punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation and sustain public resources, but only if the natural growth rate of the resource pool is sufficiently high. When the resource growth rate is low, even universal cooperation (driven by strong punishment or reward) cannot prevent resource depletion, so efficiency remains low. For moderate or high growth rates, the system can reach stable states with high cooperation and sustainable resources, but the tax-punishment model can exhibit bistability, meaning the final outcome depends on initial conditions. The magnitude of the tax (which determines the strength of punishment or reward) also affects whether the system stabilizes at full cooperation, coexistence, or defection. The models provide explicit, parameterized predictions for group efficiency and resource sustainability as a function of player count, tax size, and resource growth rate.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment (and reward) on group efficiency and resource sustainability in public-goods-like resource management games. The models are not standard linear PGGs, but are close variants with dynamic, renewable resources and tax-based centralized punishment/reward. The findings show that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by the natural growth rate of the resource pool and the magnitude of the tax (punishment strength). For low growth rates, punishment does not improve efficiency; for moderate/high growth rates, punishment can improve efficiency, but outcomes may be bistable. The results are most applicable to settings where resource dynamics and centralized sanctioning are key features. The paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions in such environments.

- source: Wang, Y. F., Vasilakos, A. V., & Ma, J. H. (2015). VPEF: A Simple and Effective Incentive Mechanism in Community-Based Autonomous Networks. *Ieee Transactions on Network and Service Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: VPEF, an incentive mechanism based on voluntary participation, round-based entry fees, and voluntary costly punishment, leads to high efficiency in community-based collaborative networks. The system spends most of its time in the punisher state, where all participants contribute and punish defectors, minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss is analytically shown to be 4/(8+M), which becomes negligible for large communities. Simulations confirm the theoretical predictions. The mechanism is robust to changes in group size, entry fee (as long as it is not too high), and multiplexing/diversity factor. The model assumes strong selection (users imitate higher-payoff strategies) and does not require tracking long-term behavior or permanent exclusion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling voluntary costly punishment in a public-goods-like environment with voluntary participation and a small entry fee can dramatically increase efficiency, driving the system to near-full cooperation (punisher state) and minimizing efficiency loss. The efficiency loss formula (4/(8+M)) can be used to predict the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game, given the number of users. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values, provided the multiplexing/diversity factor is above 1 and defectors can be reliably detected. The model is most applicable to dynamic, anonymous, and voluntary participation settings, and less so to settings with continuous contribution levels or where punishment/reward is not voluntary.

- source: Farjam, M., Faillo, M., Sprinkhuizen-Kuyper, I., & Haselager, P. (2015). Punishment Mechanisms and Their Effect on Cooperation: A Simulation Study. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment can facilitate higher group efficiency (average energy) in environments where resources are scarce (low MPCR), but only when the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid antisocial punishment and minimize costs (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun). Unrestricted punishment consistently reduces efficiency. When resources are abundant (high MPCR), punishment mechanisms do not improve and may reduce efficiency compared to no-punishment. The results are robust to variations in group size and punishment cost ratio. The study provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by resource availability (analogous to MPCR in PGGs) and by the design of the punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that the efficiency impact of enabling punishment in PGG-like environments depends critically on both the marginal per-capita return (MPCR, here operationalized as resource availability) and the specific punishment mechanism. When MPCR is low (resources scarce), enabling well-designed punishment (legitimate, zero loss, hired gun) increases efficiency relative to no-punishment, but unrestricted punishment reduces efficiency. When MPCR is high (resources abundant), punishment does not improve and may reduce efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the effect of punishment on efficiency should be modeled as conditional on both MPCR and the punishment mechanism. The results are robust to moderate changes in group size and punishment cost ratio, so these dimensions are less likely to moderate the direction of the effect.

- source: Fatas, E., Jimenez-Jimenez, F., & Morales, A. J. (2010). Blind Fines in Cooperatives. *Applied Economic Perspectives and Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that a blind, probabilistic punishment mechanism based on group performance can significantly increase average group quality (success ratio) in a cooperative production setting, but only when the cost of contributing high quality is not too high. In the LC treatment, average group quality rises from about 40% to about 70% of the maximum after punishment is introduced, while in the HC treatment, there is no significant improvement. The mechanism does not require individual monitoring, and its effectiveness depends on the underlying cost structure. The main outcome is group quality, which is closely related to efficiency, though not reported as explicit group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a group-level, blind punishment mechanism can substantially increase group performance (as measured by average quality/success ratio) in public-goods-like environments, but only when the cost of cooperation is not too high. For prediction tasks, the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely to be positive when the marginal cost of cooperation is low relative to the group benefit, and negligible when costs are high. The design features—no chat, fixed group size (n=4), 20 rounds per block, no individual monitoring, and punishment based on group outcome—are all relevant for mapping to prediction settings. The main limitation is that the outcome is group quality, not direct group payoff, but the two are tightly linked in this design.

- source: Jiang, L. L., Chen, Z., Perc, M., Wang, Z., Kurths, J., & Moreno, Y. (2023). Deterrence through punishment can resolve collective risk dilemmas in carbon emission games. *Chaos*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab experiment simulating a collective-risk public goods game (carbon emission game), costly punishment of free-riders (with fines imposed probabilistically and costs shared by all group members) increases the probability that groups reach the collective target, but only when the punishment risk is high (p > 0.8). At low punishment risk, deterrence is weak, and group failure rates remain high. Larger groups are less likely to succeed at any given punishment risk, requiring higher punishment risk for similar deterrence. The introduction of punishment shifts most players to fair-share contributions, reducing both free-riding and maximal altruism. The effect of punishment on group success (a proxy for efficiency) is robust to different loss probabilities. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the probability of reaching the target and average investment are closely related and show a positive effect of punishment when sufficiently strong.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in collective-risk public goods games with a threshold target and probabilistic costly punishment, enabling punishment increases group success rates (and thus efficiency) only when the punishment risk is high. The effect is moderated by group size (larger groups require higher punishment risk) and is robust to different loss probabilities. For prediction, the presence and strength of punishment (punishment risk, fine size, cost structure), group size, and the absence of communication are key moderators. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low group success rates, especially as group size increases. The findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency when punishment is strong and credible, but little or no effect when punishment is weak or infrequent.

- source: Xu, C. Y., Qin, B. T., & Rawlings, D. (2022). Motivational crowding effects of monetary and nonmonetary incentives: Evidence from a common pool resources experiment in China. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a lab CPR game with 5 players per group and 18 rounds, the introduction of mandates with fines (punishment) for over-extraction significantly reduced extraction (i.e., increased conservation and group welfare) compared to a normative information baseline. High fines had a persistent 'crowd-in' effect on conservation, especially among those who initially extracted the least. Low fines had weaker or no persistent effects. In contrast, a public revelation mechanism (where one player's extraction is revealed each round) did not reduce extraction and led to a 'crowd-out' effect among conditional cooperators, likely because the revealed extractions were higher than expected, shifting descriptive norms upward. The study does not report efficiency as a ratio, but the extraction results map directly to group welfare. Heterogeneity analyses show that the effects of punishment and public revelation depend on behavioral type and prosocial orientation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a CPR game with 5 players, continuous extraction choices, no chat, and a known social optimum, the introduction of monetary punishment (especially with high fines) increases group welfare by reducing extraction, both during and after the intervention. The effect is strongest for high fines and among those with low initial extraction. Public revelation mechanisms may backfire, especially among conditional cooperators, if the revealed behavior is less cooperative than expected. For prediction, the presence of monetary punishment (with sufficient magnitude and justification) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to a normative information baseline. The mapping from extraction to efficiency is direct, so these results are highly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in similar CPR or PGG-like environments.

- source: Grimalda, G., Belianin, A., Hennig-Schmidt, H., Requate, T., & Ryzhkova, M. (2022). Sanctions and international interaction improve cooperation to avert climate change. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment tested the effect of sanctions (costly punishment) in a repeated collective-risk social dilemma with German and Russian participants, both in national and international groups. Sanctions increased group cooperation (as measured by the probability of loss avoidance, PLA), especially for Russian participants and in international groups. However, the cost of sanctioning meant that final payoffs were actually higher in the no-sanction treatments, as shown in the supplementary material. The effect of sanctions was positive for increasing cooperation and loss avoidance, but negative for monetary efficiency. The combination of international interaction and sanctions was particularly effective at raising cooperation among low cooperators (Russians), leading to convergence with high cooperators (Germans). The effect of revealing nationality was minimal. Sanctioning was mostly prosocial in international groups, and antisocial sanctioning (punishing high contributors) was reduced. Only a small fraction of the endowment was spent on sanctions, but this was sufficient to increase cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling costly punishment (sanctions) in a repeated collective-risk social dilemma increases group cooperation (PLA) but reduces final payoffs due to the cost of sanctioning. For prediction tasks, this means that the efficiency of the punishment-enabled game may be higher or lower than the control, depending on whether the benefit from increased loss avoidance outweighs the cost of sanctions. In this experiment, the cost of sanctions was high enough that final payoffs were higher in the no-sanction condition, even though cooperation (PLA) was higher with sanctions. The effect is especially pronounced for groups with low baseline cooperation, and in international groups. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, group composition (nationality/culture), and the structure of the risk/loss. The game is a close variant of a PGG, but with a threshold and probabilistic loss, so direct transfer to standard PGGs should be cautious.

- source: Galan, J. M., Latek, M. M., & Rizi, S. M. M. (2011). Axelrod's Metanorm Games on Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of punishment and metapunishment to sustain cooperation in a public-goods-like environment is highly sensitive to the underlying network structure. Specifically, higher clustering and interconnectedness (more triplets per agent) make it more likely that the population will spend most of its time in a cooperative, norm-enforcing state, which is associated with higher expected payoffs. In contrast, higher average degree (more neighbors per agent) can make norm collapse (defection, low punishment) more likely, reducing group welfare. The results are derived from both mathematical analysis of expected payoffs and extensive agent-based simulations, but the main simulation outcomes are behavioral (frequency of norm establishment/collapse) rather than explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, especially when considering the role of network structure. It suggests that punishment (and metapunishment) can lead to higher efficiency (more time in cooperative states) when the network has high clustering and interconnectedness, but may be less effective or even counterproductive in networks with high average degree and low clustering. However, because the paper does not report explicit efficiency or group payoff values, its support for quantitative prediction is limited to qualitative or relative effects based on network features. The findings are most relevant when the game design includes networked interactions, costly punishment, and possibly metapunishment.

- source: de Melo, G., & Piaggio, M. (2015). The perils of peer punishment: Evidence from a common pool resource framed field experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Nonmonetary peer punishment (social disapproval) reduces resource extraction (increases cooperation) in mixed-community groups but has no significant effect in in-group (same community) groups. The effect is measured as a reduction in the average number of nets used per person. There is substantial antisocial punishment (punishing cooperators), which can undermine the effectiveness of punishment. The study does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff, but lower extraction implies higher efficiency. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of a unique social norm limit the positive effect of punishment. The findings suggest that the context (group composition) moderates the effect of punishment on cooperation, and that coordination is needed for punishment to be effective.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that enabling peer punishment (social disapproval) can reduce resource extraction (increase cooperation) in common pool resource games, especially in mixed-group settings. However, the effect on efficiency is not directly measured; it must be inferred from extraction levels. The presence of antisocial punishment and lack of norm consensus can limit or even reverse the positive effect of punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that punishment is more likely to improve efficiency in mixed-group contexts than in homogeneous groups, but the risk of antisocial punishment and unclear norms can reduce or negate efficiency gains. The specific game design (field context, nonmonetary punishment, no communication, continuous extraction choice) should be considered when generalizing.

- source: Shutters, S. T. (2012). Punishment Leads to Cooperative Behavior in Structured Societies. *Evolutionary Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in agent-based simulations of a continuous prisoner's dilemma, cooperation (measured as mean contribution) does not evolve in the absence of both punishment and social structure. When only one of these is present, cooperation also does not evolve. However, when both social structure (local interactions on networks) and the option for costly punishment are present, populations can evolve to high levels of cooperation, depending on the strength of punishment and the network structure. The transition from defection to cooperation requires stronger punishment as network density increases. Scale-free networks are an exception, showing little or no response to punishment. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of both local social structure and effective punishment mechanisms is necessary for the evolution of high cooperation rates in continuous-contribution social dilemmas. However, because the outcome is mean contribution rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings must be interpreted as indirect evidence for efficiency effects. The results suggest that, in structured populations (except scale-free networks), enabling punishment can dramatically increase cooperation rates, which would likely increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this directly. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment is only effective in structured (non-well-mixed) populations, and that the effectiveness of punishment depends on network density and punishment strength.

- source: Wang, Z., Li, R. D., Jin, X., & Ding, H. (2023). Emergence of Social Norms in Metanorms Game With High-Order Interaction Topology. *Ieee Transactions on Computational Social Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in metanorm games on uniform random hypergraphs (URH), increasing group size (G) promotes the emergence of social norms, leading to higher cooperation rates and more agents willing to punish defectors. Network connectivity (number of groups) does not affect norm emergence. On heterogeneous hypergraphs (HRH), standard BV-learning fails to establish norms for 'leaver' agents, but a new dynamic relevance BV-learning algorithm enables both hub and leaver agents to establish norms and cooperate. Real-world hypergraph data (coauthorship networks) show similar patterns. The main findings are about the conditions for norm emergence and high cooperation, not about efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is informative for predicting the effect of punishment on cooperation and norm compliance in public-goods-like games with high-order (group) interactions, especially regarding the role of group size and network structure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that larger group size and effective learning algorithms for punishment/metapunishment can promote cooperation and norm compliance, which may be associated with higher efficiency in related settings, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Korman, A., & Vacus, R. (2021). On the role of hypocrisy in escaping the tragedy of the commons. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that in large networked public goods games with negligible MPCR, cooperation can emerge from a population of mostly defectors if mild but appropriately calibrated punishment (social-pressure) is applied, especially when an intermediate behavioral type (hypocrisy) is present. The emergence of cooperation is highly sensitive to the level of punishment against hypocritical players: too little or too much punishment leads to persistent defection or hypocrisy, while an intermediate level enables a transition to widespread cooperation. The model is theoretical and supported by numerical simulations, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into the conditions under which punishment can promote cooperation in large public goods games with negligible MPCR. It suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the calibration of punishment levels, especially toward intermediate types like hypocrites. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental or empirical settings. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment calibration and the role of intermediate behavioral types in the dynamics of cooperation.

- source: Cheng, H. H., Meng, X. Z., Hayat, T., & Hobiny, A. (2023). Multistability and bifurcation analysis for a three-strategy game system with public goods feedback and discrete delays. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can promote the survival of cooperators and the abundance of public goods, but only when their intensities are sufficiently high. Moderate reward and severe punishment are most conducive to cooperation. Excessively high reward does not always further increase cooperation. The cost of reward has a stronger negative effect on cooperation than the cost of punishment. Initial conditions and time delays can lead to multistability, oscillations, or extinction of cooperation. Large delays are detrimental to cooperation and can lead to the extinction of cooperators. The model highlights the complex interplay between feedback, incentives, and dynamic stability in public goods environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence that the effect of punishment on cooperation and public good abundance is positive when punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly, especially when combined with moderate reward. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, its findings should be interpreted as supporting the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and public good provision under certain parameter regimes, but not as direct evidence for efficiency gains. The results suggest that the effect of punishment is sensitive to the costs of incentives, the presence of reward, and dynamic factors such as delays and initial conditions. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment can improve group outcomes in public-goods-like settings, but the mapping to efficiency is indirect.

- source: Johnson, S. (2015). Escaping the tragedy of the commons through targeted punishment. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in large-N social dilemmas (modeled after public goods games), targeted punishment strategies—where only a small, strategically chosen subset of defectors are punished at a time—can enable a small number of punishers to shift a population from full defection to full cooperation. This is in contrast to standard punishment strategies, which require much higher punishment capacity to achieve the same result. The main outcome is the stationary proportion of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The model is robust to heterogeneity in player predispositions and to some randomness in the ordering of players. The findings are relevant for understanding how to design punishment institutions or strategies to escape the tragedy of the commons, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the structure and targeting of punishment can strongly affect the ability to achieve cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when initial cooperation is low. However, because the model's outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results suggest that, all else equal, targeted punishment strategies may be more effective at increasing cooperation (and thus potentially efficiency) than uniform punishment, but the lack of direct payoff analysis limits quantitative prediction. The model assumes binary (all-or-nothing) contribution choices, no explicit payoff or MPCR parameters, and does not model communication, reward, or information features. Thus, its main value is in highlighting the importance of punishment targeting and heterogeneity, rather than providing parameter-specific efficiency predictions.

- source: Sarkar, S. (2023). Managing ecological thresholds of a risky commons. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a coupled replicator-resource model with inspection and punishment, showing that punishment and monitoring can avert the tragedy of the commons (TOC) and sustain cooperation and resource abundance, but only under certain conditions. At low resource growth rates, even full cooperation (driven by punishment) cannot prevent resource collapse. At moderate growth rates, increasing punishment and monitoring can shift the system from oscillatory TOC to sustainable coexistence. At high growth rates, the resource persists even with high defection, but cooperation is harder to sustain. The model highlights the importance of ecological parameters (growth rate, predation) in moderating the effectiveness of punishment and monitoring. However, the paper does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes in the sense of total group earnings or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining cooperation and resource abundance depends on ecological parameters (especially resource growth rate and predation) as well as institutional parameters (punishment severity and monitoring probability). However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. It is most useful for understanding the conditions under which punishment can prevent resource collapse or sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments with ecological feedbacks. The model structure and qualitative findings may inform priors about when punishment is likely to be effective, but quantitative efficiency predictions are not possible from this paper.

- source: Ringsmuth, A. K., Lade, S. J., & Schlüter, M. (2019). Cross-scale cooperation enables sustainable use of a common-pool resource. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that even weak social sanctioning (punishment) between communities can prevent the collapse of cooperation that would otherwise occur due to biophysical coupling (resource sharing). When only biophysical coupling is present and communities do not communicate or sanction each other, increased resource access can undermine cooperation in one community. However, if communities can sanction defectors in both communities, or if they communicate when setting norms, cooperation is more robust. The model identifies parameter regions where cooperation or defection collapses, depending on the strength of biophysical and social coupling and on whether communities collaborate in norm setting. The findings highlight the importance of cross-community sanctioning and communication for sustaining cooperation in common-pool resource systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment (social sanctioning) in public-goods-like environments can prevent the collapse of cooperation that may occur due to resource sharing or biophysical coupling. However, the model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so its relevance to predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect. The results suggest that the presence and strength of punishment mechanisms, as well as opportunities for communication and norm setting, are important moderators of cooperation stability. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that punishment will have a more positive effect on cooperation (and likely efficiency) when social coupling is present, especially if communities can communicate and set norms collaboratively. However, quantitative predictions about efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., Liang, S., Li, L., & Song, Y. J. (2024). Inter-layer feedback mechanism with reinforcement learning boosts the evolution of cooperation in multilayer network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that adaptive punishment mechanisms, when combined with reinforcement learning in hypergraph-structured PGGs, substantially increase the fraction of cooperators. Key findings include: (1) higher punishment probability and intensity promote cooperation, (2) moderate punishment costs are optimal for sustaining cooperation, (3) smaller group sizes (hyperedges) facilitate earlier and higher cooperation, and (4) higher learning rates and discount factors in Q-learning further enhance cooperation. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, not empirical.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that adaptive punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in PGG-like environments, especially when punishment is likely, strong, and not too costly, and when group sizes are small. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for predicting how design dimensions (punishment cost, intensity, group size, etc.) affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency but are not equivalent. For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides indirect, mechanistic support for the positive effect of punishment on cooperation, but not direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Dodds, W. K. (2005). The commons, game theory and aspects of human nature that may allow conservation of global resources. *Environmental Values*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper argues that human societies have a deeply ingrained tendency to punish cheaters, even at personal cost, and that this tendency supports higher levels of cooperation in commons dilemmas. It references experimental findings (e.g., Fehr and Gächter 2002) showing that allowing punishment in public goods games increases cooperation, but does not provide quantitative data or analyze efficiency or group payoff. The paper also discusses the importance of social norms, education, and regulation in sustaining cooperation and resource conservation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation in public goods game-like environments, but does not provide quantitative evidence or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes. It is useful for understanding the mechanisms and social context of punishment, but does not inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper is best used as background or for mechanism discussion, not for quantitative modeling.

- source: Freytag, A., Güth, W., Koppel, H., & Wangler, L. (2014). Is regulation by milestones efficiency enhancing? An experimental study of environmental protection. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: This laboratory experiment tests whether adding intermediate contribution targets ('milestones') to a cumulative threshold public goods game increases efficiency, measured by group payoffs and the probability of reaching a final target. The game is played in groups of 5 over 6 rounds, with treatments varying the strictness of milestones, the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), and the probability of group loss if targets are missed. The main findings are: (1) In a scenario with no continuous efficiency gains (i.e., only reaching the target matters), strict milestones increase both contributions and the probability of reaching the target, thus enhancing efficiency. (2) In a scenario with continuous efficiency gains (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones actually reduce average contributions and efficiency, as groups focus on meeting milestones rather than maximizing payoffs. (3) In an intermediate scenario, the positive and negative effects cancel out. The effect of milestones is thus context-dependent, and their efficiency impact is not universally positive.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment-like milestone mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the underlying payoff structure. When the only way to generate group payoffs is to reach a threshold (no continuous efficiency gains), strict milestones (punishment for missing intermediate targets) can improve efficiency by increasing coordination and contributions. However, when continuous efficiency gains are present (i.e., contributions always increase payoffs), strict milestones can reduce efficiency, as they may distract from maximizing group welfare. For prediction, this means that the presence and structure of milestone/punishment mechanisms, the marginal per-capita return, and the risk of group loss must all be considered. Control efficiency (without milestones/punishment) is a strong moderator: if control efficiency is already high, milestones may not help and can even hurt. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from design dimensions in cumulative threshold public goods games with milestone/punishment features.

- source: Sääksvuori, L. (2014). Intergroup conflict, ostracism, and the evolution of cooperation under free migration. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper experimentally studies a dynamic public goods game with endogenous group formation, free migration, and treatments varying the presence of intergroup competition and ostracism (social exclusion by majority vote). The main findings are: (1) Ostracism increases average group size and contributions to the public good, resulting in higher net payoffs and aggregate returns, both with and without intergroup competition. (2) Intergroup competition alone does not increase group size or contributions, and leads to more imbalanced group structures, which reduces aggregate returns. (3) The combination of ostracism and competition still produces higher cooperation and payoffs than competition alone. (4) The threat of ostracism reduces the number of free-agents and increases the desirability of joining groups, outweighing the effect of involuntary exclusion. (5) Intergroup competition reduces the likelihood of ostracism, as groups are less willing to expel members when facing external threats.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that enabling a social sanctioning mechanism (ostracism) in a dynamic, migration-allowed public goods environment increases both cooperation and group-level payoffs (i.e., efficiency), regardless of whether intergroup competition is present. The effect of ostracism is robust and positive for efficiency-related outcomes. Intergroup competition alone does not improve efficiency and may even reduce it by fragmenting groups. The design features—such as endogenous group formation, free migration, and ostracism by majority vote—are important moderators. The paper does not use classic costly punishment (with explicit cost per punishment point), but ostracism functions as a group-level sanction. Thus, for environments with similar group formation and migration, the presence of ostracism is a strong positive predictor for efficiency gains from sanctioning.

- source: Bastian, B., Brewer, M., Duffy, J., & Van Lange, P. A. M. (2019). From cash to crickets: The non-monetary value of a resource can promote human cooperation. *Journal of Environmental Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that highlighting the non-monetary value of a resource (e.g., the moral significance of not harming crickets) leads to reduced personal consumption in a resource dilemma game, indicating increased cooperation. This effect is robust across two studies and is not moderated by individual differences in empathy or moral expansiveness. When a punishment mechanism is introduced (participants can pay to fine a greedy player), there is no significant difference in punishment behavior between the moral-value and control conditions. The main outcome is behavioral (points taken), not direct group efficiency or payoff, but the results suggest that moral framing can promote cooperation even in the presence of defection and punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that moral or non-monetary framing of the resource can increase cooperation (reduce consumption) in resource dilemma games, which may translate to higher group efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, and the punishment mechanism (costly punishment of defectors) did not significantly affect behavior. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that game design features that increase the perceived moral value of the resource may enhance cooperation, but the effect of punishment per se on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper. The design is close to a public goods/common pool resource game, but not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are not efficiency or payoff-based.

- source: Brick, K., & Visser, M. (2010). Meeting a national emission reduction target in an experimental setting. *Climate Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Voluntary cooperation alone is insufficient to meet a group emission reduction target in a public good game with heterogeneous players. Allowing communication increases average contributions and the proportion of groups meeting the target (from 35% to 50%), but also increases polarization between full cooperation and free-riding. Introducing a tax-based sanction (automatic fine for under-contribution) leads to near-universal compliance with the target (88–91% of groups meet the target), but also crowds out voluntary contributions above the minimum required. The effect of taxation on contributions above the minimum differs by player type: for capital, taxation reduces excess contributions; for labour, taxation can encourage excess contributions relative to baseline. The study does not directly analyze efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff, but the compliance rates and average earnings suggest that tax-based sanctions are highly effective at achieving the group target.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that automatic, rule-based sanctions (taxes/fines for under-contribution) are highly effective at increasing compliance with group targets in public good games, even with heterogeneous players. Communication alone improves cooperation but is less reliable than sanctions. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of institutional (not peer) punishment on group compliance and contributions, especially in settings with asymmetric player types and marginal returns. However, because the study does not report efficiency in the standard sense (group payoff as a share of maximum possible), its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. Still, the high compliance rates under tax-based sanctions suggest that efficiency would be much higher with punishment enabled than in the voluntary or communication-only conditions.

- source: Lang, M. R., Chvaja, R., & Purzycki, B. G. (2024). The role of costly commitment signals in assorting cooperators during intergroup conflict. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper demonstrates that costly commitment signals can facilitate the assortment of cooperators in public-goods-game-like environments with intergroup competition, leading to higher group efficiency and greater probability of winning. However, the relationship between signal cost and efficiency is not monotonic: low-cost signals are generally more effective at producing efficient, cooperative groups, except when the cost is directly subtracted from the investable endowment during intergroup conflict, in which case high-cost signals can create highly efficient, winning groups. Forced signaling (where participants are assigned to signal or not) does not reliably increase efficiency or group payoff. The study does not include a punishment phase or peer-sanctioning mechanism, so its direct relevance to punishment is limited, but the costly signal serves as a commitment device that can be interpreted as a form of self-imposed cost or pre-commitment. The findings suggest that the design of commitment mechanisms (including their cost structure and voluntariness) is crucial for predicting their effect on group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of costly commitment mechanisms (but not peer punishment) on group efficiency in public-goods-game-like settings with intergroup competition. It shows that voluntary, low-cost signals are generally more effective at producing efficient groups, except when the cost is directly subtracted from the investable endowment, in which case high-cost signals can be effective. Forced (non-voluntary) signaling does not reliably increase efficiency. The findings suggest that the effect of commitment mechanisms on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure, voluntariness, and whether the cost reduces investable resources. However, because the study does not include peer punishment or sanctions, its direct applicability to standard punishment treatments is limited. For prediction tasks involving commitment devices or entry costs, the results are directly relevant; for peer punishment, the transfer value is more indirect.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Liu, Y., Zhao, J. H., & Gu, C. L. (2023). Evolutionary games with environmental feedbacks under an external incentive mechanism. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that external incentives (subsidies/carbon credits) can promote cooperation and improve environmental quality in a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, but the effect is not always positive. Higher subsidies increase the competitiveness of cooperators (both those who reinvest in the public good and those who use credits for personal gain), but can also create conditions where defectors survive by exploiting improved environments, leading to coexistence or even dominance of defectors. The use of incentives to promote cooperation is thus sensitive to the balance between environmental repair and degradation rates, the structure of incentives, and the spatial dynamics of strategy clusters. The model does not include punishment or sanctions in the standard sense, but the 'second-order social dilemma' (where some cooperators act as free-riders on others' altruism) is analogous to the problem of sustaining costly punishment. The main outcomes are cooperation rates and environmental quality, with payoffs determined by these variables.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of external incentives (akin to rewards) on cooperation and group welfare in spatial public goods games with environmental feedback. It suggests that the effect of such mechanisms on efficiency or payoff is highly context-dependent: increasing the incentive can improve cooperation and welfare in some parameter regions, but can also reduce them in others due to complex spatial and strategic dynamics.
