The findings caution against assuming monotonic positive effects of incentives and highlight the importance of considering environmental feedbacks and the possibility of second-order free-riding. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctions as defined in the prediction task, so its direct applicability to punishment effects is limited. The results are most relevant for reward-based or subsidy-based interventions in spatial, environmentally-coupled PGGs.

- source: Howe, E. L., Murphy, J. J., Gerkey, D., & West, C. T. (2016). Indirect Reciprocity, Resource Sharing, and Environmental Risk: Evidence from Field Experiments in Siberia. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment found that voluntary sharing was substantial and directed toward those in need, especially when reputations could be built. Sharing was conditioned on cooperative behavior when such information was available. However, the presence of sharing, rewards, and reputation mechanisms did not increase contributions to the public good (i.e., did not increase cooperation in the production domain). The expected returns from contributing to the group activity, even with the possibility of receiving sharing, were not high enough to incentivize greater cooperation. Thus, the mechanisms studied did not improve group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in environments where sharing is voluntary and not highly efficient, and where rewards/punishments are not strong or direct, the introduction of sharing and reputation mechanisms may not increase group efficiency or cooperation in public goods games. The findings imply that unless the payoff structure makes rewards or punishments highly effective, simply enabling sharing or reputation is unlikely to boost efficiency. For prediction, if the control game has low efficiency and the only change is the addition of a voluntary, non-committal sharing/reward mechanism (with no direct punishment and no high-impact reward), the efficiency in the treatment is unlikely to increase. The paper provides indirect evidence that the effectiveness of punishment/reward mechanisms depends on their efficiency and observability.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Senthilnathan, A., & Gavrilets, S. (2021). The dynamics of cooperation, power, and inequality in a group-structured society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and efficiency in group-structured societies depend strongly on group size, the degree of rivalrousness of goods, and the strength of institutional checks and balances (modeled by the incumbency parameter ε). Stronger checks and balances (lower ε) promote cooperation and reduce inequality. Larger groups tend to be more powerful and successful in between-group cooperation, except when goods are highly rivalrous, in which case smaller groups may outperform larger ones (Olson's paradox). The model predicts multiple stable equilibria and non-equilibrium dynamics depending on parameter values. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms; these are only discussed as possible future extensions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how game design dimensions such as player count, group size, benefit parameters, and rivalrousness affect efficiency and payoff outcomes in nested public-goods-like environments. However, it does not address the effect of punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency impact of enabling punishment. It is useful for understanding baseline efficiency and the role of structural and institutional moderators, but not for predicting the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Zhang, J. L., & Zhang, C. Y. (2015). The evolution of altruism in spatial threshold public goods games via an insurance mechanism. *Journal of Statistical Mechanics-theory and Experiment*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=weak | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in threshold public goods games, offering insurance to cooperators (but not defectors) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies, especially when the compensation for failed games is high and the threshold for success is high. However, if defectors are also allowed to purchase insurance, insured defection can become dominant, leading to a decrease in cooperative behaviors. The network structure (random regular vs. scale-free) also moderates these effects. The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; efficiency or group payoff is not directly reported, but can be inferred from the modeled payoffs. The results suggest that insurance can substitute for some risk-mitigating effects of punishment, but the mechanism is distinct.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it studies insurance as a risk-mitigation mechanism rather than punishment or sanctions. However, it does provide indirect evidence about how risk-mitigation mechanisms (like insurance) can affect cooperation and, by implication, group welfare. The findings suggest that mechanisms which reduce the risk of loss for contributors (such as insurance) can promote cooperation, but if such mechanisms are also available to defectors, they may undermine cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper is most relevant for understanding the effects of risk and insurance, not punishment. It does not provide direct parameterizations for the target game design dimensions related to punishment.

- source: Nockur, L., Arndt, L., Keller, J., & Pfattheicher, S. (2020). Collective choice fosters sustainable resource management in the presence of asymmetric opportunities. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: In a lab common resource game with groups of four, participants played under three systems: individual choice, median choice, and majority voting. In both symmetric and asymmetric opportunity conditions, collective choice systems (median and majority voting) led to lower average extraction rates and higher profits than individual choice. The effect was especially pronounced for disadvantaged group members in asymmetric groups, whose profits increased substantially under collective choice. Advantaged group members' extraction was curtailed under collective choice, but their profits did not increase. Satisfaction and fairness ratings were also higher under collective choice. No punishment or sanctioning mechanisms were present in any condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in common resource games with or without asymmetric extraction opportunities, implementing collective choice mechanisms (median or majority voting) increases efficiency and group payoff compared to individual choice. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for predicting the effect of collective decision rules (not punishment) on efficiency, and less relevant for the downstream task of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is close to a public goods dilemma, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled settings.

- source: Joshi, J., Brännström, & Dieckmann, U. (2020). Emergence of social inequality in the spatial harvesting of renewable public goods. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that when harvesting efficiency is low, all agents evolve to be sedentary and harvest at low rates, resulting in equitable but inefficient resource use. As harvesting efficiency increases, agents become mobile, and resource extraction approaches the maximum sustainable yield, achieving both efficiency and equity. Further increases in harvesting efficiency or reductions in dispersal cost lead to overexploitation and the emergence of social inequality, with a minority of mobile, overexploitative agents and a majority of frugal, sedentary agents. Rapid social learning (impatience) and local information (myopia) further aggravate inequality and inefficiency. The model identifies key moderators of efficiency and equity but does not include or analyze punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how efficiency in spatial public goods–like environments depends on ecological and behavioral parameters such as harvesting efficiency, dispersal cost, consumer density, and information structure. However, it does not provide any evidence about the effects of punishment or sanctioning, as these mechanisms are absent from the model. Therefore, while the findings are useful for predicting efficiency in control (no-punishment) conditions and for understanding the role of mobility and social learning, the paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to the introduction of punishment. It is best used for baseline efficiency estimation and for understanding moderators of overexploitation and inequality in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Yamamoto, T., Ito, H., Nii, M., Okabe, T., Morita, S., & Yoshimura, J. (2019). A single 'weight-lifting' game covers all kinds of games. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper demonstrates mathematically that the weight-lifting game can represent all major two-player cooperation/defection games by adjusting three parameters: the incremental success probabilities from cooperation and the cost-to-benefit ratio. It provides a unified payoff matrix and phase diagrams showing how efficiency and cooperation depend on these parameters. The model is shown to encompass the standard public goods game as a special case when the success probability increases linearly with the number of cooperators. The paper does not consider punishment, sanctions, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is useful for understanding how efficiency and group payoff outcomes in public-goods-like games depend on the underlying payoff structure, especially the cost/benefit ratio and the synergy of cooperation. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or any intervention mechanism, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for baseline modeling of efficiency in the absence of punishment, or for understanding how the structure of the game (e.g., synergy, cost/benefit) sets the stage for potential intervention effects.

- source: Hergueux, J., Henry, E., Benkler, Y., & Algan, Y. (2023). Social Exchange and the Reciprocity Roller Coaster: Evidence from the Life and Death of Virtual Teams. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study links laboratory measures of reciprocity (from a one-shot public goods game) to field measures of reciprocity and project-level outcomes in open-source software teams. It finds that, while teams with more highly reciprocal members can achieve higher success scores (activity percentile) if they survive, they are also more likely to fail. The mechanism is that reciprocity amplifies both positive and negative contribution dynamics: highly reciprocal teams excel when cooperation is high, but are more fragile and prone to collapse after negative shocks or periods of inactivity. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and thus does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency. The outcomes are group performance and survival, not direct PGG efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding the dynamics of reciprocity and cooperation in repeated public goods-like environments without punishment. The absence of punishment is a key design feature. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high reciprocity can lead to both high performance and high fragility, but do not inform how enabling punishment would affect efficiency. Therefore, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful as a control case for environments without punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Zhang, X. M., Chen, W. M., & Wang, X. J. (2023). Cooperation dynamics in collective risk games with endogenous endowments. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a two-stage PGG-CRG model with endogenous endowments, higher perceived risk and lower required contribution ratios increase the proportion of the population that successfully avoids catastrophic loss (target achieved rate). The effect of the first-stage enhancement factor (analogous to MPCR) is non-monotonic: when the required contribution ratio is small, higher enhancement factors help, but at moderate contribution ratios, a moderate enhancement factor is optimal. The model does not include or analyze punishment or sanction mechanisms, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or related outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or collective risk games, as no punishment or sanction mechanism is present in the model. However, it does provide informative context on how other game design parameters (risk, contribution ratio, enhancement factor) affect cooperation and group success in a threshold public goods/collective risk environment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper should not be used as evidence for the effect of punishment, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency or cooperation rates in the absence of punishment.

- source: Frey, U. J. (2017). Cooperative strategies outside the laboratory - evidence from a long-term large-N-study in five countries. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a context-rich, long-term, repeated threshold public goods environment (an online game), the distribution of cooperative strategies differs from laboratory public goods games: free riding is as common as in the lab, but high cooperation is less frequent and conditional cooperation is somewhat less common. These strategies are stable over time and across different group contexts. The results suggest that context matters for the prevalence of cooperative strategies, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor on the effects of punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative about the baseline prevalence and stability of cooperative strategies in a long-term, context-rich threshold public goods environment without punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that in the absence of punishment, free riding remains common, high cooperation is rare, and conditional cooperation is less prevalent than in some lab settings. The findings may help calibrate expectations for control (no-punishment) efficiency in similar environments, but do not inform the effect of enabling punishment.

- source: Shi, Y. D., Zhong, L. X., & Xu, W. J. (2013). Effects of group sensitivity on cooperation in N-person snowdrift game with dynamic grouping. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that in an N-person snowdrift game where agents can adjust their preferred group size based on group sensitivity (a parameter reflecting emotional response to group outcomes), moderate group sensitivity leads to the highest levels of cooperation. Both the cost-to-benefit ratio and group sensitivity affect the evolution of preferred group size and cooperation frequency. There is a functional relationship between the frequency of cooperators and the average preferred group size. However, the paper does not include punishment, sanctions, or direct efficiency/payoff analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how group size dynamics and emotional factors (group sensitivity) can affect cooperation rates, but it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Kelly, D., Claessens, S., Sibley, C. G., Chaudhuri, A., & Atkinson, Q. (2022). Cooperative phenotype predicts climate change belief and pro-environmental behaviour. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individuals who behave more cooperatively in a suite of economic games (including a public goods game) are more likely to believe in climate change and to report pro-environmental behaviors. The relationship between cooperative phenotype and climate change belief/behavior is robust to many demographic and personality controls, but is attenuated by political party support. Mediation analyses suggest that climate change belief may mediate the effect of cooperative phenotype on pro-environmental behavior. However, the study does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or sanctions in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it uses a public goods game as part of a battery to estimate cooperative phenotype, it does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Liu, Y. Y., Liu, L. J., Guo, R. Q., & Zhang, L. (2023). N-player repeated evolutionary trust game under government management. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a government-managed punishment and reward mechanism in a repeated N-player trust game can sustain trust and investment, leading to higher group payoffs. There is a critical threshold of punishment intensity: below it, untrustworthy behavior dominates and payoffs are low; above it, trust and investment are maintained, and payoffs increase. The model also shows that as punishment intensity increases further, the system can reach a stable coexistence of strategies with high trust and investment. The results are robust across different group sizes and parameter values, and are supported by both theoretical analysis and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated trust-game-like environments with group structure, introducing or increasing the intensity of punishment (and associated rewards for reporting) can substantially increase group efficiency/payoff, provided the punishment is above a critical threshold. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so the qualitative prediction that punishment increases efficiency is likely to transfer to PGG-like settings, especially those with repeated interactions and third-party enforcement. The results are most informative for settings where punishment is implemented by an external authority and is contingent on reporting.

- source: Kimbrough, E. O., & Rubin, J. (2015). Sustaining Group Reputation. *Journal of Law Economics & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated trust game with random rematching and group reputation, the introduction of an in-group punishment mechanism (jury) leads to a significant increase in the amount sent by senders, which directly translates to higher total welfare/efficiency. Information sharing about group members' experiences also increases welfare, but mainly by reinforcing positive or negative group reputations (path dependence). The effects of punishment and information sharing are additive rather than superadditive or substitutive. The presence of punishment increases both the amount sent and the amount returned, with the largest gains in the treatment where both punishment and information sharing are present. The effect of punishment is primarily due to deterrence—the threat of being punished is enough to change behavior, not just actual punishment events.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments with group reputation and random rematching, enabling a punishment mechanism (with moderate cost and effectiveness) increases efficiency (total welfare) relative to a control with punishment disabled. The effect is robust and additive with information sharing. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be higher than with punishment disabled, especially when punishment is feasible and not prohibitively costly. The results are most directly applicable to trust games but are relevant for adjacent public-goods-like settings where group reputation and in-group punishment are possible. The paper also provides evidence that the marginal per-capita return (here, the tripling of the amount sent) is important for welfare, and that communication (cheap talk) alone does not have a significant effect. The design dimensions most critical for prediction are the presence and cost of punishment, information sharing, and the structure of the trust game.

- source: Langlois, C. C., & Langlois, J. P. P. (2004). Provisions for noncompliance and treaty value: A game theoretic perspective. *International Studies Quarterly*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated prisoner's dilemma environments with possible unintentional noncompliance (noise), the design of punishment schemes has a large effect on long-run efficiency (treaty value). Probabilistic trigger schemes, which are analogous to mutual punishment phases after a deviation, are self-punishing and reduce efficiency because both the perpetrator and the victim suffer during punishment. Contrite tit-for-tat (CTFT) schemes, which allow for redress (compensation to the victim) and delay retaliation (generosity), achieve much higher efficiency by minimizing the time spent in mutual defection and ensuring that only the guilty party is punished. The paper provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for how efficiency declines with noise under each scheme, and shows that CTFT dominates trigger schemes in most of the relevant parameter space.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the structure of the punishment scheme (especially whether punishment is self-punishing or allows for redress) is a critical moderator of the efficiency impact of punishment in repeated social dilemma games. If the punishment mechanism is costly to the punisher and involves mutual punishment phases (as in standard trigger schemes), efficiency is substantially reduced, especially as noise increases. If the punishment mechanism allows for redress (compensation to the victim) and avoids mutual punishment (as in CTFT), efficiency is much higher. The paper also shows that the speed of retaliation (trigger probability) and the expected duration of punishment phases are important: swifter, shorter punishments are more efficient, but only if they still deter defection. These insights should inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment based on the punishment technology, cost structure, and whether redress is possible.

- source: Yaman, A., Leibo, J. Z., Iacca, G., & Wan Lee, S. (2023). The emergence of division of labour through decentralized social sanctioning. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that decentralized social sanctioning, implemented as a matrix of role-based rules for reward and punishment, enables groups of self-interested agents to learn and maintain efficient divisions of labour. This leads to substantially higher average group payoff compared to groups without sanctioning, or with only altruistic or selfish learning. The effect is robust across different environmental conditions and is close to the theoretical upper bound. Complexity regularization can promote the evolution of simpler, convergent social norms without sacrificing group payoff. The model shows that sanctioning transforms the incentive structure, making it individually rational to take on critical but otherwise unrewarding roles, thus solving the social dilemma.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and computational evidence that enabling decentralized social sanctioning (punishment and/or reward) in public-goods-like environments can substantially increase group efficiency, especially when critical roles are otherwise unrewarding. The effect is robust across a range of environmental parameters and is close to the optimal achievable by global optimization. The model is not a direct PGG but is structurally adjacent, with payoff interdependence and social dilemmas. The findings support the prediction that, all else equal, enabling punishment (and/or reward) will increase efficiency relative to a control with sanctioning disabled, especially in settings where division of labour is required. The model also highlights the importance of norm complexity and the potential for simple sanctioning rules to be highly effective.

- source: Honjo, K., & Kubo, T. (2020). Social Dilemmas in Nature-Based Tourism Depend on Social Value Orientations. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of outcomes in a two-player common-pool resource game depends critically on the social value orientations (SVOs) of the players. When both players are competitive, the Nash equilibrium is often Pareto-inefficient (i.e., group efficiency is low), and this inefficiency is robust to the introduction of fair punishment. In fact, punishment can increase the parameter space in which inefficiency occurs for competitive players. When at least one player is individualistic or prosocial, the Nash equilibrium is almost always Pareto efficient (i.e., high group efficiency). The model demonstrates that punishment does not always improve efficiency and can backfire in certain social contexts.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games is highly sensitive to the social preferences of the players. In environments where players are competitive (i.e., care about outperforming others), introducing fair punishment can worsen efficiency, increasing the likelihood of Pareto-inefficient outcomes. In contrast, with individualistic or prosocial players, efficiency is generally high even without punishment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment does not guarantee improved efficiency and may even reduce it in competitive settings, especially in small, binary-choice, one-shot games. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, single-round, all-or-nothing, common-pool resource dilemmas, but the underlying logic may transfer to similar public goods environments.

- source: Thomadsen, R., & Bhardwaj, P. (2011). Cooperation in Games with Forgetfulness. *Management Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of forgetfulness on efficiency in repeated games with punishment is fundamentally ambiguous: it can either reduce or increase the set of discount factors and game parameters under which full cooperation (and thus maximum efficiency) is sustainable. When the stage game is such that the cooperative action always yields nonnegative payoffs, forgetfulness weakens punishment and makes cooperation harder to sustain, reducing efficiency. However, when the stage game allows for the possibility that a forgetful player can be punished below the minimax, forgetfulness can make severe punishments credible and thus expand the range of parameters supporting cooperation, increasing efficiency. The paper provides explicit formulas and graphical illustrations for these effects in the repeated prisoner's dilemma and a franchising effort game. The results are theoretical and depend on the structure of the stage game and the probability of forgetfulness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games is not monotonic and can be moderated by cognitive or institutional features like forgetfulness. In environments where punishment is only as severe as the stage minimax, introducing forgetfulness will generally reduce the efficiency gains from punishment. However, in environments where forgetfulness allows for below-minimax punishments, punishment can become more effective at sustaining cooperation, potentially increasing efficiency beyond what is possible with perfect recall. For prediction tasks, this means that the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined solely from standard game design dimensions; one must also consider whether the environment allows for forgetfulness and whether the stage game payoffs permit below-minimax punishments. The paper does not provide empirical estimates but gives clear theoretical boundaries and mechanisms.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2017). A remarkable effect of the combination of probabilistic peerpunishment and coevolutionary mechanism on the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences, together with a coevolutionary mechanism allowing players to break and form connections, leads to a substantial increase in the average payoff of all players (i.e., group efficiency) in evolutionary prisoner's dilemma games on networks. This effect is especially pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate values of the punishment coefficient. The combination of these mechanisms expands the parameter range (punishment coefficient r) over which high cooperation and high average payoff are sustained, compared to models with only one or neither mechanism. The effect is less pronounced or absent in regular networks with high average degree.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in evolutionary networked prisoner's dilemma games, enabling a punishment mechanism (specifically, probabilistic peer-punishment based on payoff differences) increases group efficiency (average payoff), especially when combined with a coevolutionary mechanism for network rewiring. The effect is robust across a range of network topologies and average degrees, but is most pronounced in random and scale-free networks and for intermediate punishment cost/effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar networked games is likely to increase efficiency, particularly when players can also adjust their social ties. However, the results are from a simulation model, not a human experiment, and the game is a prisoner's dilemma on a network, not a standard public goods game.

- source: Libois, F. (2022). Success and failure of communities managing natural resources: Static and dynamic inefficiencies. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The model shows that introducing sanctions (punishment) can increase efficiency in CPR games by enabling the enforcement of rules that prevent over-extraction (static inefficiency) and/or promote conservation (dynamic inefficiency). The effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the strength of the sanctioning mechanism relative to the incentives to deviate from cooperation. When sanctions are strong enough, the first-best (fully efficient) outcome can be achieved. With limited sanctions, only partial improvements are possible, and sometimes increasing the resource's value or growth rate can make enforcement harder, reducing efficiency. The model also shows that the number of players, extraction cost, and inequality among players all moderate the effect of sanctions on efficiency. In particular, more players or greater inequality can make conservation harder to enforce, while higher extraction costs can make it easier. The model provides explicit formulas and comparative statics for these effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment (sanctions) on efficiency in CPR-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment depends on the interplay between the sanctioning technology (maximum enforceable sanction), the number of players, the resource's growth rate, extraction cost, and initial value. If sanctions are strong enough relative to the incentives to deviate, enabling rules can be enforced and efficiency increases. If not, efficiency may not improve, or may even decrease if resource value/growth increases incentives to defect. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the logic of how punishment affects efficiency is highly transferable. The paper is especially informative for settings where the punishment mechanism is exogenous and bounded, and where resource dynamics (growth, conservation) are important. For prediction, the model suggests that the effect of enabling punishment depends on whether the sanctioning parameters cross key thresholds relative to the game's other parameters.

- source: Mondal, S. S., Pathak, M., & Chakraborty, S. (2022). Reward versus punishment: averting the tragedy of the commons in eco-evolutionary dynamics. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that both punishment and reward can prevent the tragedy of the commons in a dynamic resource game, but their effects on efficiency differ. Sufficiently strong punishment leads to a stable equilibrium with all cooperators and a fully replete resource, achieving the social optimum (maximum possible group payoff). In contrast, even with strong reward, some defectors persist, so the group does not reach the maximum efficiency. The model also shows that the effect of punishment or reward depends on the initial state and the intensity of the intervention, and that bistability (multiple possible long-run outcomes) can occur. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis, and is not based on empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in dynamic resource games with feedback (and by extension, in public-goods-like environments), enabling punishment (with sufficient intensity) can increase group efficiency and achieve the social optimum, while enabling reward alone cannot fully eliminate inefficiency due to persistent defectors. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is a prisoner's dilemma with resource feedback, and where punishment or reward is implemented as a direct modification of payoffs. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment should be expected to increase efficiency more than enabling reward, especially when the goal is to reach the maximum possible group payoff. However, the model is theoretical and does not address practical implementation details or empirical constraints.

- source: Duéñez-Guzmán, E. A., & Sadedin, S. (2012). Evolving Righteousness in a Corrupt World. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a small benefit to punishers (such as status or a payment from cooperators) destabilizes corrupt equilibria and enables the evolution and stability of 'righteousness'—a state where cooperative punishers dominate and both corruption and defection are suppressed. Righteousness is associated with higher collective payoffs (efficiency/wellbeing) than either corruption or defection. The model demonstrates that egalitarian and harsh punishment (i.e., similar and sufficiently high penalties for defectors and corrupt punishers) are key to achieving righteousness. Power inequalities that favor corrupt punishers stabilize corruption, but removing or reducing these inequalities (and/or providing small benefits to punishers) can shift the population to righteousness. Once established, righteousness is highly stable and resistant to invasion by defectors or corrupt punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that, in public-goods-like environments, enabling punishment (especially when punishers receive even small additional benefits) can increase group efficiency/wellbeing compared to control conditions without punishment or with only corrupt punishment. The model suggests that the design dimensions most relevant for maximizing efficiency gains from punishment are: (1) ensuring punishers are not disadvantaged relative to cooperators (e.g., via small payments or status), (2) making punishment of defectors and corrupt punishers sufficiently harsh, and (3) minimizing power inequalities that allow corrupt punishers to avoid punishment. While the model is not an exact PGG, its structure and outcomes are highly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games, especially regarding the role of corruption, punishment severity, and benefits to punishers.

- source: Gámez, M., López, I., Rodríguez, C., Varga, Z., & Garay, J. (2018). Game-theoretical model for marketing cooperative in fisheries. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that effective punishment (penalty parameter β > 1) stabilizes full cooperation in a marketing cooperative, leading to the group achieving the maximum possible profit (i.e., efficiency). When punishment is limited (β = 1), cooperation is unstable and members tend to defect, reducing group profit. The model is dynamic, with payoffs and strategies evolving over time, but the key result is that sufficiently strong punishment ensures convergence to the efficient outcome. The findings are demonstrated both analytically and through simulation examples.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling sufficiently strong punishment in a public-goods-like environment (here, a marketing cooperative) will increase efficiency, moving the group toward the maximum possible collective payoff. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar: members choose how much to contribute to the group (by selling through the cooperative), and the group can punish defectors. The explicit analysis of payoff functions and the demonstration that efficiency is achieved only when punishment is strong enough (β > 1) are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, especially in settings where the punishment technology is effective and the cost/impact ratio is favorable. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide empirical effect sizes.

- source: Wang, X. L., Wu, J., Shu, G., & Li, Y. (2014). Punishment Based on Public Benefit Fund Significantly Promotes Cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment funded by a public benefit fund (taxes on high earners) significantly increases both cooperation and average payoff (efficiency) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) is more effective than severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage) in raising average payoff. The effect of punishment is stronger when the temptation to defect is higher. The results are robust across square lattice and small world network topologies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong simulation-based evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with costs funded by a public benefit fund) increases group efficiency (average payoff) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game, compared to no punishment. The effect is stronger for slight punishment (low intensity, broad coverage) than for severe punishment (high intensity, narrow coverage). The findings are most directly relevant to spatial PD games with local interactions, not standard PGGs, but the efficiency outcome is directly reported. For prediction tasks, the presence of a punishment mechanism (especially with broad coverage and moderate intensity) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially as the temptation to defect increases.

- source: Ito, H., & Yoshimura, J. (2015). Social penalty promotes cooperation in a cooperative society. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a social penalty (punishment) for non-cooperative actions in already-cooperative societies universally increases cooperation and group welfare in theoretical models of the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games. The penalty reduces the payoff of non-cooperators, shifting the equilibrium toward more cooperation. The effect is robust to the size of the penalty: larger penalties can lead to full cooperation. The results are generalized to non-cooperative games, with the claim that social penalties always promote cooperation and never reduce it. The paper also discusses the implications for real-world legal systems and public goods provision, but does not model or simulate public goods games directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing or increasing punishment for non-cooperation will increase group efficiency and average payoff in non-cooperative games, including those adjacent to public goods games. The results are derived analytically and show that the effect of punishment is always non-negative for cooperation and group welfare. However, the models are not public goods games per se, and do not address multi-player, repeated, or more complex institutional features. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment is externally imposed and not subject to strategic choice by players. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially as punishment becomes more severe or certain.

- source: Barron, D., & Guo, Y. N. (2021). THE USE AND MISUSE OF COORDINATED PUNISHMENTS. *Quarterly Journal of Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in relational/trust games with coordinated punishment, the possibility of extortion (i.e., agents threatening to trigger punishment unless paid, regardless of their effort) can completely undermine cooperation and efficiency. In the baseline model with extortion, equilibrium effort and surplus are zero. However, if institutions can tie the ability to trigger punishment (leverage) to actual effort—such as through public signals of effort or strong dyadic relationships—then positive effort and efficiency can be restored, though often at the cost of rents to agents or the need for strong relationships. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore not universally positive: it depends critically on whether misuse/extortion is possible and whether institutional features can prevent it.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games is highly sensitive to the possibility of extortion and the institutional context. If punishment can be misused (e.g., via action-contingent threats), enabling punishment may reduce efficiency to zero. However, if the game design includes mechanisms that tie punishment power to actual cooperative behavior (e.g., public effort signals, strong dyadic relationships), punishment can support high efficiency. For prediction, this means that the presence or absence of extortion opportunities, and the presence of institutional safeguards, are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency. Game design dimensions that affect the ability to make credible threats, the observability of effort, and the structure of relationships are especially important.

- source: Heller, W. B., & Sieberg, K. K. (2010). Honor among thieves: Cooperation as a strategic response to functional unpleasantness. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation and high group efficiency can emerge and persist in populations where all individuals are fundamentally self-interested and prefer to cheat, as long as some are willing to punish cheaters and some are strategic enough to cooperate when the risk of being punished is high. The presence of 'Strategic Unpleasant' types—who punish cheaters and cooperate only to avoid punishment—can lead to equilibria where most or all players cooperate, resulting in high average payoffs (efficiency). The model provides explicit payoff matrices and equilibrium conditions showing that, under reasonable parameter values (punishment not too costly, punishment more aversive than being cheated, etc.), punishment enables efficient outcomes. The analysis also shows that if punishment is too costly or ineffective, cooperation and efficiency break down.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency, provided that punishment is not excessively costly and is sufficiently effective to deter cheating. The model clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment depends on the cost/impact structure of punishment and the strategic response of players. If the control (no-punishment) game is dominated by cheaters (low efficiency), introducing punishment—even by self-interested types—can shift the equilibrium to high efficiency. The paper's explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions can be used to inform quantitative predictions, especially regarding the importance of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and the strategic incentives for cooperation.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Guo, J. L. (2019). A synergy of punishment and extortion in cooperation dilemmas driven by the leader. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a networked donation game, a single high-degree leader node using a combined punishment and extortion strategy can induce full cooperation in the population with probability close to 1, regardless of initial conditions or the presence of other strategies. This combined strategy yields the highest group and leader payoffs, and is robust to variations in the temptation to defect and the set of available strategies. Pure punishment or pure extortion by the leader is less effective. The results suggest a strong synergy between punishment and extortion in promoting cooperation and maximizing group welfare in networked social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that, in networked prisoner's dilemma/donation games, the presence of a punishment mechanism—especially when combined with extortion and wielded by a central leader—can dramatically increase group payoff and the probability of full cooperation. While the setting is not a standard public goods game, the findings are likely to transfer to PGG-like environments with network structure and leader influence. The results suggest that enabling punishment (especially in combination with other mechanisms) can shift efficiency from low to near-maximal, even when baseline cooperation is low. However, the effect depends on the presence of influential nodes and may not generalize to well-mixed or non-networked settings.

- source: Shen, C., Song, Z., Shi, L., Tanimoto, J., & Wang, Z. (2025). Exit options sustain altruistic punishment and decrease the second-order free-riders, but it is not a panacea. *Science China-information Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that adding an exit option to a prisoner's dilemma with altruistic punishment changes the evolutionary dynamics. In well-mixed populations, exiters replace defectors as the exit payoff increases, but altruistic punishers do not become dominant. In networked populations, exiters can support the coexistence of altruistic punishers, defectors, and exiters via cyclic dominance, but this effect is unstable and depends on the exit payoff. Sometimes, non-punishing cooperators replace altruistic punishers, and the system can exhibit bi-stability between these outcomes. The effect of exiters on cooperation and punishment is nuanced and context-dependent. The paper does not provide direct predictions or measurements of group efficiency or welfare, but the results suggest that exit options can sometimes support, but also sometimes undermine, the mechanisms that sustain cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the presence and incentive level of an exit option (voluntary participation) can affect the evolutionary stability of punishment and cooperation in social dilemmas. While the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, it suggests that exit options can destabilize defection and, under some conditions in networked populations, support the coexistence of altruistic punishment. However, the effect is highly sensitive to the exit payoff and population structure, and exiters can also undermine punishment by enabling non-punishing cooperators to dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of an exit option and its incentive level are important moderators of punishment's effect, especially in structured populations, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: SCHMITT, D. R., & MARWELL, G. (1970). REWARD AND PUNISHMENT AS INFLUENCE TECHNIQUES FOR ACHIEVEMENT OF COOPERATION UNDER INEQUITY. *Human Relations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a dyadic task with unequal cooperative payoffs, the ability to reward (transfer money) is much more effective than the ability to punish (subtract money) in inducing and sustaining high rates of cooperation. Groups where the reward strategy was used spent more than twice as much time cooperating compared to those using punishment or no strategy. Communication between subjects further increased the use of rewards and the likelihood of achieving steady-state cooperation, particularly among males. Punishment, when used alone, was generally ineffective in producing cooperation, and its use declined over time. The study does not report direct measures of group efficiency or total earnings, but the behavioral outcomes suggest that reward-based influence is more conducive to sustained cooperation than punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment and reward on cooperation in a setting adjacent to public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment alone is generally ineffective at inducing sustained cooperation, while reward (and especially the combination of reward and communication) is much more effective. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is dyadic, with asymmetric payoffs and the ability for only one player to reward or punish, which differs from standard PGGs. Thus, the main value for prediction is as a caution that punishment may not always increase cooperation or efficiency, especially when reward is also available and more salient.

- source: Batistoni, T., Barclay, P., & Raihani, N. J. (2022). Third-party punishers do not compete to be chosen as partners in an experimental game. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that third-party punishment and helping are both used as signals of cooperative intent, but helping is a stronger and less ambiguous signal than punishment. Observability increases investments in both, but the possibility of partner choice does not further escalate investments. Higher investments in punishment or helping increase the likelihood of being chosen as a partner and being trusted, but this effect is much stronger for helping. Investments in both are positively associated with actual trustworthiness in a Trust Game. The study does not address group efficiency or total payoff in a public goods game, but provides evidence on the reputational and signaling functions of punishment and helping.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a Dictator Game with third-party punishment/helping and a subsequent Trust Game. The main outcomes are individual-level signaling, partner choice, and trust/trustworthiness, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment is a weaker and more ambiguous signal of cooperative intent than helping, and that reputational incentives may increase costly punishment/helping, but do not escalate under partner choice. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the social and reputational motivations for punishment, not about its effect on group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2021). Cognitive load decreases cooperation and moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment option. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that cognitive load reduces both cooperation and moral punishment in a one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma game with punishment. Hypocritical and antisocial punishment are not significantly affected by cognitive load, but a general punishment bias (punishing regardless of partner behavior) increases under cognitive load. The amount spent on punishment, when punishment occurs, does not differ between conditions. The results suggest that both cooperation and moral punishment require cognitive resources and are not purely intuitive. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a two-player one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, the presence of punishment (and its purposeful use) depends on cognitive resources. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding how cognitive load moderates the behavioral use of punishment and cooperation, not their impact on group efficiency. The game is a Prisoner's Dilemma, not a public goods game, so transfer to PGG settings should be cautious.

- source: Nese, A., O'Higgins, N., Sbriglia, P., & Scudiero, M. (2018). Cooperation, punishment and organized crime: a lab-in-the-field experiment in southern Italy. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that Camorra inmates are highly cooperative and strongly punish defectors, consistent with their internal codes of conduct. However, when external punishment (third-party punishment) is introduced, their cooperation decreases, indicating a rejection of external authority. Ordinary criminals, in contrast, increase cooperation when external punishment is possible, suggesting opportunism. Students' cooperation also decreases with TPP. The main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation and punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The findings highlight the importance of group-specific social norms and attitudes in shaping responses to punishment opportunities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how the introduction of punishment affects cooperation rates in a Prisoner's Dilemma setting, but does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player PD and a three-player PD with third-party punishment, rather than a standard multi-player PGG. The results suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly on efficiency) is highly dependent on group norms and attitudes: punishment can reduce cooperation in some groups (students, Camorra) and increase it in others (ordinary criminals). However, because efficiency is not measured, the paper's direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The evidence is most useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment under different social norms, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes.

- source: Li, S. Y., Ma, S. M., Wang, D. Y., Zhang, H. J., Li, Y. Z., Wang, J. X., Li, J. Y., Zhang, B. Y., Gross, J., De Dreu, C. K. W., Wang, W. X., & Ma, Y. N. (2022). Oxytocin and the Punitive Hub-Dynamic Spread of Cooperation in Human Social Networks. *Journal of Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in repeated networked games (trust game, ultimatum bargaining, and two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment), oxytocin administration to central (hub) players increases their willingness to enforce cooperation norms via costly punishment. This enforcement, rather than mere cooperation or trust, is what enables cooperation to spread throughout the network. Agent-based simulations confirm that high punishment rates by central nodes are more effective than high cooperation rates alone in promoting network-wide cooperation. Manipulating peripheral nodes' punishment or cooperation has much weaker effects. The main outcomes are cooperation and punishment rates, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some payoff equality measures are reported in the ultimatum game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence and effectiveness of costly punishment, especially when wielded by central/influential players, is a key moderator of cooperation in repeated networked social dilemmas. However, the main outcomes are behavioral (cooperation, punishment, norm enforcement), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that, in networked repeated games with punishment, enabling effective punishment by central players can substantially increase cooperation rates, and by extension, may improve efficiency, but this is not directly measured. The results are most relevant for predicting behavioral outcomes (cooperation, norm enforcement) rather than efficiency as defined by group payoff relative to full cooperation. The design dimensions most strongly supported are network structure (heterogeneity, centrality), punishment existence and effectiveness, and repeated interaction. The study does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency change due to punishment, so its use for predicting efficiency outcomes is indirect and should be supplemented with studies that report group payoff or efficiency directly.

- source: Pansini, R., Shi, L., & Wang, R. W. (2016). Women Tend to Defect in a Social Dilemma Game in Southwest China. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, women in Southwest China defect more and cooperate less than men, a pattern that contrasts with previous findings from Western and other Asian samples. Both genders earned less when they punished, and there was no significant gender difference in overall monetary earnings. The use of punishment did not increase individual earnings, and the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total welfare. The main findings are about behavioral strategies and gender differences, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in a dyadic iterated Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment option, punishment is used more frequently than in comparable Western samples, but its use is associated with lower individual earnings. The design is not a public goods game but a two-player PD, so direct prediction for PGG efficiency is limited. The main decision support is that punishment, as implemented here, does not increase individual payoffs and may reduce them. The paper does not report group efficiency or the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments is limited.

- source: Xie, E. H., Liu, M. D., Liu, J. Q., Gao, X. X., & Li, X. C. (2022). Neural mechanisms of the mood effects on third-party responses to injustice after unfair experiences. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that individuals who experience disadvantageous unfairness (receiving less than others) are more likely to choose third-party punishment in a subsequent justice game, while those who experience advantageous unfairness (receiving more than others) are more likely to choose third-party compensation. These effects are mediated by mood (negative emotion increases punishment, positive emotion increases compensation) and sense of unfairness. Neural data show that increased DLPFC activity is associated with compensation after advantageous unfairness, while decreased DLPFC activity is associated with punishment after disadvantageous unfairness. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a standard public goods game but a third-party justice game with costly punishment and compensation options.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs, focusing on third-party responses to unfairness rather than first-party cooperation or group provision. However, it provides informative evidence about the psychological and neural mechanisms that may underlie punishment and compensation choices, and how prior experiences and mood can shift the likelihood of punishment versus compensation. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not address how punishment affects group payoff or efficiency.

- source: Bone, J. E., McAuliffe, K., & Raihani, N. J. (2016). Exploring the Motivations for Punishment: Framing and Country-Level Effects. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment behavior in a two-player stealing game is strongly influenced by country of origin. US-based participants are more likely to punish in response to both losses and disadvantageous inequality, while India-based participants are more punitive overall and engage in more antisocial punishment, but only increase punishment when experiencing disadvantageous inequality. Methodological factors such as stake size and punishment cost (within the tested range) do not significantly affect punishment rates. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and all outcome measures are behavioral (punishment frequency) rather than payoff-based.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not report or analyze efficiency, welfare, or total earnings. However, it provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior varies with country of origin and with the presence of disadvantageous inequality, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is likely to be used. The design is adjacent to PGGs (two-player stealing game with punishment), but not a PGG or threshold public goods game. The findings suggest that punishment rates and motives can vary substantially by context and population, which may moderate any efficiency effects in related games.

- source: Schlaepfer, A. (2018). The emergence and selection of reputation systems that drive cooperative behaviour. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a population of initially non-cooperative individuals, a reputation system based on punishment is likely to emerge first and drive the initial evolution of cooperation. However, once cooperation is established, it is mainly sustained by a reputation system based on cooperative actions (social image). The model shows that cooperation rates are highest when both punishment-based and cooperation-based reputation systems are available. The emergence and stability of these systems depend on the costs of information acquisition (gossip) and the relevance of active gossip channels. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but focuses on cooperation rates and the use of reputation systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-based reputation systems can help establish cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, especially when information about others' behavior is costly to acquire. However, it does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a standard public goods game (it uses a dyadic Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage). The findings suggest that punishment may be most important for the initial emergence of cooperation, but less so for its maintenance. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only indirectly informative, mainly by highlighting the role of punishment-based reputation in overcoming initial defection.

- source: Li, J. B., Pan, J. J., Zhu, C. K., & Wang, Y. W. (2022). Inter-brain synchronization is weakened by the introduction of external punishment. *Social Cognitive and Affective Neuroscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of external punishment (i.e., making responder rejection payoff-relevant in the rrUG) leads to significantly higher mean offers from proposers compared to the rrDG (no punishment). However, the main focus is on neural outcomes: inter-brain synchronization is significantly lower in the punishment condition (rrUG) than in the no-punishment condition (rrDG). There is no significant effect of punishment on the rejection rate of responders. The study interprets the neural results as evidence that punishment crowds out moral motivation, reducing shared cognitive/emotional processing between players. No direct measures of group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (via responder rejection in a two-player ultimatum game) increases proposer generosity (mean offer size), which could be associated with higher group payoffs if responders accept. However, the study does not report group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games but is not a PGG or close variant. The main outcome is neural (inter-brain synchronization), not payoff-based. Thus, the paper offers only weak, indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Bauer, M., Cahlfková, J., Chytilová, J., Roland, G., & Zelinsky, T. (2023). Shifting Punishment onto Minorities: Experimental Evidence of Scapegoating. *Economic Journal*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when a member of the punisher's own group is harmed by a wrongdoer, majority-group punishers are much more likely to punish an innocent minority (Roma) scapegoat than an innocent majority scapegoat. The amount of punishment assigned to the scapegoat increases with the intensity of harm done by the wrongdoer, and this increase is twice as large when the scapegoat is from the minority group. No ethnic bias is observed when no harm is done. The effect is robust to controls and is not explained by collective responsibility, over-attribution of blame, or unconditional spite. The design does not involve a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides direct evidence on punishment behavior and ethnic bias in scapegoating.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or social dilemma settings, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in such games. However, it provides high-quality evidence on how punishment behavior can be biased by group identity and context, especially in situations where punishment is displaced onto innocent parties. The punishment mechanism here is highly effective (low cost, high impact), and punishment is purely destructive. The findings may be relevant for understanding the social and psychological moderators of punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes in standard PGG-like environments.

- source: Rommel, J. (2015). What can economic experiments tell us about institutional change in social-ecological systems?. *Environmental Science & Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
