  findings: The study finds that allowing third-party punishment of traders (who cause negative externalities) leads to a significant reduction in market entry rates compared to the baseline, especially for sellers. About 86% of observers are willing to punish, spending on average more than half their endowment to do so. The conditional entry treatment also reduces market entry, as about a quarter of participants condition their entry on others' behavior, reflecting norm conformity. The main outcome is the proportion of participants who choose to trade (i.e., cause the externality), not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings show that punishment can enforce social norms and reduce harmful market behavior, but the study does not measure efficiency as defined in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the availability of costly punishment reduces the frequency of norm-violating behavior (trading that causes negative externalities) in a large market setting. However, because the outcome is market entry rate (not group efficiency or total payoff), the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency in public goods games. The punishment mechanism is effective at deterring harmful behavior, especially among sellers, and most observers are willing to punish at a cost. For prediction tasks, the main signal is that enabling punishment reduces the rate of norm violation, but the mapping to efficiency or group payoff in standard PGGs is indirect. The market structure, one-shot design, and externality to a third party differ from standard PGGs, so caution is needed in transferring quantitative effects.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Kteily, N. S. (2023). How reputation does (and does not) drive people to punish without looking. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that reputational incentives can increase the likelihood that individuals engage in punishment (signing punitive petitions), and as a byproduct, some of this punishment occurs without considering opposing perspectives. However, when the process of 'looking' is made observable, individuals are more likely to consider opposing perspectives, and punishment without looking does not increase. Evaluators (the audience) reward punishment in general, but do not reward punishment without looking; in fact, they see it as less fair and competent. The effect is stronger with more ideological audiences. The study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency, but rather focuses on reputational and behavioral outcomes in a Dictator Game context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or social dilemmas. Its design is adjacent: it uses a reputational Dictator Game with real-world punitive actions (signing petitions) and manipulates observability of punishment and information-gathering. The main outcomes are behavioral (punishment, looking) and reputational (evaluations, money shared as a reward), not group efficiency or welfare. The findings suggest that making punishment observable increases punishment (and punishment without looking), but making deliberation observable increases deliberation. For prediction tasks about efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about how reputational incentives can increase punishment rates, but not about their effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Weiss, M., Saulin, A., Iotzov, V., Hewig, J., & Hein, G. (2023). Can monetary incentives overturn fairness-based decisions?. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that offering monetary incentives that conflict with fairness-based punishment or compensation reduces the frequency of fairness-congruent decisions and slows the decision process (lower drift rate in DDM). This effect is similar for both punishment and compensation domains. The effect of conflicting incentives is stronger at higher incentive levels. The study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, but rather focuses on behavioral and cognitive process outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that monetary incentives can undermine fairness-based punishment and compensation behavior, reducing the likelihood and efficiency of such decisions. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, using a modified dictator game with punishment/compensation framing, but lacks repeated interaction, group structure, or explicit efficiency measures. The main predictive value is in showing that the cost and alignment of incentives can strongly moderate the behavioral impact of punishment mechanisms.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2017). WHY THE WEST BECAME WILD Informal Governance with Incomplete Networks. *World Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the ability of peer punishment to sustain cooperation depends critically on the social network structure and the reach of gossip. Incomplete networks with peripheral members cannot sustain full cooperation: the most peripheral members are likely to be persistently cheated and ostracized, as their negative gossip does not reach enough punishers to deter defection. Shocks that increase the gains from defection or that create more peripheral positions (e.g., through rapid population growth or enclave settlement) lead to a ratcheting down of cooperation, with more members becoming persistent cheaters. The model generates hypotheses about when and where cooperation will break down, but does not provide quantitative predictions about group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insight into how network structure and information transmission affect the limits of peer punishment in sustaining cooperation. It is relevant for understanding moderators of punishment effectiveness, especially the role of network incompleteness and peripheral positions. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that punishment will be less effective at increasing efficiency in settings with incomplete networks, high population turnover, or enclave settlement, but does not provide parameterized estimates or control-treatment efficiency comparisons.

- source: Pan, Q. H., Wang, Y., & He, M. F. (2022). Impacts of special cooperation strategy with reward and punishment mechanism on cooperation evolution. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing special cooperators who can both reward and punish leads to a variety of stable states, including coexistence of all three strategies or pairs of strategies. Punishment is necessary to suppress defectors and enable cooperation, but excessive punishment can reduce the prevalence of special cooperators. Rewards only promote ordinary cooperation when punishment is at least moderate. The effects of reward and punishment on cooperation are non-linear: moderate levels are most effective, while too much of either can be counterproductive. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so findings are limited to behavioral strategy frequencies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment (especially when combined with reward) can promote the prevalence of cooperative strategies in spatial evolutionary games. However, because the outcomes are proportions of strategy types rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The findings suggest that moderate punishment is most effective for promoting cooperation, and that the effect of reward depends on the level of punishment. For prediction of efficiency, this paper suggests that the relationship between punishment and efficiency may be non-monotonic, with moderate punishment being optimal, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Lee, H. W., Cleveland, C., & Szolnoki, A. (2024). Suppressing defection by increasing temptation: The impact of smart cooperators on a social dilemma situation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that adding a 'smart cooperator' strategy (who both punishes defectors and rewards cooperators, at a cost) to a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to cyclic dominance among defectors, cooperators, and smart cooperators. This cyclic dominance produces counter-intuitive effects: for example, increasing the temptation to defect can reduce the prevalence of defectors by indirectly supporting smart cooperators. Similarly, increasing punishment does not always reduce defection, and lowering the cost of smart cooperation can benefit unconditional cooperators more than smart cooperators. The main reported outcomes are the stationary fractions of each strategy, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how punishment and reward mechanisms interact in a spatial social dilemma with multiple strategies, but does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that the introduction of complex sanctioning strategies can lead to non-linear and sometimes counter-intuitive effects on cooperation and defection rates, but the lack of efficiency or payoff reporting limits its direct utility for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments. The main value is in highlighting the potential for cyclic dominance and unexpected behavioral dynamics when multiple sanctioning strategies are present.

- source: Zhao, S. S., Pan, Q. H., Zhu, W. Q., & He, M. F. (2023). How punishing evil and promoting good promotes cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'punishing evil and promoting good' strategy can promote the survival of cooperation in social dilemmas. Increasing punishment strength alone can induce the emergence of this strategy and ordinary cooperators, but is not always sufficient to eliminate defectors. Increasing reward strength can shift the system from full defection to full cooperation when the temptation to defect is low, but is insufficient when temptation is high. The most robust promotion of cooperation occurs when both punishment and reward are present, with certain combinations of minimum values able to eliminate defectors entirely. The results are presented as equilibrium proportions of each strategy, not as efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that both punishment and reward mechanisms can promote cooperation in social dilemma settings, and that their effects are complementary. However, because the outcomes are reported as equilibrium strategy frequencies rather than efficiency or group payoff, the results are only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is adjacent to public goods games (it is a prisoner's dilemma variant), and the findings suggest that the presence and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms are important moderators of cooperation, but do not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency.

- source: Wang, R. Y., & Ng, C. N. (2015). Can Centralized Sanctioning Promote Trust in Social Dilemmas? A Two-Level Trust Game with Incomplete Information. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a centralized sanctioning authority into a repeated trust game with incomplete information can increase trust and reciprocity, especially when actors believe the authority is likely to punish defectors. The model shows that the threat of centralized punishment relaxes the conditions under which trust is placed and honored, compared to the baseline trust game without an authority. The effect is strongest when there is optimism about the authority's willingness to punish. The model predicts that centralized sanctioning can mitigate the end-game effect (decline in cooperation in final rounds). However, the analysis is theoretical and does not provide explicit efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that adding a centralized punishment mechanism to a trust-based social dilemma can increase trust and cooperation, especially under incomplete information. While the model is not a public goods game, it is adjacent and relevant for predicting the effect of centralized punishment in repeated social dilemmas. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling centralized punishment is likely to increase cooperation and, by implication, group payoffs or efficiency, particularly when the punishment is sufficiently strong and actors believe the authority is likely to punish. However, the paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency or group payoff, so its value for direct efficiency prediction is limited to qualitative inference.

- source: Vollan, B., Blanco, E., Steimanis, I., Petutschnig, F., & Prediger, S. (2020). Procedural fairness and nepotism among local traditional and democratic leaders in rural Namibia. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that both traditional chiefs and democratically elected leaders in rural Namibia are similarly likely to implement democratic decision-making procedures and are similarly unlikely to engage in nepotistic punishment. Chiefs may be slightly more likely to choose democratic procedures and less likely to be lenient toward relatives in punishment, but these differences are not statistically robust. The main outcomes are behavioral (procedural choice, nepotism in punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The punishment task is a trust game with third-party punishment, not a public goods game. The study suggests that, in this context of legal pluralism, traditional leaders are not more autocratic or nepotistic than elected leaders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on punishment behavior (altruistic punishment) and procedural fairness in a trust game with third-party punishment, not in a public goods game. It does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it does show that, in a real-world setting, leaders' willingness to punish norm violations is not strongly affected by whether they are democratically elected or traditional chiefs, and that nepotism in punishment is low for both. The design and context differ from standard PGGs, so the transfer value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs is limited.

- source: Tang, Z. X., Qu, C., Hu, Y., Benistant, J., Moisan, F., Derrington, E., & Dreher, J. C. (2023). Strengths of social ties modulate brain computations for third-party punishment. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment increases as the social distance between the punisher and the norm violator increases. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment assigned, but the effect of social distance remains robust. Computational modeling shows that participants integrate social distance, inequity aversion, and cost to punish when making punishment decisions. fMRI results identify brain regions involved in these computations. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and thus does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the willingness to punish norm violators depends on the social distance between the punisher and the violator, and that costliness of punishment reduces punishment levels. However, because the experiment is a third-party punishment variant of the Dictator Game (not a public goods game), and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of social distance and cost, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes.

- source: Bartos, V., & Levely, I. (2021). Sanctioning and trustworthiness across ethnic groups: Experimental evidence from Afghanistan. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that during periods of scarcity (lean season), participants are significantly less willing to engage in costly third-party punishment to enforce sharing norms, as measured by a lower minimum acceptable offer (MAO) in the TPPG. However, the amount shared by dictators (Player A) in both the DG and TPPG remains stable across seasons, indicating that sharing behavior is robust to scarcity even as enforcement weakens. The results are robust to various controls and alternative explanations. No direct analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare is provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the willingness to punish norm violations (i.e., enforce sharing) is sensitive to resource scarcity, but actual sharing behavior is not. However, because the design is a one-shot dictator/TPPG (not a repeated public goods game), and because no group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated PGG-like environments. The main implication is that punishment may become less frequent or intense under scarcity, but this does not necessarily reduce sharing in the short run. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be context-dependent and potentially weaker under scarcity, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shen, C., He, Z. X., Shi, L., Wang, Z., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Prosocial punishment bots breed social punishment in human players. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a fraction of bots that always cooperate and punish defectors (CP) into a population of normal players in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma can significantly increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment among normal players. This effect is stronger when bots are placed on high-degree nodes in scale-free networks or when players have a conformist bias (copying the majority). The effect is robust to different network structures, learning rules, and parameter values. However, the main reported outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies), not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not report the effect of punishment on efficiency or total group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the conditions under which punishment (specifically, prosocial punishment implemented by bots) can increase the prevalence of prosocial punishment and cooperation in a two-stage Prisoner's Dilemma. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings may be useful for understanding how network structure, imitation rules, and the presence of committed punishers affect the spread of prosocial punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Gao, D. D., & Guo, W. (2025). Evolutionary game and simulation analysis of tripartite subjects in public health emergencies under government reward and punishment mechanisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that government reward and punishment mechanisms (subsidies and fines) for pharmaceutical companies and the public promote compliant production and cooperation in prevention and control, respectively. Increasing the magnitude of rewards or punishments accelerates the convergence to full compliance and cooperation. The initial willingness of parties to participate also affects the speed of convergence. However, the model does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and all results are in terms of the probability of adopting certain strategies and the speed of convergence to stable equilibria.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical support for the idea that increasing the strength of punishment and reward mechanisms can promote compliance and cooperation in a multi-party public-goods-like setting. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical results. The findings may inform the qualitative direction of punishment effects in similar environments, but cannot be used for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes as required by the downstream task.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Li, L., Shang, J., & Zhao, H. (2024). Periodic update rule with Q-learning promotes evolution of cooperation in transition with mechanism. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a punishment mechanism, where cooperators can choose to become punishers at a cost, increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect is strongest when the punishment cost is low and the update period for strategy revision is short. The Q-learning-based periodic update rule (switching between Fermi and aspiration-driven rules) further enhances cooperation. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The model is not a public goods game but a spatial pairwise prisoner's dilemma with punishment and learning dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas, especially when punishment is not too costly and agents update strategies frequently. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it should be used only as weak, adjacent evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The findings suggest that low punishment cost and frequent update opportunities are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, but the translation to efficiency is uncertain.

- source: Lönnqvist, J. E., Rilke, R. M., & Walkowitz, G. (2015). On why hypocrisy thrives: Reasonable doubt created by moral posturing can deter punishment. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in ultimatum games, proposers can reduce the likelihood of being punished for unfair offers by claiming that a random process (coin flip) determined the outcome. This effect holds even when responders do not believe the claim and is observed for both direct victims (responders) and third-party observers (judges). The reduction in punishment is not explained by responders' self-interest or gratitude. The studies do not report on group efficiency or total earnings, but the findings suggest that moral posturing can deter punishment and thus may allow unfair behavior to persist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of plausible deniability (moral posturing) can reduce the frequency of punishment in bargaining games, even when punishment is available and even if the claim is not believed. However, the study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The findings are most relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, especially the role of uncertainty or doubt about intent, rather than the direct effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Xie, Y. Y., Zhang, S. H., Zhang, Z. P., & Bu, H. Y. (2020). Impact of binary social status with hierarchical punishment on the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing only a subset of agents (the 'powerful' group) to punish defectors can promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma, especially when the punishment fine is sufficiently high. However, the relationship is non-linear: too many powerful agents or excessively strong punishment can reduce cooperation. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of hierarchical punishment on cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent to public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment. The results suggest that the structure and exclusivity of punishment rights, as well as the intensity of punishment, can have non-monotonic effects on cooperation, which may be relevant for interpreting similar mechanisms in public goods games. Use this paper for qualitative or mechanistic insight, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., Wang, Z., & Wang, R. W. (2022). Asymmetric strategy setup solve the Prisoner's Dilemma of the evolution of mutualism. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing asymmetric carrot-stick strategies (where one population can reward or punish the other) leads to a rich set of evolutionary outcomes, including stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, bistability, and dominance, depending on parameter values. The presence and strength of punishment and reward (relative to benefit) are key moderators. The model suggests that asymmetric sanctions can help maintain cooperation and avoid the tragedy of the commons, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. However, the analysis is entirely theoretical and focuses on strategy frequencies and stability, not on group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that the availability and strength of punishment and reward mechanisms (especially when asymmetric) can qualitatively change the evolutionary dynamics of cooperation. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that punishment (and reward) can stabilize cooperation under certain parameter regimes. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that the presence and magnitude of punishment and reward (relative to benefit and cost) are important moderators, but it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment/reward can sustain cooperation, rather than for direct prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Guo, T., Du, C. P., & Shi, L. (2024). Evolution of cooperation on interdependent networks: The impact of asymmetric punishment. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that asymmetric punishment, where only one group can punish another and only across network layers, can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators in spatial prisoner's dilemma games on interdependent networks. The effect is stronger when the punishment is more severe (higher fine per unit cost). When the temptation to defect is low, punishment is more effective than reward at promoting cooperation; when the temptation is high, reward becomes more effective. The main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The paper does not report efficiency or total group payoff, so its findings are most directly relevant to predicting cooperation rates rather than efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of spatial prisoner's dilemma games with asymmetric punishment, increasing punishment strength or effectiveness increases cooperation rates. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results suggest that, in similar environments, enabling or strengthening punishment will likely increase cooperation rates, which may be correlated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly tested. The paper also shows that the effect of punishment depends on the temptation to defect and the cost-to-fine ratio. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design (asymmetry, cost-to-fine ratio) affects cooperation rates, with only indirect implications for efficiency.

- source: Zhao, C. Y., Zheng, G. Z., Zhang, C., Zhang, J. Q., & Chen, L. (2024). Emergence of cooperation under punishment: A reinforcement learning perspective. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that punishment can promote cooperation in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning agents, but only if the punishment is sufficiently strong and not too costly. If the cost of punishment is high or the punishment intensity is low, introducing punishment can actually reduce cooperation below the baseline with no punishment. The system can exhibit both continuous and discontinuous transitions in cooperation as punishment parameters are varied. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; group efficiency or total payoff is not reported. The findings are based on agent-based simulations, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, intensity, probability) affect cooperation rates in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma with reinforcement learning. While the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, the results suggest that punishment does not always improve group outcomes: if punishment is too costly or too weak, it can reduce cooperation. For prediction tasks, this implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency may be non-monotonic and sensitive to cost-effectiveness. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings should be used as qualitative, mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Niu, X. T., Xu, J. W., & Deng, Z. H. (2017). Conditional punishment resolves social dilemma in spatial network. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that introducing and increasing the probability of social punishment (punishers who pay a cost to penalize defectors) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma model leads to a higher steady-state density of cooperators. When the willingness to punish is high, defectors are eliminated and cooperation dominates. The mechanism is shown to be robust across a range of temptation-to-defect values. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates and spatial patterns).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation rates in spatial prisoner's dilemma settings, but does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only indirectly informative: higher cooperation rates may imply higher efficiency, but the cost of punishment and its effect on total group payoff are not analyzed. The model is also not a public goods game, but an adjacent two-player game with spatial structure. Use this paper as indirect support for the behavioral effect of punishment, but not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Zhang, Y. J., Zeng, Z. Y., Pi, B., & Feng, M. Y. (2023). An evolutionary game with revengers and sufferers on complex networks. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a revenge-based costly punishment mechanism in the PDG on networks increases the frequency of cooperation, especially when the cost to the punisher is low and the cost to the punished is high. The effect is robust across network types (square lattice and small-world), cost-to-benefit ratios, and network sizes. However, the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff. The model does not report on group welfare, surplus, or total earnings, so direct implications for efficiency are not available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment mechanisms (specifically, revenge-based costly punishment) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemma games on networks. However, because the main outcome is the frequency of cooperation and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The model structure and parameterization may inform mechanistic expectations, but quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Zhang, M., Zhang, X., Qu, C. Q., Wang, G. H., & Lu, X. (2022). The combination of social reward and punishment is conducive to the cooperation and heterogeneity of social relations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that a combined reward-punishment mechanism (RPM) in a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma can significantly increase the average frequency of cooperation compared to either pure punishment or pure reward alone. Pure punishment only promotes cooperation under high temptation to defect, while pure reward can actually undermine cooperation. The combination of both, with appropriately tuned intensities, sustains high cooperation even under strong temptation to defect. The model also shows that the RPM leads to spontaneous emergence of network heterogeneity, with cooperators becoming influential hubs. However, the main outcome is cooperation frequency, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence and calibration of both punishment and reward mechanisms can strongly affect cooperation rates in dynamic networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a dynamic network prisoner's dilemma rather than a standard public goods game, the findings should be transferred to efficiency prediction with caution. The results suggest that in environments where both punishment and reward are available and appropriately balanced, cooperation (and likely efficiency) can be sustained at higher levels than with either mechanism alone, especially under high temptation to defect. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Wang, G. C., Su, Q., & Wang, L. (2022). Evolution of cooperation with joint liability. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that joint liability, when combined with costly punishment, strongly promotes the evolution of cooperation in a lending game. Punishment alone is insufficient to sustain cooperation, but joint liability provides incentives for individuals to punish defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and low levels of defection. The condition for cooperation to evolve is relaxed from β−γ>4c (without joint liability) to β>γ (with joint liability and low tolerability for defection). The effect is robust to model extensions, including intragroup donation games (Prisoner's Dilemma). The abundance of cooperators and punishers is maximized at intermediate values of selection intensity, population size, and mutation rate. The model is adjacent to public goods games but differs in structure and outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the combination of joint liability and costly punishment can greatly increase cooperation in group settings similar to, but not identical with, public goods games. The findings suggest that punishment is much more effective at promoting cooperation when joint liability is present, and that the threshold for punishment to be effective is lower. However, because the model is not a standard PGG and does not report efficiency as a ratio of realized to maximum group payoff, the results should be transferred to PGG efficiency predictions with caution. The paper is most informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group-level liability interact, and for predicting that punishment will be more effective in environments with joint liability or similar group-level consequences.

- source: Woodcock, S., & Heath, J. (2002). The robustness of altruism as an evolutionary strategy. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms, such as enforcement and ostracism, dramatically increase the robustness of altruism in evolutionary simulations of the prisoner's dilemma. While group selection and imitation can help sustain altruism under certain conditions (e.g., small group size, high imitation rates), punishment allows altruism to persist in much larger groups and under a wider range of conditions. The key mechanism is that punishment increases correlation among altruists, allowing them to interact more with each other and less with defectors. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare as outcomes; it focuses on the evolutionary persistence of altruism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that punishment mechanisms can greatly increase the persistence of altruism (cooperation) in evolutionary environments similar to the prisoner's dilemma. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of altruists rather than group efficiency or payoff, the findings are only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency in public goods games. The results suggest that, all else equal, adding punishment mechanisms is likely to increase cooperation and thus could plausibly increase efficiency, but the paper does not quantify this effect in payoff terms. The design dimensions most relevant here are the presence and effectiveness of punishment, group size, and the structure of interaction (e.g., group partitioning, imitation).

- source: Cofnas, N. (2018). POWER IN CULTURAL EVOLUTION AND THE SPREAD OF PROSOCIAL NORMS. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that mainstream cultural evolutionary models, which treat punishment as an individual-level, uncoordinated action, fail to capture the collective, power-based enforcement of prosocial norms observed in real-world societies, especially among hunter-gatherers. It contends that collective punishment and coordinated norm enforcement are central to the evolution of morality and prosociality, and that these processes are not well-modeled by standard public goods game frameworks. The paper reviews anthropological evidence for collective punishment and discusses how this shapes moral psychology, but does not report or synthesize efficiency or payoff outcomes from experimental games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical context for interpreting the role of punishment in public-goods-game-like environments, emphasizing that real-world punishment is often collective and coordinated, not merely the sum of individual actions. However, it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it analyze how game design dimensions (such as player count, rounds, or punishment cost) moderate these effects. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that models which ignore collective decision-making and power dynamics may misrepresent the real impact of punishment, but it does not provide data or direct evidence to calibrate predictions of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Dhaliwal, N. A., Skarlicki, D. P., Hoegg, J., & Daniels, M. A. (2022). Consequentialist Motives for Punishment Signal Trustworthiness. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that individuals who express or are perceived to have consequentialist motives for punishing norm violators are rated as more trustworthy, are entrusted with more money in Trust Game scenarios, and are chosen more often as cooperation partners compared to those with deontological motives. This effect is mediated by lower perceived psychopathy. The findings are robust across different phrasings of motives, experimental vignettes, and real-world recall. However, the studies do not examine actual group cooperation rates, efficiency, or payoff-based outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are relevant for understanding how the rationale for punishment affects the punisher's reputation and trustworthiness, which may indirectly influence cooperation, but it does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding psychological mechanisms or for modeling how punishment motives affect trust and partner selection.

- source: Abramson, N. R., & Senyshyn, Y. (2010). Effective Punishment Through Forgiveness: Rediscovering Kierkegaard's Knight of Faith in the Abraham Story. *Organization Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that punishment, when combined with forgiveness and a commitment to reconciliation (as in the Knight of Faith model), is more likely to restore trust and effective relationships than traditional retributive approaches. It draws on philosophical analysis, biblical narrative (the Abraham story), and real-world examples (South African Truth and Reconciliation Commission vs. Sarbanes-Oxley Act) to argue that restorative justice processes can lead to repentance and reconciliation, whereas retributive punishment alone often leads to retaliation, distrust, and ongoing conflict. However, these findings are conceptual and interpretive, not empirical or quantitative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative data relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games or similar environments. Its value for the prediction task is limited to conceptual insights: it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation and restoring group functioning may depend on the presence of forgiveness and opportunities for reconciliation, rather than punishment alone. However, it does not address or manipulate the game design dimensions relevant to the prediction task, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Molho, C., De Petrillo, F., Garfield, Z. H., & Slewe, S. (2024). Cross-societal variation in norm enforcement systems. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper reviews 28 empirical studies on cross-societal variation in punishment and norm enforcement, covering a range of methods (economic games, vignettes, ethnography, surveys). It finds that punishment and norm enforcement are present in many societies, but their prevalence, forms, and social acceptance vary widely. Socio-ecological factors (community size, pathogen prevalence, subsistence type) and cultural factors (individualism/collectivism, honor, kinship intensity, power distance, trust) are discussed as moderators of punishment behaviors. The review notes that evidence on these moderators is often mixed or context-dependent. While some included studies use public goods games with punishment, the review does not aggregate or report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral and attitudinal measures of punishment and norm enforcement.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-level theoretical and contextual background on how punishment and norm enforcement vary across societies and what factors may moderate these behaviors. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates of the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. For the downstream prediction task, this review may help identify potentially relevant moderators (e.g., community size, cultural values) but does not supply parameter-level evidence or efficiency outcomes needed for direct prediction. It is best used for contextual framing and hypothesis generation rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Salcedo, J. C., & Jimenez-Leal, W. (2024). Severity and deservedness determine signalled trustworthiness in third party punishment. *British Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across four experiments, the paper finds that (1) third-party punishment signals trustworthiness to observers, but only when the punishment is deserved and appropriately severe; (2) the trustworthiness signal increases with punishment severity, but only when higher severity entails higher cost to the punisher; (3) when punishment severity is increased without increasing cost, the trustworthiness signal does not increase; (4) refraining from punishing when punishment is undeserved can also signal trustworthiness, sometimes more than punishing. The main outcome is the amount entrusted to the punisher in a subsequent trust game, not group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public goods games or their close variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment is perceived and how it affects trust in subsequent interactions, but not for predicting changes in group efficiency when punishment is enabled. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs (using TPP and trust games), and the main outcomes are behavioral (trust, partner choice) rather than payoff-based. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments, but may be informative for understanding reputational and signaling mechanisms.

- source: Baumgartner, T., Hausfeld, J., dos Santos, M., & Knoch, D. (2021). Who initiates punishment, who joins punishment? Disentangling types of third-party punishers by neural traits. *Human Brain Mapping*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study demonstrates the existence of three distinct types of third-party punishers (independent, conditional, nonpunishers) in a laboratory setting. These types are characterized by different neural traits (gray matter volume in right TPJ, right DLPFC, and bilateral caudate) and by different self-reported motives. The paradigm allows for conditional punishment, where individuals can choose to join punishment only if someone else initiates it. The main findings are about the classification and neural/psychological correlates of these types, not about the impact of punishment on group efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is informative about individual heterogeneity in punishment behavior and the existence of conditional punishment, which may be relevant for modeling or interpreting punishment dynamics. However, it does not report efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game design dimensions in relation to efficiency. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to contextual or mechanistic insight about punishment types.

- source: Konishi, N., Oe, T., Shimizu, H., Tanaka, K., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2017). Perceived Shared Condemnation Intensifies Punitive Moral Emotions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that perceived shared condemnation increases moral outrage and, to a lesser extent, moral disgust, while reducing empathy for norm violators. These emotional shifts are associated with greater willingness to punish, both in hypothetical scenarios and in self-reported real-life interventions. The experimental manipulation in Study 2 shows that providing information about others' condemnation causally increases moral outrage and willingness to impose a fine. However, the studies do not use economic games, do not measure actual punishment behavior with real stakes, and do not report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar economic games. It does, however, suggest that social cues about shared condemnation can increase willingness to punish, which may be relevant for understanding psychological drivers of punishment in group settings. For the prediction task, this paper is only indirectly informative: it supports the idea that punishment is more likely when people believe others will also punish, but it does not address how this affects group efficiency, payoff, or cooperation rates in economic games.

- source: Turpie, J., & Letley, G. (2021). Would community conservation initiatives benefit from external financial oversight? A framed field experiment in Namibia's communal conservancies. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that individuals' willingness to impose social sanctions (disapproval, social exclusion) for norm violations is conditional on their beliefs about the frequency of such violations: the more common a violation is believed to be, the less likely people are to sanction it. This conditionality is strongest for weaker norms (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high regardless of beliefs. The study uses survey data from Austria and applies instrumental variable analysis to establish causality. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the determinants of willingness to sanction norm violations in real-world, non-experimental settings, showing that sanctioning is conditional on perceived prevalence for weaker norms. However, it does not study public goods games or variants directly, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Thus, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main relevance is conceptual: it supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment as a deterrent may depend on beliefs about norm prevalence, which could inform mechanism design or interpretation of experimental results, but it does not provide quantitative or design-specific guidance for efficiency prediction.

- source: van Dijk, E., De Dreu, C. K. W., & Gross, J. (2020). Power in economic games. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds strong evidence for conditional norm enforcement: individuals' stated willingness to sanction norm violations (e.g., tax evasion, fare dodging) is lower when they believe such violations are more common. This effect is most pronounced for weaker norms and among more educated respondents. For strong norms (e.g., drunk driving), sanctioning is high and largely unconditional. The study establishes a causal link between beliefs about norm prevalence and willingness to sanction using an instrumental variables approach. However, all outcomes are based on self-reported intentions in a survey, not on actual behavior or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. Its main contribution is to show that willingness to sanction norm violations is conditional on beliefs about others' compliance, especially for weaker norms. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing cooperation or efficiency may depend on participants' beliefs about the prevalence of norm violations, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or group payoff. The findings are most relevant for understanding psychological and social moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions.

- source: Rai, T. S. (2022). Material Benefits Crowd Out Moralistic Punishment. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that paying people to engage in third-party punishment of unfair or immoral behavior reduces their willingness to do so, compared to when no payment is offered. This effect is robust across both real-stakes economic games and hypothetical vignettes. The reduction in willingness is attributed to the corruption of the moral signal that punishment provides; payment makes the act appear less morally motivated. Willingness to punish can be restored either by offering a sufficiently large payment (which compensates for the lost moral signal) or by providing moral assurances that payment does not undermine the punisher's virtue. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and does not use a repeated public goods game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its main contribution is to show that introducing material incentives for punishment can reduce individuals' willingness to punish, unless the incentives are large or are reframed to preserve moral signaling. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff, this paper is only indirectly relevant: it suggests that the design of punishment incentives (especially the presence and framing of material rewards for punishers) could affect the frequency of punishment, which in turn could affect cooperation and efficiency in other settings. However, no direct evidence is provided about efficiency or payoff outcomes.

- source: Ferguson, E., Quigley, E., Powell, G., Stewart, L., Harrison, F., & Tallentire, H. (2019). To help or punish in the face of unfairness: men and women prefer mutually-beneficial strategies over punishment in a sexual selection context. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three studies using a one-shot 3PPC game, men primed with a romantic context are more likely to choose compensation or both compensation and punishment, rather than punishment alone or doing nothing. Women rate men who compensate (or both compensate and punish) as more attractive, compassionate, fair, and strong, regardless of whether they are considering short-term or long-term relationships. Trait empathy and left-wing political ideology are associated with a preference for compensation. The studies do not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct monetary outcomes for the group; all outcomes are behavioral preferences or social evaluations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-like games. The design is a one-shot third-party punishment and compensation game, and the outcomes are social preferences and evaluations, not efficiency or payoff. Therefore, it does not directly inform prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It may be relevant for understanding social or reputational motivations for punishment and compensation, but not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Qian, X. H., Huang, M., Zhang, Q. Y., Yin, M. Q., & Wang, X. W. (2018). Mechanism design of incentive-based reverse auctions with loss-averse 3PLs under incomplete attributes. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that combining incentive-based contracts (cost-sharing) with contingent penalties (punishment for delivery failure) in reverse auctions increases the ex ante expected profit (efficiency) of the system and the principal, compared to standard reverse auctions without these mechanisms. The efficiency gains are moderated by the number of agents (more agents increase efficiency), the fraction of low-risk agents, and the degree of loss aversion (higher loss aversion reduces efficiency). The presence and magnitude of the penalty (punishment) is a key design variable, and its optimal value depends on delivery risk parameters. The model provides explicit formulas for optimal efficiency under different mechanism designs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that introducing punishment (contingent penalty) in a competitive procurement game with cost uncertainty and risk can increase system efficiency, but the effect is moderated by agent risk aversion and the number of agents. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that, all else equal, adding a punishment mechanism to a reverse auction or similar environment will increase efficiency, especially when there are more agents and when agents are less loss-averse. However, the magnitude of the effect depends on the specific design of the punishment and incentive parameters. The findings are most directly applicable to procurement/auction settings but are informative for adjacent public-goods-like environments.

- source: Nuño, J. C., Herrero, M. A., & Primicerio, M. (2010). Fighting cheaters: How and how much to invest. *European Journal of Applied Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that in the absence of anti-cheating expenditure, cheaters dominate and total wealth collapses. Introducing and increasing expenditure to fight cheating (via social or punitive means) reduces the cheater population and increases total wealth, but only up to an optimal point. Beyond this, further expenditure reduces wealth due to the cost of enforcement. The optimal strategy involves a balance between social promotion and deterrence, with the best compromise (for the parameter values used) being to spend about 5% of wealth on anti-cheating measures, allocating about 90% of that to social promotion. The model also shows that the effectiveness of punishment (police efficiency) is a critical moderator: if punishment is highly effective, more resources should be allocated to it; if not, social measures are preferable. The findings are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by sensitivity analyses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment or anti-cheating measures in a public-goods-like environment increases efficiency (total wealth) up to an optimal point, after which further increases in punishment expenditure can reduce efficiency. The model highlights the importance of both the amount spent and the effectiveness of punishment. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment (or anti-cheating expenditure) will increase efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline, but the magnitude depends on the cost, effectiveness, and allocation of resources. The model also warns that excessive punishment spending can be counterproductive. While the model is not an experimental PGG, its structure and outcomes are closely analogous and informative for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Xu, J., Song, Y. B., & van der Schaar, M. (2014). Sharing in Networks of Strategic Agents. *Ieee Journal of Selected Topics in Signal Processing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that distributed rating-based sanctioning protocols can fully or nearly fully restore efficiency in repeated network sharing games with strategic agents, provided certain incentive compatibility conditions are met. When the network's connectivity is not too high and agents' discount factors are sufficient, the protocol achieves the social optimum (PoA=1). As network connectivity increases or agents become more impatient, efficiency declines but remains higher than with direct reciprocity mechanisms. The protocol is robust to imperfect monitoring and can be tuned for dynamic networks. Theoretical and simulation results show that the protocol outperforms Tit-for-Tat and similar direct punishment schemes, especially in heterogeneous or asymmetric networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that distributed, rating-based punishment mechanisms can substantially increase efficiency in repeated networked cooperation environments, often achieving the social optimum. The effect is most pronounced when the network is not too highly connected and agents are sufficiently patient. The protocol's performance is robust to imperfect monitoring and network dynamics. For prediction tasks, the presence of a distributed, indirect punishment mechanism (ratings affecting future access) is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially compared to direct, bilateral punishment. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with costly sharing, which are adjacent to but not identical to standard public goods games.

- source: O'Connor, C. (2016). The Evolution of Guilt: A Model-Based Approach. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that guilt, modeled as a behavioral trait leading to cooperation, apology, and reparative actions, can be individually adaptive in evolutionary games. In both stag hunt and prisoner's dilemma settings, guilt-proneness increases the likelihood of cooperation, which in turn raises individual and group payoffs. When punishment for defection is present, guilt-proneness further increases individual fitness by reducing the risk of being punished. The models show that costly or honest signals of apology (as a proxy for guilt) can stabilize cooperation and prevent the invasion of defectors or 'fakers.' The efficiency of the group (measured as average fitness or payoff) is higher in populations where guilt-proneness and apology are present, especially when punishment mechanisms exist.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms, when combined with behavioral traits like guilt-proneness and apology, can increase efficiency (group payoff) in social dilemma games. While it does not directly model public goods games, the findings are adjacent and suggest that enabling punishment and supporting honest signals of intent (like apology) are likely to increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment. The models also highlight the importance of the cost and credibility of punishment and apology mechanisms. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that introducing punishment (especially with some cost or honest signaling) will increase efficiency, provided that the game environment allows for reciprocity and repeated interaction.

- source: Handfield, T., Huang, P. H., & Simpson, R. M. (2016). Climate change, cooperation and moral bioenhancement. *Journal of Medical Ethics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that enhancing retributive (punishment) tendencies in social dilemma games (including public goods and prisoner's dilemma paradigms) is likely to lower group efficiency, even if it increases cooperation rates. The authors review theoretical models and cite experimental evidence indicating that harsh punishment can make groups poorer, and that those who punish often gain less than those who do not. The effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost/benefit ratio but relatively insensitive to punishment severity. The paper also notes that anti-social punishment (punishing cooperators) can be highly destructive to cooperation and efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and literature-based evidence that enabling or increasing punishment in public-goods-game-like environments is likely to reduce group efficiency, especially when punishment is harsh or misapplied. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, all else equal, adding or intensifying punishment mechanisms may not increase and may even decrease efficiency, despite possible increases in cooperation rates. The effect is particularly negative when punishment is severe or when anti-social punishment is possible. The paper is less informative about the precise quantitative effect sizes but is clear about the direction of the effect on efficiency.

- source: Fabbri, M., Barbieri, P. N., & Bigoni, M. (2019). Ride Your Luck! A Field Experiment on Lottery-Based Incentives for Compliance. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: A field experiment on city buses in Italy tested a lottery-based reward for buying tickets, layered on top of existing probabilistic sanctions for fare-dodging. Treated buses saw a significant increase in on-board ticket sales (18–30% increase per hour), with no overall reduction in off-board ticket sales, indicating a net increase in compliance and revenue. The increased revenue covered the cost of the prizes actually claimed, but not the total value of all prizes offered, so cost-effectiveness depended on unclaimed prizes. The effect was not persistent after the lottery ended. Suburban routes showed a substitution from off-board to on-board ticket purchases. The study provides evidence that combining probabilistic sanctions with lottery-based rewards can increase compliance and revenue in a real-world public good context, but the net efficiency gain is limited by the cost of rewards and the sustainability of the effect.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides field evidence that adding a lottery-based reward to an environment with probabilistic sanctions increases compliance and revenue (a proxy for efficiency), but the net efficiency gain depends on the cost of the reward and whether all prizes are claimed. The effect is not persistent after removal. For prediction, the presence of both probabilistic sanctions and a reward mechanism (lottery) increases efficiency relative to sanctions alone, but the magnitude of the gain may be modest and sensitive to the cost structure and salience of the reward. The design is not a standard PGG, but the compliance/public good context and outcome measures are closely related. The findings are most informative for settings where compliance is individually observable and can be incentivized with both sanctions and probabilistic rewards.

- source: Verma, P., Nandi, A. K., & Sengupta, S. (2017). Bribery games on inter-dependent regular networks. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in structured populations, the spread of honest officers (and thus reduction in bribery) is favored over a larger region of parameter space compared to mixed populations. However, the effect of network connectivity is non-monotonic: increasing the degree of the citizen network initially helps honest strategies, but beyond an optimal point, further increases hinder the spread of honesty. Similarly, the degree of the interaction network (citizen-officer links) has an optimal value for maximizing the prevalence of honest officers. Asymmetry in population sizes (more citizens per officer) makes it harder to eradicate bribery. The cost of complaining and the bribe amount are key parameters: lower cost of complaining and lower bribe amounts favor honest strategies. The main dependent variables are the fixation probability of honest officers and the phase space area where honest strategies dominate, not direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for the prediction task. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it analyzes how network structure, punishment asymmetry, and behavioral parameters affect the prevalence of honest (non-corrupt) strategies. In public-goods-like environments with asymmetric punishment, structured populations can reduce corruption, but only up to an optimal level of network connectivity.
