The findings suggest that, for similar games, enabling punishment (asymmetric, targeting only one side) in a structured population can increase the prevalence of socially beneficial behavior, but the effect depends on network connectivity and behavioral costs. For prediction, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve outcomes (in terms of honest behavior) in structured settings, but warns that too much connectivity can undermine this effect. However, because efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured, the findings should be used as indirect evidence.

- source: Molina, M., Nee, V., & Holm, H. (2023). Cooperation with Strangers: Spillover of Community Norms. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that among Chinese CEOs, stronger belief that norm violations are met with retaliatory sanctions in their business community is associated with a higher probability of cooperating with a stranger in a one-shot PD. This association is especially strong in some cities (Nanjing, Wenzhou). An online experiment with managers and nonmanagers shows that priming reciprocity norms increases cooperation with strangers in some subgroups and cities. However, the outcome is always the individual choice to cooperate, not group payoff or efficiency. There is no direct evidence on how punishment or norm enforcement affects efficiency or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its main contribution is showing that belief in norm enforcement (especially retaliatory sanctions) is associated with higher cooperation rates in a one-shot PD. While this suggests that punishment norms can promote cooperation, the lack of payoff or efficiency outcomes, and the use of a PD rather than a PGG, limits its direct applicability to the efficiency prediction task. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of punishment norms in promoting cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Julle-Danière, E., Whitehouse, J., Vrij, A., Gustafsson, E., & Waller, B. M. (2020). The social function of the feeling and expression of guilt. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that individuals who feel more guilt after being blamed for a cooperative failure allocate more of a shared reward to their partner, regardless of friendship strength. When the victim of a transgression (Player 2) perceives their partner as guilty, they are more likely to 'punish' (i.e., take more for themselves) if the partner is a close friend, though this effect is sensitive to outliers. The expression of guilt thus has a complex social function, sometimes leading to harsher punishment from close friends. The main behavioral outcome is the reward split in a dictator game, not efficiency or group payoff. The study does not directly address the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is adjacent to PGGs, using a one-shot dictator game after a cooperative task with induced guilt, but does not involve repeated contribution decisions, group efficiency, or explicit punishment mechanisms as in standard PGGs. The main outcome is the allocation of a fixed reward, interpreted as relationship repair or punishment, but not as efficiency. The findings suggest that social context (friendship, perceived guilt) can modulate punitive or reparative behavior, but do not provide quantitative evidence on how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games. Thus, the paper is more informative about the social and emotional moderators of punishment-like behavior than about efficiency outcomes relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2022). Advanced defensive cooperators promote cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing advanced defensive cooperators, who can identify and punish anti-defensive defectors at a cost, leads to higher equilibrium fractions of cooperators compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The presence of advanced defensive cooperators is especially important when the temptation to defect is high. The model shows that the effectiveness of advanced defensive cooperation depends on parameters such as the temptation value, defensive benefit, defensive loss, fine (punishment), and screening cost. While the model tracks payoffs in the replicator equations, the main reported results are the fractions of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment-like mechanisms (here, advanced defensive cooperation with screening and fines) can increase the prevalence of cooperation in social dilemma settings. However, because the main outcomes are strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the results are only adjacent to the prediction task of forecasting efficiency. The model is a prisoner's dilemma variant, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as a ratio of actual to maximum possible group payoff. Thus, while the findings suggest that enabling punishment can increase cooperation, they do not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency changes in public-goods-game-like environments.

- source: Chambers, C. R. (2024). Nonmonetary Reward Systems, Counterproductive Behavior, and Responses to Sanctions in Open Collaboration Environments. *Organization Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that proximity to nonmonetary reward thresholds is associated with a higher likelihood of counterproductive behavior (suspension), suggesting that reward systems can create performance pressures leading to negative behaviors. However, individuals who are suspended tend to increase their productive contributions just before suspension, and their contribution rates drop after suspension. The paper does not measure efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that formal sanctions may have unintended negative consequences for sustaining contributions in open collaboration environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers indirect evidence that formal sanctions (suspensions) may reduce subsequent contribution rates, which could imply a negative effect on group output or efficiency if mapped to a PGG context. The findings suggest that while sanctions may deter counterproductive behavior, they may also discourage future productive contributions, highlighting a potential tradeoff. For prediction tasks, this paper signals caution in assuming that punishment will always improve group outcomes, especially in voluntary, open collaboration settings.

- source: Rosas, A. (2008). Multilevel selection and human altruism. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the evolution of human altruism depends on the degree to which individuals (or their social/psychological mechanisms) can control assortative grouping, such as by punishing defectors and maintaining group cohesion. It claims that in human societies, mechanisms like punishment, reputation, and social norms can, in principle, prevent subversion from within and make altruism individually adaptive. The discussion references public goods games and the importance of punishment for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence on efficiency or payoff effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical context for how punishment and social mechanisms might enable stable cooperation in public goods settings by engineering assortative grouping and deterring free-riders. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or variants. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of mechanisms (like punishment and reputation) that allow groups to control subversion from within, suggesting that such mechanisms could, in principle, improve group outcomes. For direct prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in experimental public goods games, this paper is not directly informative.

- source: Romano, A., Gross, J., & De Dreu, C. K. W. (2024). The nasty neighbor effect in humans. *Science Advances*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper demonstrates that, contrary to standard expectations from in-group favoritism, people are more likely to waste resources in costly competition (attack and defense) with in-group members than with out-group members or strangers. This 'nasty neighbor effect' is observed in attacker-defender contest games and in a nested social dilemma with an attack option. The effect is robust across 51 countries, a field experiment in Kenya, and representative UK samples. The increased conflict investment with in-group members directly reduces efficiency and group earnings. The effect is not explained by a lack of in-group favoritism in cooperation, as trust and trustworthiness are still higher toward in-group members. Instead, the two phenomena coexist and are uncorrelated at the individual and country level. The nasty neighbor effect is stronger when individuals perceive greater resource scarcity or lower status within their group. In a nested public goods game with an attack option, participants assigned more costly deductions to lower-status in-group members, further reducing group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in contest or public-goods-like environments with an option for costly attack or punishment, the availability and use of such options can reduce group efficiency, especially when directed at in-group members. The findings suggest that enabling costly punishment or attack (even when it is not norm-enforcing but status-seeking or competitive) can decrease total group earnings and joint welfare, particularly under conditions of perceived resource scarcity or status competition. For prediction tasks, this paper indicates that the presence of a punishment/attack option, especially with high punishment effectiveness (e.g., 1:3 cost-impact ratio), is likely to reduce efficiency relative to a control without such options, even in the absence of explicit norm violations. The effect is robust across cultures and group compositions. However, the contest/attack framing is not a standard PGG, so direct quantitative transfer to classic PGGs with norm-enforcing punishment should be cautious.

- source: Pérez-Escudero, A., Friedman, J., & Gore, J. (2016). Preferential interactions promote blind cooperation and informed defection. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games where one player can gather information before cooperating or defecting, aversion to information gathering (i.e., a preference for 'blind' cooperation) can be sustained either by punishment (ending the game if the player looks) or by preferential interactions (continuing only with reliable partners). In homogeneous populations, punishment is required to enforce blind cooperation and thus higher payoffs. In heterogeneous populations, preferential interactions can achieve similar or greater efficiency without punishment. The model also predicts that if detecting information gathering is costly, punishment-based mechanisms break down in homogeneous populations, but preferential interactions remain effective in heterogeneous ones. The effect of punishment on efficiency is therefore positive in homogeneous populations but redundant or unnecessary in heterogeneous ones.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated cooperation games depends on population heterogeneity. In homogeneous populations, punishment (ending the game after information gathering or defection) is necessary to enforce cooperation and increase efficiency. In heterogeneous populations, preferential partner selection can achieve high efficiency without punishment. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should consider the degree of population heterogeneity and the possibility of preferential interactions. If the environment is homogeneous and punishment is strong, enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency. If the environment is heterogeneous and allows for preferential interactions, punishment may have little or no additional effect on efficiency.

- source: Agranov, M., Fréchette, G., Palfrey, T., & Vespa, E. (2016). Static and dynamic underinvestment: An experimental investigation. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment finds that underprovision of the public good is substantial in both static and dynamic settings, with dynamic inefficiency (the additional inefficiency due to intertemporal linkage) increasing as the survival rate of the public good rises. When the public good is highly durable, dynamic inefficiency accounts for a large share of total inefficiency. Subjects use both minimum winning coalition (MWC) strategies and strategic cooperation, including informal punishment (exclusion from coalitions) to reward or punish past behavior. However, the game does not include a formal punishment technology (e.g., costly punishment as in standard PGGs). Welfare losses are decomposed into static and dynamic components, and the results show that dynamic inefficiency is empirically significant and increases with the strength of the dynamic link. The findings are robust across different strategy types and are consistent with theoretical predictions, though the magnitude of inefficiency is somewhat less than predicted by theory.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how dynamic linkages and coalition bargaining affect efficiency in public good provision, especially in legislative or committee settings. However, it does not manipulate or include a formal punishment mechanism as in standard PGGs. Instead, it studies endogenous, informal punishment via coalition exclusion. Therefore, while the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in dynamic, coalition-based public goods environments, they are only indirectly relevant for predicting the effect of adding formal punishment to a standard PGG. The main lesson is that efficiency is sensitive to dynamic linkages and coalition rules, and that informal punishment can partially substitute for formal mechanisms, but the quantitative effect of formal punishment cannot be inferred from this study.

- source: Ahn, T. K., Balafoutas, L., Batsaikhan, M., Campos-Ortiz, F., Putterman, L., & Sutter, M. (2016). Securing property rights: A dilemma experiment in Austria, Mexico, Mongolia, South Korea and the United States. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: This paper studies a property rights dilemma game in which subjects allocate effort among production, theft, and protection. Three institutional treatments are compared: (1) no collective protection (NCP, baseline), (2) voluntary collective protection (VCP), and (3) collective protection by majority vote (VOTE, mandatory if chosen). Efficiency, measured as group earnings relative to the social optimum, is lowest in NCP (24.6%), higher in VCP (36.6%), and highest in VOTE (48.0%). The VOTE treatment achieves the highest efficiency when the mandatory scheme is adopted, but this occurs in only 64% of opportunities. The efficiency gains from collective protection are robust across five countries, but the magnitude varies with national trust levels. The results show that institutional mechanisms for collective protection (analogous to public enforcement or taxation) can substantially increase efficiency in social dilemmas involving appropriation, but their effectiveness depends on both institutional design and cultural context.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving the effect of introducing a collective protection or sanctioning institution in a social dilemma with appropriation (theft), this paper provides direct evidence that such institutions increase efficiency, with mandatory (voted) collective protection yielding the largest gains. The efficiency increase from baseline to voluntary collective protection is modest but significant, while the increase to mandatory collective protection is larger but not maximal due to incomplete adoption and suboptimal implementation. The design dimensions most relevant are the presence and type of collective protection, the method of funding (voluntary vs. mandatory), and the cultural context (trust). While the game is not a standard PGG, the efficiency results are closely analogous and informative for settings where punishment or public enforcement is introduced. The findings suggest that enabling effective, enforceable collective protection (akin to punishment) is likely to increase efficiency, but the realized gains may be less than theoretical predictions due to behavioral and cultural factors.

- source: Murase, Y., & Hilbe, C. (2024). Computational evolution of social norms in well-mixed and group-structured populations. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in well-mixed populations, even with complex (third-order) social norms, cooperation and high group payoff (efficiency) are difficult to sustain: defecting strategies (ALLD) are most abundant, and average payoffs are low. In group-structured populations, however, cooperation and high efficiency can evolve and persist, especially when third-order norms are available. The most successful norm (L1) treats cooperation as always good and defection as bad, except when defection is 'justified' (against bad recipients). This norm is robust to disagreements and errors in private assessment. The results highlight the importance of population structure and norm complexity for the evolution of efficient, cooperative outcomes. The model does not implement explicit costly punishment, but some norms functionally resemble punishment by allowing justified defection without reputational cost.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the population (well-mixed vs. group-structured) and the complexity of available social norms (second-order vs. third-order) are critical moderators of the effect of reputational 'punishment' (justified defection) on group efficiency. In well-mixed populations, enabling more complex norms or reputational punishment does not reliably increase efficiency; in group-structured populations, it can lead to high efficiency. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the presence of group structure and the richness of the norm/strategy space are key predictors of whether enabling reputational punishment will improve efficiency. However, the results may not transfer directly to standard PGGs with explicit costly punishment, as the model is dyadic and punishment is reputational, not material.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2024). The universal probabilistic reward based on the difference of payoff realizes the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a universal probabilistic reward mechanism, where players with higher payoffs reward their neighbors (cooperators or defectors) with a probability proportional to the payoff difference, leads to a significant increase in both cooperation and average group payoff (efficiency) in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is robust across different network topologies (regular, random, scale-free) and a range of reward coefficients (t), except when t=0 or t=1, where the mechanism is inactive. The study provides detailed analysis of how the speed and stability of cooperation and efficiency depend on the reward parameter and network structure. No punishment mechanism is implemented in the model, but the paper situates its contribution in the context of prior work on both reward and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that a universal, payoff-difference-based reward mechanism can substantially increase group efficiency in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when rewards are given to both cooperators and defectors. While the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, it is highly informative for predicting the effects of reward-based interventions on efficiency, especially in networked or spatially structured games. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, well-designed reward mechanisms can achieve high efficiency. However, the results may not directly transfer to public goods games with explicit punishment options, as the model does not include a punishment treatment or control. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that reward mechanisms (especially those not limited to cooperators) can be highly effective at increasing efficiency, but does not provide direct evidence about the marginal effect of adding punishment to a baseline.

- source: Gao, L., Li, Y. T., & Wang, R. W. (2015). The shift between the Red Queen and the Red King effects in mutualisms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in multiplayer snowdrift games modeling mutualisms, the introduction and increase of a collective reward mechanism can shift the allocation of mutualistic benefits from favoring the faster-evolving species (Red Queen effect) to favoring the slower-evolving species (Red King effect). Asymmetries in the number of interacting partners or in payoffs can also shift the balance of surplus allocation. The model predicts that, depending on the reward intensity and asymmetries, the efficiency (total surplus generated and its division) can be increased or made more unequal. The model is theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that the presence and intensity of a reward mechanism (analogous to a positive incentive or bonus for cooperation) can substantially alter the efficiency and allocation of surplus in multiplayer social dilemmas. While it does not directly model punishment or public goods games, its results suggest that reward mechanisms can increase group efficiency and shift the distribution of payoffs, especially in the presence of asymmetries. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that enabling a reward mechanism (with sufficient intensity) can increase efficiency and alter the distribution of payoffs, but the effect depends on the details of the reward and the structure of the game (e.g., group size, asymmetries). The findings are most relevant for settings with reward rather than punishment, and for snowdrift-like rather than public-goods-like games.

- source: Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2023). Grouping promotes both partnership and rivalry with long memory in direct reciprocity. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The main finding is that group structure, when combined with the availability of longer-memory strategies (which allow for friendly rival strategies), leads to the evolution of nearly full cooperation and maximal efficiency in the repeated donation game. In well-mixed populations, longer memory does not have this effect. The results are robust to the benefit-to-cost ratio and to details of the evolutionary process. The paper does not study public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, but the repeated donation game is adjacent to PGGs, and friendly rival strategies function as a form of endogenous sanctioning (never allowing the co-player to earn more). Efficiency is measured as the fraction of time mutual cooperation is achieved, which is equivalent to group payoff relative to the maximum.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in two-player repeated donation games (adjacent to PGGs), the combination of group structure and the availability of longer-memory strategies (enabling friendly rival strategies) can dramatically increase efficiency, even when cooperation would otherwise be difficult. While there is no explicit punishment mechanism, friendly rival strategies act as endogenous enforcers of cooperation by never allowing the co-player to earn more. For prediction tasks, this suggests that in environments with group structure and sufficient strategic complexity, efficiency can be much higher than in well-mixed or memory-limited settings, even without explicit punishment. However, the results may not transfer directly to multi-player PGGs or to settings with explicit costly punishment.

- source: Iwamura, Y., Nagashima, K., & Tanimoto, J. (2020). Evolutionary dynamics of a 3-strategy game: Cooperator, defector and costly cooperative loner strategic types. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a costly cooperative loner (CL) as a third strategy in a prisoner's dilemma game does not prevent the collapse to all-defection, nor does it improve social average payoff (efficiency) compared to the standard two-strategy game. The CL strategy is always outcompeted and goes extinct, after which defectors dominate. This is in contrast to models with costly punishment, where punishment can sometimes sustain cooperation and higher efficiency. The paper provides a detailed analysis of the payoff matrix and evolutionary trajectories, showing that the addition of CL does not alter the efficiency outcome: the system always ends in the least efficient (all-defector) state.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a negative case: simply adding a third strategy that is not an active punisher (but pays a cost to avoid defectors) does not improve efficiency in a prisoner's dilemma environment. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of a 'costly loner' or similar non-punishing third strategy is not sufficient to increase efficiency. The results reinforce that only certain types of third strategies (e.g., active costly punishment or rewarding) can shift efficiency outcomes. The findings are most relevant for infinite, well-mixed populations and may not generalize to finite or structured populations. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment per se, but its comparison to punishment models supports the view that punishment (with sufficient impact and cost structure) is necessary for efficiency gains in these settings.

- source: Johnstone, R. A., & Bshary, R. (2007). Indirect reciprocity in asymmetric interactions: when apparent altruism facilitates profitable exploitation. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in asymmetric games where only one party (the actor) can help or exploit, and the other (the recipient) can only choose whether to interact, a form of indirect reciprocity can evolve. Actors help when the gains from exploitation are low to maintain a positive image, which encourages recipients to approach them in the future, allowing profitable exploitation when the gains are high. This strategy is stable and increases the long-term average payoff for both actors and recipients, provided certain conditions on observability, state-switching, and payoff parameters are met. The model shows that efficiency (average group payoff) is higher under these conditions than if actors always exploit or if recipients always avoid interaction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in asymmetric games with image scoring and conditional approach, mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, avoidance of exploiters) can increase efficiency by supporting conditional cooperation. The findings suggest that when harmful behavior is observable and actors' states vary, efficiency is maximized for intermediate values of key parameters (cost of harm, switching probability, observability). While not a PGG or direct punishment setting, the model is adjacent and supports the idea that social mechanisms that deter exploitation (even without direct punishment) can improve group efficiency. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling mechanisms for reputation-based avoidance or indirect sanctions can increase efficiency, especially when exploitation is not always profitable and can be detected.

- source: Sabin, N., & Reed-Tsochas, F. (2020). Able but Unwilling to Enforce: Cooperative Dilemmas in Group Lending. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that group repayment (a direct measure of economic cooperation/efficiency) is highest in groups with moderate social cohesion and no disconnected subgroups. Both very high and very low cohesion are associated with worse repayment. Disconnected subgroups further reduce repayment, and the negative effect is mitigated by higher overall cohesion. The mechanisms are traced to a trade-off between ability and willingness to punish: high cohesion increases ability but reduces willingness to enforce, while disconnected subgroups increase willingness to punish out-group members but reduce ability to do so effectively. The net effect on efficiency is determined by the lesser of ability or willingness. These results are robust to controls for financial ability, group composition, and alternative outcome measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of informal punishment mechanisms on efficiency in public-goods-like settings is highly sensitive to group social structure. In environments where punishment is informal and depends on social ties, both the ability and willingness to punish must be considered. Efficiency is maximized at moderate levels of cohesion; too much cohesion reduces willingness to punish, undermining enforcement. Disconnected subgroups are detrimental to efficiency, even if willingness to punish out-group members is high, because ability is low. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not monotonic in group connectedness and is context-dependent. The findings are most relevant for settings with informal, endogenous punishment and may not generalize to formal, externally imposed punishment mechanisms.

- source: Lee, S., Murase, Y., & Baek, S. K. (2021). Local stability of cooperation in a continuous model of indirect reciprocity. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the stability of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity depends on the sensitivity of the assessment and behavioral rules (social norms). It derives explicit conditions (in terms of derivatives of the assessment and behavioral functions) under which a cooperative norm is stable against local mutants, and shows that the benefit-to-cost ratio required for stability increases if reputation is insensitive to observed behavior or if cooperation is insensitive to recipient reputation. The analysis is supported by numerical simulations, which confirm the theoretical predictions about payoff differences between resident and mutant norms. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms as in standard PGGs, but reputation loss functions as an indirect sanction. The main outcome is the expected payoff (efficiency) of the group under different norms and parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation evidence about how the structure of social norms (in terms of sensitivity to behavior and reputation) affects the efficiency (payoff) of cooperation in a continuous donation game with indirect reciprocity. While not a direct PGG or explicit punishment game, it is relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where cooperation is enforced by reputation-based indirect sanctions rather than direct punishment. The findings suggest that the efficiency of cooperation depends on the responsiveness of the reputation system and the behavioral rule, and that certain parameter regimes (high benefit-to-cost ratio, high sensitivity) are required for stable, efficient cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where indirect reciprocity is the main enforcement mechanism, and less so to direct punishment or reward treatments in PGGs.

- source: Ille, S. (2021). The evolution of sectarianism. *Communications in Nonlinear Science and Numerical Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the evolution of coercive (punishment-like) strategies in a repeated bargaining game can lead to either efficient egalitarian outcomes or inefficient sectarian contracts, depending on the relative costs and benefits of coercion and the structure of social learning. When the cost of coercion is low and potential gains from inegalitarian contracts are high, societies are more likely to settle into inefficient, conflictual, or sectarian equilibria. When both groups have similar coercive capacities and the cost of coercion is high, egalitarian and efficient outcomes are more likely. The model also shows that collective memory, identity politics, and initial asymmetries can tip the balance toward persistent conflict or sectarianism, reducing overall efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and simulation-based evidence that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (coercion) on efficiency is highly context-dependent. If punishment is symmetric and costly, it can support efficient cooperation (egalitarian contracts). If punishment is asymmetric or the gains from inegalitarian outcomes are high, punishment can entrench inefficient, conflictual, or exploitative equilibria. The model suggests that game design dimensions such as the cost and effectiveness of punishment, the symmetry of player power, and the structure of social learning critically moderate the efficiency impact of punishment. However, the model is not a direct PGG or public goods game, but rather a repeated bargaining/coordination game with endogenous punishment-like coercion. Thus, its findings are most relevant for adjacent settings where punishment can be used to enforce either efficient or inefficient group outcomes.

- source: Kas, J., Corten, R., & van de Rijt, A. (2020). Reputations in mixed-role markets: A theory and an experimental test. *Social Science Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated dyadic trust/lending game, providing information about a participant's past behavior in both roles (mixed-role reputation) increases the rate at which trust is placed and honored in the Lending Game (free exchange, where lending is costly and not compensated), but not in the Trust Game (paid exchange, where lending is compensated). Specifically, in the Lending Game, the trust rate and the 'success rate' (trust placed and honored) are significantly higher under mixed-role reputation than under single-role reputation. In the Trust Game, there is no significant difference between reputation conditions. The paper interprets these results as evidence that mixed-role reputation systems facilitate reciprocal lending and increase overall cooperation and welfare in free exchange settings.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks involving public-goods-like environments where the main intervention is a change in the information structure (e.g., adding mixed-role reputation), this paper provides evidence that such interventions can increase efficiency-related outcomes (success rate) in free exchange settings, but not in paid exchange settings. The results are most relevant for environments where cooperation is costly and not directly compensated, and where reputation can be built across multiple roles. The findings suggest that enabling richer reputation signals (beyond single-role) can improve group outcomes in these contexts. However, the paper does not directly study classic public goods games or explicit punishment mechanisms, so the transfer to PGGs with punishment should be made with caution.

- source: Smith, K. B. (2006). Representational altruism: The wary cooperator as authoritative decision maker. *American Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments show that individuals acting as allocators in one-shot, anonymous dictator/ultimatum-like games often divide resources evenly or even disadvantage themselves to appear fair, supporting the 'wary cooperator' hypothesis. The presence or absence of a veto (ultimatum vs. dictator) does not significantly affect allocations; rather, information asymmetry (who values the resource more) and the framing of the allocator's role (merit vs. trust) are key. When allocators are told they are entrusted with responsibility, they are more likely to allocate less to themselves than when told they earned the role by merit. The findings suggest that internalized social preferences, not external punishment or veto threats, drive representational altruism and fairness in allocation decisions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting efficiency in public-goods-like environments with and without punishment. While it does not use a standard PGG or explicit punishment mechanism, it shows that internalized social preferences can lead to fair or altruistic outcomes even in the absence of external sanctions. The findings suggest that, in some contexts, adding external punishment or veto power may have limited additional effect on efficiency if internalized fairness norms are strong. However, context (information asymmetry, framing of authority) can shift behavior toward more or less self-interested allocations. For prediction, this implies that game design features affecting perceived fairness and social expectations may be as important as formal punishment mechanisms.

- source: Phillips, T., Li, J. W., & Kendall, G. (2014). The Effects of Extra-Somatic Weapons on the Evolution of Human Cooperation towards Non-Kin. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Simulating a population of 40 players using 25 well-known IPD strategies, the authors compare two conditions: a standard evolutionary IPD and a 'weapons use' IPD where repeated defections trigger a risk of elimination (interpreted as a proxy for lethal punishment or high-cost disputes). Cooperative strategies, on average, survive significantly longer under weapons use, while non-cooperative strategies survive for fewer generations. The 'Always Cooperate' strategy, typically the least successful, becomes the most successful cooperative strategy as the risk of elimination increases. The model suggests that high costs for repeated defection (analogous to punishment) can shift evolutionary success toward cooperation, even in the absence of explicit monitoring or repeated interactions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence that increasing the cost of within-group conflict (modeled as a risk of elimination after repeated defections) can increase the evolutionary success of cooperative strategies and decrease that of non-cooperative strategies. While the model is not a public goods game and does not report group efficiency per se, the findings are relevant for predicting that introducing or increasing the severity of punishment in repeated social dilemmas is likely to increase the relative payoff or evolutionary success of cooperation. The results are most applicable to settings where punishment is severe and unavoidable, and less so to standard PGGs with explicit, voluntary punishment stages. The paper does not provide direct evidence on the effect of specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, MPCR, chat), but supports the general principle that costly sanctions can promote cooperation and group-level success.

- source: Milinski, M. (2022). Extortion - A voracious prosocial strategy. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that extortionate strategies in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma games can enforce high cooperation from coplayers, but at the cost of an unequal payoff distribution favoring the extortioner. When coplayers attempt to punish extortioners by withholding cooperation, both parties' payoffs decrease, demonstrating a form of costly punishment. In symmetric settings, this can discipline extortioners, but in asymmetric settings (e.g., with replacement power or extra incentives), extortioners can persist and maintain higher payoffs. The review highlights that the efficiency of the group (total payoff) may not increase with extortion, as the gains are captured by the extortioner and punishment reduces total surplus. The findings are based on a synthesis of theory and several experimental studies, but the review itself does not report new experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, costly punishment via withholding cooperation) on efficiency depends on the symmetry of the game and the power structure. In symmetric games, punishment can reduce the extortioner's advantage but at a cost to both players, lowering efficiency. In asymmetric games, extortioners can avoid being disciplined and extract higher payoffs, further reducing the coplayer's share. The review does not directly address public goods games or classic punishment institutions, but the mechanisms discussed (costly punishment, power asymmetry, incentive structures) are relevant for predicting when punishment will improve or reduce efficiency in related environments. The evidence is more about distributional outcomes and the limits of punishment than about average efficiency increases from punishment.

- source: Camera, G., Casari, M., & Bigoni, M. (2013). EXPERIMENTAL MARKETS WITH FRICTIONS. *Journal of Economic Surveys*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The new experiment shows that allowing free-form chat among randomly matched strangers in an indefinitely repeated prisoner's dilemma leads to much higher cooperation rates than either no communication or structured, anonymous public messages. Structured communication does not significantly improve cooperation or coordination relative to no communication, likely due to the prevalence of deceptive use of messages. The study finds that public messages are informative about intentions, but deception dilutes their effectiveness. The experiment does not include a punishment treatment, but the literature review summarizes prior findings that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, especially in small groups. The main outcomes reported are behavioral (cooperation rates, communication patterns), not payoff-based (efficiency or group earnings).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. The experimental design is an indefinitely repeated, random-matching prisoner's dilemma among strangers, which is adjacent to public goods games. The main manipulated dimension is communication: free-form chat vs. structured messages vs. no communication. The findings suggest that free-form communication can strongly increase cooperation, but structured, anonymous communication does not. The experiment does not manipulate or report on punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The literature review section summarizes prior work showing that peer punishment can increase cooperation and sometimes efficiency, but these are not tested in the new experiment. Thus, this paper is most informative about the effect of communication (chat) and monitoring (public/anonymous) on cooperation, and only indirectly about punishment or efficiency. It should be used as contextual or indirect evidence for prediction, especially regarding the role of communication and monitoring, but not as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Vandermeer, J., Hosey, C., Epley, N., & Keysar, B. (2019). Escalation of negative social exchange: Reflexive punishment or deliberative deterrence?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that negative escalation in repeated taking/giving games is driven by reflexive punishment rather than strategic deterrence. Deliberation reduces escalation, while reflexive or high cognitive load conditions increase it. Escalation occurs only in response to perceived negative actions (taking), not positive ones (giving). Explicitly instructing participants to focus on future consequences or to deter does not increase escalation; only a goal to punish does. The findings are robust across one-shot and repeated interactions, and across different manipulations of deliberation and cognitive resources. However, the study does not use public goods games, does not implement a punishment stage, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The experimental paradigm is a repeated dictator/taking game, not a public goods game, and the main outcomes are escalation and behavioral responses to harm, not group efficiency or payoff. There is no punishment stage where players can pay to reduce others' payoffs, and no analysis of group welfare or efficiency. The findings may be relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punitive escalation, but do not inform the quantitative effect of punishment mechanisms on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Wang, W. J., Xu, Z. H., Hua, S. J., Cui, L. Q., Zhang, J. L., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Threshold-initiated spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that intergroup competition significantly increases the rate of volunteering in the volunteer's dilemma, even when there is no additional monetary incentive or change in the payoff structure. Risk (a 50% chance of public good failure) reduces volunteering rates, but intergroup competition mitigates this negative effect. Gender and risk aversion moderate these effects: females volunteer more in no-risk settings, males increase volunteering more under risky intergroup competition. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but notes in the discussion that over-volunteering (stimulated by intergroup competition) is socially inefficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about volunteering rates in a volunteer's dilemma, which is structurally adjacent to but not the same as a standard public goods game. The results suggest that intergroup competition increases pro-social action rates, especially under risk, but also that this can lead to inefficiency through over-volunteering. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the main takeaway is that mechanisms increasing pro-social action (like competition) may not always improve efficiency, especially in anti-coordination settings like the volunteer's dilemma. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the paper's predictive value for the target task.

- source: Tanaka, H., Ohtsuki, H., & Ohtsubo, Y. (2016). The price of being seen to be just: an intention signalling strategy for indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that participants in a dyadic donation game use a costly signalling option (abandoning a resource) to manage their reputation, especially after justified defection or implementation error. Others interpret this signal as a sign of cooperative intent and are more likely to cooperate with signallers. The signalling option allows players to maintain a good reputation without requiring second-order information. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that such signalling can stabilize cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only indirectly relevant to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The experimental design is a dyadic donation game, not a multi-player PGG, and the 'punishment' is a costly self-imposed signal (abandoning a resource) rather than a punishment directed at others. The main outcomes are behavioral (signalling and cooperation rates), not efficiency or payoff. However, the findings suggest that providing players with a costly signalling option can help maintain cooperation in the absence of second-order information, which may be relevant for understanding mechanisms that stabilize cooperation in related environments. The results do not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Ma, L. K., Tunney, R. J., & Ferguson, E. (2014). Gratefully Received, Gratefully Repaid: The Role of Perceived Fairness in Cooperative Interactions. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that recipients of unconditional (free) help perceive the donor as more genuinely helpful, feel more gratitude, and report a higher tendency to reciprocate in the future compared to recipients of conditional (repayment-required) help. Among those who received conditional offers and accepted, 38% defaulted (either partially or fully) on repayment, suggesting a behavioral response to perceived unfairness. The effect of offer conditionality on willingness to reciprocate is serially mediated by perceived helpfulness and gratitude. The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, and the game is a one-shot, dyadic trust game rather than a repeated public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how conditionality (akin to punishment or sanctions) affects willingness to reciprocate and actual repayment behavior in a one-shot, dyadic trust game. It does not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings suggest that perceived fairness and the framing of help (conditional vs. unconditional) can influence cooperation and defaulting, which may be relevant for understanding psychological mechanisms in cooperation, but the results cannot be directly used to predict efficiency changes in public goods games with punishment enabled or disabled.

- source: Rankin, D. J., & Eggimann, F. (2009). The evolution of judgement bias in indirect reciprocity. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that judgement bias—weighting bad acts more heavily than good acts in reputation updating—can evolve in indirect reciprocity models, especially when the benefit-to-cost ratio is low. Under 'standing' reputation rules, negative judgement bias is favored over a wider range of parameters than under 'scoring.' Allowing judgement bias to evolve increases the level of cooperation in the population. The model does not include explicit costly punishment, but negative judgement bias acts as a form of social sanctioning by reducing the likelihood of future help to defectors. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that mechanisms that increase the reputational cost of defection (such as negative judgement bias) can promote cooperation, especially when the cost of cooperation is high. The findings are most relevant for settings where reputation-based indirect reciprocity is the main mechanism, rather than explicit costly punishment. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Hu, Q., Jin, T., Jiang, Y. L., & Liu, X. W. (2024). Reputation incentives with public supervision promote cooperation in evolutionary games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that a reputation incentive mechanism with public supervision can significantly increase the fraction of cooperators in a spatial PDG. The effect is stronger when the evaluation intensity (the range of possible evaluation values) is lower, allowing players to more quickly update their evaluations and reputations. The model demonstrates that reputation-based rewards can compensate cooperators for losses due to defectors, promoting the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, so its findings are limited to behavioral outcomes (cooperation rates) rather than payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that reputation-based reward mechanisms with public supervision can increase cooperation rates in spatial PDG-like environments, especially when evaluation intensity is low. However, because the model is not a public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The findings may be useful for understanding the role of reputation and reward mechanisms as adjacent or complementary to punishment, but should not be used as direct evidence for efficiency outcomes in PGGs with punishment.

- source: Li, G. Y., Du, H. F., & He, X. C. (2024). The evolutionary prisoner's dilemma game in continuous signed networks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that negative relationships, modeled as punitive ties that reduce payoffs for defectors, can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation in networked prisoner's dilemma games. The effect is stronger when the negative coefficient (punishment strength) or the proportion of negative ties is higher. Adaptive networks, where agents can adjust their ties, further facilitate cooperation. Structural balance (alignment of positive ties within groups and negative ties between groups) also supports higher cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff, focusing instead on the behavioral outcome of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment-like mechanisms (here, negative relationships that reduce payoffs) can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas. However, because the main outcome is the proportion of cooperators rather than efficiency or group payoff, and because the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The findings suggest that increasing the cost or prevalence of punishment can promote cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency or welfare.

- source: Shutters, S. T., & Hales, D. (2013). Tag-Mediated Altruism is Contingent on How Cheaters Are Defined. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that the sustainability of tag-mediated altruism is highly sensitive to how cheaters are defined. When cheaters are defined as agents who can never donate (strong cheaters), altruism collapses completely and rapidly. With medium cheaters (who can revert to altruism via mutation), donation rates are very low. Only with ultraweak or weak cheaters do substantial donation rates persist. The paper also finds that parameters such as neighborhood size, number of pairings, and mutation rate affect donation rates, but the presence of strong cheaters overwhelms these effects. The authors suggest that additional mechanisms, such as punishment or multi-level selection, may be necessary to sustain cooperation in the presence of strong cheaters, but these are not modeled directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or close variants. It does not implement punishment or sanctions, nor does it report group efficiency or payoff outcomes. However, it is informative in showing that, in tag-based donation models, the presence of strong cheaters (analogous to unpunished defectors) eliminates cooperation, and that additional mechanisms (such as punishment) may be required to sustain altruism. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that in environments where strong, unpunished defection is possible, efficiency will be low unless additional mechanisms (like punishment) are present. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates or direct manipulation of punishment, so its value for prediction is mainly conceptual and indirect.

- source: Deng, L. L., Zhang, X. X., & Wang, C. (2021). Coevolution of spatial ultimatum game and link weight promotes fairness. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists
  findings: The coevolution of game strategy and link weight in a spatial ultimatum game model leads to higher average proposal and acceptance levels, interpreted as greater fairness. The key driver is the link weight boundary control parameter (\delta), which, when increased, allows for greater heterogeneity in social ties and promotes fairer strategies. The adjustment amplitude (\Delta) has a smaller or context-dependent effect. The model does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its direct relevance to efficiency prediction in public goods or similar games is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The model is an ultimatum game (not a PGG or direct variant), and the main outcomes are behavioral (fairness, proposal/acceptance levels) rather than payoff-based. The 'punishment' in this context is the weakening of social ties, not costly punishment of the type used in PGGs. Therefore, while the findings may be conceptually adjacent—suggesting that dynamic social relationships can promote fairer behavior—they do not inform quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment.

- source: Zhang, Y. L., Yang, S., Chen, X. J., Bai, Y. B., & Xie, G. M. (2023). Reputation update of responders efficiently promotes the evolution of fairness in the ultimatum game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that indirect reciprocity mechanisms based on reputation can strongly promote fairness in the ultimatum game. Specifically, updating responders' reputation via image scoring leads to a very high level of fairness (over 95%), while updating proposers' reputation alone increases fairness above the baseline (no reputation) for many social norms, with a few norms achieving over 80% fairness. When both proposers' and responders' reputations are updated, most social norms yield even higher fairness than updating proposers' reputation alone, but never exceed the effect of updating responders' reputation alone. The results are based on evolutionary game-theoretic modeling and analysis of strategy dynamics, not on empirical or experimental data. The main dependent variable is the frequency of fair offers and rejections of unfair offers, not group payoff or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it analyzes how indirect reciprocity and reputation mechanisms affect fairness (a behavioral outcome) in the ultimatum game, which is a related but distinct environment. The findings suggest that mechanisms analogous to punishment (here, rejection of unfair offers, i.e., altruistic punishment) can strongly promote norm compliance and fairness, especially when reputation is at stake. However, because the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the game is not a public goods game, the results should be used only as weak, indirect evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Podder, S., Righi, S., & Takács, K. (2021). Local reputation, local selection, and the leading eight norms. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the leading eight social norms for indirect reciprocity can maintain high levels of cooperation in networked populations, even when reputation and strategy updating are local rather than global. However, the ability to sustain cooperation depends on the accuracy of reputation transmission, the speed of evolutionary updating, and the specific norm used. Norms that reward justified defection are less robust to noise and invasion by defectors. The main outcome is the proportion of cooperation, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are adjacent: they show that certain reputation-based norms can sustain high cooperation in repeated pairwise Prisoner's Dilemma games on networks, and that the robustness of cooperation depends on information and evolutionary parameters. For prediction tasks about efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper suggests that the structure of information and the harshness of social norms can affect the sustainability of cooperation, but it does not quantify efficiency or payoff outcomes. Use this paper for qualitative context about the importance of information structure and norm design, not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Li, S. L., He, Z. X., Jia, D. Y., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Granting leaders priority exit options promotes and jeopardizes cooperation in social dilemmas. *Neurocomputing*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that granting exit rights to high-degree (leader) nodes in a spatial prisoner's dilemma can either suppress or promote cooperation, depending on the strength of the social dilemma and the network's assortativity. In weak dilemmas, leader-priority exit reduces cooperation, while in strong dilemmas, it can promote cooperation by isolating defectors and allowing cooperative clusters to survive among low-degree nodes. Random and fringe-priority exit have weaker or negligible effects. The main outcome is the relative cooperation ratio among non-exiters. The model does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not a public goods game. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism per se, but can act as a hard sanction by denying payoffs to others.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with an exit option, not a PGG, and the main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The exit option is not a punishment mechanism, though it can function as a hard sanction. The findings may be relevant for understanding how voluntary exclusion or exit options affect cooperation in networked dilemmas, but do not provide direct evidence for the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. Use this paper only for very indirect, mechanistic insights about how exclusion or exit options might interact with network structure and dilemma strength.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Environmental-based defensive promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that allowing cooperators to take defensive actions (at a cost) in response to environmental cues (number of other cooperators) can prevent the collapse to full defection and promote higher levels of cooperation. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the defensive cost, defensive benefit, defensive loss, and the threshold for defensive awareness. When defensive measures are sufficiently beneficial and not too costly, and when cooperators are sufficiently vigilant (high threshold and probability), the system can reach full cooperation or stable coexistence. However, the main reported outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical evidence that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce exploitation (analogous to defensive or sanctioning actions) can increase cooperation rates in social dilemmas. However, because the model is based on the prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and reports cooperation fraction rather than efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited. The results suggest that the effectiveness of defensive/sanctioning mechanisms depends on their cost, benefit, and the conditions under which they are triggered, which may inform the expected direction of punishment effects in related models. However, quantitative predictions for efficiency cannot be extracted from this paper.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2021). Effects of defensive cooperation strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The introduction of a defensive cooperation strategy (where cooperators pay a cost to reduce losses against defectors) can promote and sustain higher levels of cooperation in both well-mixed and structured populations, compared to the standard prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness of this strategy depends on the balance between the defensive cost and defensive benefit. When the defensive benefit exceeds the cost, defensive cooperators can suppress defectors and allow cooperation to persist. However, excessive defensive benefit can lead to second-order free-riding by unconditional cooperators. The model shows cyclic dominance among the three strategies in structured populations. The paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, so its findings are not directly translatable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its model is a theoretical extension of the prisoner's dilemma with a defensive cooperation strategy, which is conceptually adjacent to punishment but not equivalent. The outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not payoff-based. Therefore, while the paper suggests that self-interested defensive strategies can promote cooperation under certain conditions, it does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency or group payoff that can be directly used for the downstream prediction task. Its relevance is limited to mechanism discussion and indirect contextualization.

- source: Santos, F. P., Pacheco, J. M., Paiva, A., & Santos, F. C. (2017). Structural power and the evolution of collective fairness in social networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that the structural power of a network—a measure of how often individuals appear in each other's groups—strongly predicts the evolution of fairer strategies in Multiplayer Ultimatum Games. Networks with high SP (such as regular rings or modular community structures) promote higher average offers and acceptance thresholds, leading to more equitable outcomes. The mechanism is that repeated interactions within overlapping groups allow individuals to exert more influence and enforce fairness, analogous to effective punishment. However, the paper does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral measures of fairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction of efficiency in public-goods-like environments with punishment, by showing that network structure (specifically, high structural power) can promote fairer behavioral outcomes in group bargaining games. However, because the model is based on the Multiplayer Ultimatum Game (not a public goods game), does not include explicit costly punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff, its findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that structural features enabling repeated peer influence may enhance the effectiveness of fairness enforcement (analogous to punishment), but do not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes due to punishment or other design dimensions.

- source: Hock, K., & Fefferman, N. H. (2011). Violating Social Norms when Choosing Friends: How Rule-Breakers Affect Social Networks. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when individuals in a network break social conventions for choosing partners, the effects depend on the convention in use. In 'intermediary-based' (betweenness) networks, rule-breakers primarily affect their own social position, and only at higher frequencies do they impact group organization. In 'popularity-based' (in-degree) networks, rule-breakers do not affect their own or others' individual positions, but as their frequency increases, they significantly alter the overall network structure (decentralization).
