The paper discusses the potential for rule-breaking to transform social systems, but does not measure or report efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, efficiency, or payoff, nor does it use a PGG or close variant. Its findings on how rule-breaking alters network structure may be contextually relevant for understanding the broader consequences of norm violation, but do not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The paper may be useful for mechanism discussion or for understanding the importance of social structure, but not for direct prediction in the target task.

- source: Newton, P. K., & Ma, Y. (2021). Maximizing cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma evolutionary game via optimal control. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent

  findings: The paper shows that by optimally controlling the off-diagonal elements of the prisoner's dilemma payoff matrix (interpreted as dynamically changing incentives and penalties), it is possible to minimize the defector population (maximize cooperation) over a fixed time horizon. The optimal control is bang-bang and depends on the initial state. The reduction in defectors is most efficient for small initial defector populations. The method cycles through a sequence of different games (PD, Leader, Deadlock, etc.) to achieve the optimal outcome. The approach is adaptive over multiple cycles, recalculating the optimal schedule based on the current state. However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and is not based on experimental or empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical study of optimal control in a two-population evolutionary prisoner's dilemma, focusing on minimizing defection via dynamic changes to the payoff matrix. There is no explicit punishment mechanism, no group payoff or efficiency outcome, and the setting is not a public goods game. The findings may be conceptually relevant for understanding how dynamic incentives can shape cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence for the prediction task.

  findings: The study finds that cooperation (helping behavior) among union members is sustained not by explicit punishment, perfect monitoring, or reputation, but by a focal-point equilibrium where members help others in expectation of reciprocal help when they need it. Most members leave the union after their own disputes are resolved, indicating that helping is conditional and self-interested rather than purely altruistic. The equilibrium is supported by direct observation and mutual monitoring during joint activities, but not by formal sanctions or payoff-based incentives. The paper provides qualitative evidence and a theoretical model, but does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.

The findings may be useful for understanding the range of mechanisms that can support cooperation in loosely knit groups, but do not provide quantitative or experimental evidence relevant to the prediction task.

- source: Forber, P., & Smead, R. (2016). The Evolution of Spite, Recognition, and Morality. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that mechanisms enabling recognition and conditional behavior can support both altruism and spite, but with important asymmetries. Recognition based on exogenous signals (e.g., greenbeard effects) is more evolutionarily stable with spite than with altruism, because mimics threaten altruism more than spite. In contrast, reciprocity based on past behavior (as in repeated games) is more conducive to the evolution of altruism than spite. The models show that reciprocal altruism evolves readily in repeated games, while reciprocal spite is rare. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, but rather the evolutionary stability and frequency of these behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its models are adjacent, focusing on the evolutionary conditions for the emergence and stability of conditional harming (spite) and helping (altruism) behaviors, and the coevolution of recognition mechanisms. While it discusses repeated games and conditional strategies, it does not report or predict group efficiency, welfare, or payoff outcomes. The main relevance is conceptual: it suggests that the evolutionary stability of punishment-like behaviors depends on the mechanism of recognition and the type of conditionality (signal-based vs. reciprocity), but it does not provide quantitative or empirical evidence for efficiency effects in PGG-like games.

- source: Yan, M. H., Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2023). Doing what others do does not stabilize continuous norms. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in models where norm-relevant behaviors are continuous (e.g., contribution levels in a public goods context), similarity-based payoffs (including social sanctions or punishment for deviating from the mean) do not stabilize arbitrary or costly norms. Instead, the equilibrium norm is determined by direct effects (such as individual or group payoffs, moral preferences, or cognitive/cultural attractors). Only in discrete norm spaces, or when categorization or multiple cognitive attractors are present, can similarity-based payoffs maintain multiple stable equilibria or persistent between-group differences. For public goods-like environments, this suggests that punishment or social sanctions alone are insufficient to maintain high-cooperation norms unless additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, discrete categories, or multiple optima) are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that, in continuous public goods games or similar environments, enabling punishment or social sanctions (modeled as similarity-based payoffs) will not, by itself, maintain high-efficiency (cooperative) equilibria. The equilibrium will be determined by direct payoff incentives, not by the presence of punishment, unless the game is discretized or there are additional forces (e.g., moral preferences, cognitive attractors) that create multiple optima. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, in continuous-choice PGGs, the presence of punishment may not increase efficiency unless the design includes discrete choices, strong moral framing, or other mechanisms that create stable high-cooperation equilibria. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, but it strongly suggests that punishment alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains in continuous settings.

- source: Jordan, J. J. (2023). A pull versus push framework for reputation. *Trends in Cognitive Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper argues that reputation systems can incentivize cooperation through two distinct mechanisms: 'pull' (where evaluators are motivated to cooperate with good actors for direct reciprocal benefit) and 'push' (where evaluators are motivated by normative pressure to cooperate with good actors to maintain their own reputation). It reviews how these mechanisms map onto different game-theoretic models (e.g., coordination games, indirect reciprocity) and discusses their implications for psychology, behavior, and the design of reputation-based interventions. The framework is used to interpret existing empirical findings and to generate predictions about when and how reputation will promote cooperation or punishment, but does not test these predictions directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it offers a conceptual framework that may help interpret when and why reputation, cooperation, and punishment mechanisms (including those in PGGs) are likely to be effective, depending on whether incentives are primarily 'pull' or 'push'. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the structure of the game (e.g., whether it is a coordination game, whether social norms are salient, whether actions are observable) will moderate the effectiveness of reputation and punishment, but does not provide quantitative estimates or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yang, Z. H., & Zhang, L. P. (2023). Random migration with tie retention promotes cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that tie retention (the ability to keep social ties with ex-neighbors after migration) can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma game, even when migration is random. The maximum number of retained ex-neighbors (m) is a key parameter: higher m leads to higher cooperation rates. Social diversity in tie retention (variation in m across players) further enhances cooperation. Migration probability (p) has a non-monotonic effect: lower p generally favors cooperation, but diversity in p can create optimal conditions for cooperation. The results challenge the previous view that random migration is always detrimental to cooperation. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or model punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing that network structure, migration, and tie retention can strongly affect cooperation rates in the prisoner's dilemma game. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that social stickiness (tie retention) and migration patterns are important moderators of cooperation, but it does not inform the quantitative effect of punishment on efficiency.

The findings are relevant for understanding the emergence and maintenance of cooperation in dynamic networks but do not directly inform the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games.

Punishment is discussed as a behavioral cue, but its role is primarily in signaling trustworthiness rather than as a mechanism for increasing efficiency or group payoff. The review highlights that communication and increased mutual benefit (e.g., higher returns to cooperation) are situational factors that increase both actual and expected cooperation. However, the review does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it systematically analyze the impact of specific game design dimensions (such as punishment cost, MPCR, or player count) on efficiency. - source: Palomar, E., Alcaide, A., Ribagorda, A., & Zhang, Y. (2012). The Peer's Dilemma: A general framework to examine cooperation in pure peer-to-peer systems. *Computer Networks*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated RSP game modeling P2P interactions, the introduction of a 'loner' strategy (voluntary withdrawal) allows for stable coexistence of cooperators, non-cooperators, and loners, preventing the collapse of cooperation even in the absence of incentives or punishment. The system exhibits cyclic dominance of strategies, and the stability of cooperation is sensitive to factors such as population size, network overlay dynamism, and initial population composition. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes. Simulations show that large, weakly dynamic populations are more likely to sustain stable cycles, while small or highly dynamic overlays are prone to domination by a single strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main relevance is in showing that, in a repeated game with voluntary participation (the 'loner' option), stable cooperation can emerge without explicit punishment or reward. The model is adjacent to PGGs but differs in key respects: it is a zero-sum RSP game, not a public goods game, and does not manipulate or analyze punishment, reward, or efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding the role of voluntary participation and cyclic dominance in sustaining cooperation.

  findings: The paper finds that introducing quasi-cooperators (who are intermediate between cooperators and defectors) can, under certain parameter regimes, help maintain cooperation by resisting defectors. The model does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

- source: Yukalov, V. I., Yukalova, E. P., & Sornette, D. (2013). Utility Rate Equations of Group Population Dynamics in Biological and Social Systems. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The authors develop a system-theoretic, utility-based model for the evolution of group fractions (cooperators, defectors, regulators) in self-organized societies. They show analytically that, under resource constraints, neither defectors nor regulators can exceed about 10% of the population in stable equilibrium. The model demonstrates that cooperation can persist without explicit punishment mechanisms, provided resources are finite. When regulators (punishers) are present, their fraction is also limited by resource costs. The model is applied to real-world data from countries, but only to estimate group fractions and stability, not efficiency or payoff. No direct predictions or measurements of efficiency, group payoff, or surplus are made.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar experimental designs. It offers a theoretical perspective that, in resource-constrained societies, both defectors and regulators (punishers) are limited in equilibrium, and cooperation can be stable even without explicit punishment. However, the model does not map onto the game design dimensions or efficiency outcomes used in experimental PGG research. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like environments, but may provide indirect, high-level context about the limits of punishment and defection in self-organized systems.

The authors suggest that these evolved mechanisms are central to risk perception and cooperation, and that they help explain why people often resist narrowly self-interested, rational-maximizing behavior in favor of fairness and contract maintenance. However, the paper does not provide new empirical data or direct analysis of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

  findings: The paper argues that advocates of strong reciprocity (SR) treat SR as a proximate mechanism for human cooperation, not as an ultimate explanation. It clarifies misunderstandings in the literature about the explanatory role of SR, especially in relation to public goods experiments with punishment. The paper discusses how gene-culture co-evolutionary theory is used to provide ultimate explanations for the evolution of SR, while neuroeconomics is invoked for proximate explanations. It does not present new empirical findings or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but references existing experimental work to illustrate the existence and behavioral effects of SR.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is useful for understanding the conceptual and explanatory framework surrounding punishment and cooperation, and for clarifying that the presence of punishment in public goods experiments is interpreted as evidence for strong reciprocity as a proximate mechanism. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative effects on efficiency, payoff, or welfare, and thus offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Heath, J., & Rioux, C. (2018). Recent trends in evolutionary ethics: greenbeards!. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that many recent philosophical and scientific models that attempt to explain large-scale human cooperation by introducing commitment or punishment mechanisms (such as strong reciprocity) tacitly rely on greenbeard mechanisms, which are considered implausible in the human case due to their vulnerability to exploitation by 'falsebeards.' The authors argue that these models do not provide a satisfactory solution to the problem of human ultrasociality and that the evolution of cooperation remains an open question. The paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative outcomes relevant to efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance to the prediction task is limited to theoretical critique: it cautions that models relying on punishment to sustain cooperation may be structurally vulnerable unless they avoid greenbeard-like mechanisms. For prediction tasks based on game design dimensions and control efficiency, this paper offers only indirect, high-level theoretical context and does not inform parameter-level predictions.

- source: Hart, E., Mellers, B. A., & Bicchieri, C. (2019). Bad luck or bad intentions: When do third parties reveal offenders' intentions to victims?. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Third party observers are more likely to inform victims when harm is accidental than when it is intentional. Moral anger increases the likelihood of informing about intentional harm, while empathy increases the likelihood of informing in both cases. Observers are more likely to inform when they believe the victim's beliefs about the offender's intent are inaccurate, and when the information has consequences for future interactions (e.g., potential for punishment). However, the paper does not study or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods game environments. The design is adjacent in that it uses economic games and discusses punishment and fairness, but the dependent variables are about information sharing, not payoffs or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like settings.

- source: Kim, M., & Sivan, E. W. Z. (2017). Faking It Is Hard to Do: Entrepreneurial Norm Enforcement and Suspicions of Deviance. *Sociological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that when norm enforcement is mandated (i.e., actors are required to respond to deviance), enforcers are perceived as more sincere and less likely to be suspected of ulterior motives, even in high-accusability contexts. In contrast, when enforcement is entrepreneurial (i.e., voluntary and not mandated) and the context is high accusability (where actors have reason to fear being accused of deviance), enforcers are viewed with greater suspicion and are more likely to be seen as having ulterior motives. In low-accusability contexts, entrepreneurial enforcement does not arouse suspicion. The study does not measure or report on efficiency, group payoff, or any economic outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about social perceptions of norm enforcers, not about behavioral or payoff consequences of punishment. The design is adjacent to PGG-like environments in that it involves norm enforcement and public goods logic, but it is not an economic game and does not manipulate or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGGs, but may be useful for understanding psychological or reputational moderators of norm enforcement.

- source: Lin, C. A., & Bates, T. C. (2022). Free to choose: Mutualist motives for partner choice, proportional division, punishment, and help. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that mutualism is a reliable, stable, and discriminant psychological construct, with four components: partner choice, proportional sharing, proportional punishment, and proportional helping. Higher mutualism scores predict attitudinal opposition to equal sharing and redistribution, and preference for proportional division in hypothetical scenarios. The mutualism scale is largely independent of compassion, envy, self-interest, and utilitarianism. The findings are robust across two large samples. However, all outcomes are attitudinal or psychometric; there is no evidence on actual behavior in public goods games, punishment games, or on efficiency/welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It may be useful for understanding individual differences in motives that could influence behavior in such games, but it does not report any behavioral or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe game design dimensions. Thus, it should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment interventions. Its main value is in providing a validated measure of mutualist motives, which could be used in future studies that do measure relevant outcomes.

- source: Waldeck, R. (2013). Segregated Cooperation. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with internalized norm adherence (modeled as a private emotional cost for defecting), local interaction structures (small neighborhoods) can lead to persistent, segregated clusters of cooperators, resulting in higher average fitness (payoff) for cooperators compared to defectors. This effect is robust to various simulation parameters (error rate, number of active players, population size) but is highly sensitive to the distribution of emotional adherence and the cost of cooperation. When the basin of attraction for defection is large (e.g., high cost of cooperation or unfavorable emotion distribution), cooperation collapses even in local structures. No explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present; all norm enforcement is via internalized emotion.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or PD games, as it does not include any explicit punishment mechanism. Instead, it models norm adherence as an internalized emotional cost. The findings are relevant for understanding how local interaction structure and heterogeneity in norm adherence can affect efficiency and cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of enabling punishment. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the efficiency impact of punishment interventions, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in environments with internalized norm enforcement and local interactions.

- source: Bravo, G. (2010). Voluntary contribution to public goods in mutual-help forums: reciprocity or group attachment?. *Socio-economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that the success of the mutual-help forum depends on the complementary roles of a small core of altruistic contributors and a larger group of reciprocators. Reciprocity is a significant motivator for most users, while the core group is driven by intrinsic motivation and group attachment. The forum achieves high performance in terms of questions answered and response speed, but this is not analyzed in terms of efficiency or group payoff. There is no evidence of formal or informal punishment mechanisms affecting contribution or efficiency. The findings suggest that, in the absence of sanctioning, a privileged core group can sustain cooperation and public good provision in online communities.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides limited direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a real-world mutual-help forum without any explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how cooperation can be sustained without punishment, especially through reciprocity and intrinsic motivation. However, the absence of efficiency or payoff outcomes, and the lack of any punishment treatment, mean that this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful as contextual evidence for baseline cooperation in the absence of sanctions.

- source: Bernhard, R. M., & Cushman, F. (2022). Extortion, intuition, and the dark side of reciprocity. *Cognition*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The experiment finds that both communication and subsidies for NCH can shift groups toward more cooperative, higher-efficiency outcomes, but also that some groups coordinate on Nash equilibria (heavy spraying) that are also efficient in the game’s payoff structure. Group-level characteristics are more predictive of cooperative behavior than individual traits. The game does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so no evidence is provided about the effects of punishment on efficiency or cooperation. The findings are most relevant for understanding the effects of communication and positive incentives (subsidies) on coordination and efficiency in public-goods-like environments, but not for punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does provide informative evidence about the effects of communication and subsidies, and about the importance of group-level characteristics for coordination and efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative. For tasks involving communication or positive incentives, the findings are more relevant.

- source: Liu, G. F., Lin, C. D., & Xin, Z. Q. (2014). The Effects of Within- and Between-Group Competition on Trust and Trustworthiness among Acquaintances. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both within-group and between-group competition increase trust (investment) among acquaintances in an investment game. However, within-group competition reduces trustworthiness (return ratio) and leads to lower payoffs for trustors but higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trustee payoffs but does not significantly affect trustworthiness or trustor payoffs compared to the non-competition condition. The results suggest that competition type differentially affects efficiency for trustors and trustees, and that within-group competition can undermine overall group efficiency by harming trustor payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in the absence of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, introducing within-group competition can increase trust but reduce trustworthiness, leading to lower payoffs for trustors and higher payoffs for trustees. Between-group competition increases trust and trustee payoffs but does not harm trustor payoffs. Since the study does not include punishment, it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that competitive structures can have asymmetric effects on efficiency depending on the role. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence or absence of competition (and its type) can moderate efficiency outcomes, but the absence of punishment means the results are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., & André, J. B. (2022). Cooperation as a signal of time preferences. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which cooperation is a signal of time preferences in a repeated trust game. Individuals with lower discount rates (more future-oriented) are more likely to cooperate, and populations with lower average discount rates can sustain cooperation in a wider range of parameter settings (e.g., lower cost-benefit ratios, less reliable observation). The model provides payoff-based conditions for the evolutionary stability of cooperation and explains variability in cooperation and trust as a function of time preferences. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game is modeled. The main outcome is the equilibrium pay-off for different strategies and the conditions under which cooperation is stable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or a public goods game. However, it is relevant for understanding how individual and population-level time preferences can affect the stability and variability of cooperation and aggregate pay-offs in repeated trust-based interactions. For prediction tasks involving punishment or public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative, suggesting that time preferences and the structure of observation and reputation can moderate cooperation and efficiency, but it does not address punishment or sanctions.

- source: Johnson, T., & Smirnov, O. (2018). Inequality as information: Wealth homophily facilitates the evolution of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a well-mixed population playing repeated one-shot prisoners' dilemma games, a strategy that uses wealth homophily (COEQUALS) can successfully invade and dominate a population of defectors, leading to high levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs (efficiency). The success of COEQUALS is robust to noise, population size, number of rounds, and the presence of certain mimics. The key moderator is the ability to observe the wealth of a sufficient portion of the population: when COEQUALS agents can view at least 10-20% of the population's wealth, the strategy spreads widely and average payoffs increase. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings suggest that visible inequality can serve as an informational mechanism to support cooperation and efficiency, even in the absence of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is directly informative for predicting efficiency in repeated prisoners' dilemma environments where agents can condition their cooperation on visible wealth (past payoffs), but it does not address punishment or sanctions. The findings suggest that mechanisms enabling positive assortment (e.g., wealth homophily) can substitute for punishment in supporting high efficiency. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is less directly relevant, but it does highlight the importance of informational cues and partner selection. The results also indicate that efficiency is robust to variations in player count, number of rounds, and noise, but is sensitive to the ability to observe others' payoffs. If a public goods game allows for similar informational mechanisms, efficiency may be high even without punishment.

- source: Li, C., Xu, H. D., & Fan, S. H. (2020). Synergistic effects of self-optimization and imitation rules on the evolution of cooperation in the investor sharing game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the proposed dynamic strategy updating rule can substantially increase the density of cooperators in the investor sharing game, especially at low aspiration levels. The self-optimization rule increases the probability that defectors with high investment become cooperators, which then promotes further imitation of cooperation. Lower market monopoly (parameter alpha) and more random network structure also boost cooperation, but the effect of network randomness is stronger. Average payoffs for cooperators can exceed those for defectors at high cooperation rates, but group efficiency is not directly analyzed. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform prediction of punishment effects on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings on cooperation rates and average payoffs under different strategy updating rules and aspiration levels may be of indirect interest for understanding how endogenous learning rules affect cooperation, but it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment, nor does it report group efficiency as defined for the prediction task.

- source: Ibbotson, P., Jimenez-Romero, C., & Page, K. M. (2022). Dying to cooperate: the role of environmental harshness in human collaboration. *Behavioral Ecology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that environmental harshness is a key moderator of cooperative behavior in coordination games like the Stag Hunt. In both models and human experiments, individuals are more likely to choose cooperative, high-risk strategies when the environment is harsh and survival is threatened. Communication between agents (or players) allows for better coordination and increases the viability of cooperation. The study does not include punishment, reward, or sanction mechanisms, and does not use a public goods game structure, but rather a coordination game with binary choices and pairwise matching. The main outcomes are behavioral (strategy choice and switching), with survival probability and energy reserves as related but not directly group efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does, however, provide informative context about how environmental harshness and communication affect the viability of cooperation in coordination games. Since punishment and reward are not present, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the role of environmental context and communication in cooperation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment.

- source: Chen, W. M., Quan, J., Wang, X. J., & Liu, Y. (2023). Evolutionary dynamics from fluctuating environments with deterministic and stochastic noises. *Nonlinear Dynamics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model of evolutionary dynamics in the hawk-dove game under fluctuating environments with both payoff and demographic noise. Through simulation, it finds that both deterministic and stochastic fluctuations can promote cooperation, sometimes even reversing selection so that cooperation dominates. Deterministic fluctuations are more effective than stochastic ones at promoting cooperation. The mechanism is that noise can create unfair time-averaged payoffs that favor cooperation. High variance in noise promotes cooperation but makes its maintenance less stable. The results are robust to the type of noise distribution. The paper briefly extends the analysis to the prisoner's dilemma, finding similar qualitative results. No public goods game, punishment, or explicit group efficiency analysis is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not study public goods games, punishment, or group efficiency. Its findings about noise and cooperation in the hawk-dove and prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how environmental uncertainty affects cooperation, but not for the specific prediction task involving punishment and efficiency.

- source: Nockur, L., & Pfattheicher, S. (2021). The Beautiful Complexity of Human Prosociality: On the Interplay of Honesty-Humility, Intuition, and a Reward System. *Social Psychological and Personality Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reward system (the possibility of being rewarded by a third party) increases the likelihood and amount of giving in a dictator game. Honesty-Humility is also positively associated with giving. Intuitive decision making does not have a main effect, but among individuals low in Honesty-Humility, intuition reduces giving when no reward system is present. The reward system effect is robust across personality and decision-making style. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It does provide evidence that a reward system (not punishment) increases prosocial behavior in a dictator game, but the outcome is individual giving, not group efficiency. The design is not a PGG or close variant, and there is no analysis of efficiency or related payoff outcomes. Thus, its value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is very limited.

- source: Horiuchi, S. (2015). Emergence and Collapse of the Norm of Resource Sharing Around Locally Abundant Resources. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that workers' effort (reciprocity) increases with their own wage (direct reciprocity) and, when their own wage is unobservable, also with the coworker's wage (indirect reciprocity via signaling). When both wages are observable, the coworker's wage has no effect on effort, indicating that indirect reciprocity is driven by signaling about one's own treatment, not by altruism toward the coworker. There is no evidence that inequality aversion explains the observed patterns. The main outcome is effort, not efficiency or group payoff. No punishment or sanctioning is present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is adjacent in that it studies reciprocity and information in a gift-exchange game, but the main outcome is effort, not efficiency, and there is no punishment mechanism. The findings may inform predictions about the role of information and signaling in reciprocity, but not about punishment or efficiency. Thus, its value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency is limited.

- source: Ma, S. M., Zhang, B. Y., Cao, S. N., Liu, J. S., & Wang, W. X. (2021). Limited memory optimizes cooperation in social dilemma experiments. *Royal Society Open Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that providing players with memory of their own and their partner's actions from previous rounds (memory length L) in a repeated, randomized-pair Prisoner's Dilemma leads to a non-monotonic effect on cooperation: cooperation rates are lowest with no memory (L=0), increase sharply with minimal memory (L=1), and peak at intermediate memory (L=2), then decline with longer memory (L=3,4). The behavioral mechanism is memory-based conditional cooperation, where most subjects cooperate if their partner's recent cooperation rate is high. Theoretical models (replicator dynamics and Markov processes) fit the observed cooperation rates well. No punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes are studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant for understanding how memory and information about past actions affect cooperation rates in repeated dyadic social dilemmas, but it does not address group payoff, efficiency, or the impact of punishment or reward mechanisms. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that memory length (information about past actions) can have a non-monotonic effect on cooperation rates, but provides no evidence about efficiency or about punishment effects. It is not directly informative for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like environments.

- source: Geng, Y. N., Lu, Y. K., Hong, L. J., & Shi, L. (2025). Evolution of cooperation with asymmetric rewards. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that asymmetric rewards in a two-population prisoner's dilemma model can sustain cooperation and prevent total defection, especially in structured populations (lattices). The effect of asymmetry on cooperation depends on the dilemma strength: under strong dilemma (high temptation to defect), asymmetry promotes cooperation; under weak dilemma, strong asymmetry can actually inhibit cooperation. The model includes both prosocial and antisocial rewards but does not include punishment or sanctions. The outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation rates and the coexistence of different strategies, not in terms of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not study punishment, public goods games, or efficiency/welfare outcomes. Its findings on asymmetric rewards in the prisoner's dilemma may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) can sustain cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment or efficiency. The game structure, outcome measures, and intervention type (reward, not punishment) are all different from the target prediction context.

When only pro-social reward is allowed, defectors tend to dominate; when only anti-social reward is allowed, cooperators can dominate. The model does not include punishment or negative sanctions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The results are based on simulations and theoretical analysis of cooperation rates, not on empirical or experimental data.

It models a prisoner's dilemma with unidirectional reward (not punishment) in a networked setting, and reports only on cooperation rates, not efficiency or payoff. The findings may be indirectly relevant for understanding how positive incentives (rewards) affect cooperation, but do not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The game structure is adjacent to, but not the same as, a public goods game, and the outcome measures are not payoff-based.

  findings: The study finds that in one-shot, anonymous ultimatum games, proposer offers vary significantly across villages, but responder behavior is highly uniform: almost all offers are accepted, and responders' self-reported minimum acceptable offers are very low. There is no evidence for cultural conformity or coordinated expectations at the ethno-linguistic group level. The findings suggest that local environmental factors, rather than group-level fairness norms, may drive variation in bargaining behavior. The study does not include punishment or analyze efficiency, welfare, or group payoff.

It does not include punishment or sanctions, and does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings about variation in bargaining behavior and the lack of coordinated expectations in the ultimatum game may be contextually informative for understanding heterogeneity in social preferences, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Ramazi, P., Hessel, J., & Cao, M. (2015). How Feeling Betrayed Affects Cooperation. *Plos One*.

Increasing the number of agents with a lower betrayal factor does not always increase cooperation; there are critical points where the population becomes robust to further increases. The models show that the equilibrium cooperation rate can be predicted from the distribution of emotional types, but the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or the effect of punishment. The findings are relevant for understanding how emotional payoffs shape cooperation in social dilemmas, but do not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games.

- source: Li, S. L., Hong, L. J., Geng, Y. N., & Shen, C. (2020). Popularity-driven fitness calculation promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that integrating popularity into the fitness function (via a tunable parameter alpha) in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can significantly promote cooperation. When alpha is positive, popular individuals (those whose neighbors share their strategy) gain higher fitness, leading to the formation and expansion of cooperative clusters and, in some cases, full cooperation. When alpha is negative, isolated cooperators can persist but do not dominate. The results are supported by both numerical simulations and mean-field theoretical analysis. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, nor does it include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes. Its findings are only indirectly relevant, suggesting that mechanisms which enhance the fitness of popular strategies can promote cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the efficiency effects of punishment. The game studied is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, and the outcome of interest is the fraction of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency.

However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcome, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.

  findings: The study finds that introducing a dynamic, reputation-based teaching ability mechanism into spatial evolutionary games (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators across a wide range of parameters and network structures. The mechanism works by making players more likely to adopt strategies from neighbors with higher reputation, which itself is updated based on cooperative or defective actions. - source: Zhang, M., Wang, S. Y., Hu, X. T., & Alfaro-Bittner, K. (2021). Local and global information affect cooperation in networked Prisoner's dilemma games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The study finds that providing players with global information about their opponents' strategies, or allowing them to choose between local and global information, increases the frequency of cooperation compared to providing only local information. In the local information condition, cooperation declines steadily over time. In the global and discretionary conditions, some sessions show a reversal with increasing cooperation in later rounds. Players strongly prefer global information when given a choice. The experiment does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it studies a networked Prisoner's Dilemma without punishment or reward. Its main relevance is indirect: it suggests that the type and scope of information available to players can influence cooperation rates, which may in turn affect efficiency in related games. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper offers only weak, contextual support for the prediction task.

- source: Lu, K., Wu, B., Li, M. C., & Wang, Z. (2014). Other-regarding preference causing ping-pong effect in self-questioning game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  findings: The paper finds that increasing the weight of other-regarding preferences (alpha) promotes cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma with synchronous updating. For low alpha, cooperation collapses; for high alpha, cooperation dominates. At intermediate alpha, a 'ping-pong effect' emerges, with large fluctuations in the fraction of cooperators. The study analyzes the micro-dynamics of strategy switching but does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not include punishment, reward, or efficiency outcomes, and the game structure is a spatial prisoner's dilemma with a self-questioning update rule, not a public goods game. - source: Du, F. Q., & Fu, F. (2013). Quantifying the impact of noise on macroscopic organization of cooperation in spatial games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that the effect of noise (randomness in strategy updating) on cooperation and spatial assortment differs between the spatial prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games. In the prisoner's dilemma, intermediate noise maximizes local assortment and cooperator cluster size, while in the snowdrift game, noise generally weakens assortment and leads to smaller clusters at intermediate noise. The paper does not address public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is relevant only in that it discusses spatial structure and noise in related two-player games (prisoner's dilemma, snowdrift), but does not include group interactions, punishment, or payoff-based efficiency outcomes. Thus, it offers little direct support for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Miranda-Rodríguez, R. A., Leenen, I., Han, H., Palafox-Palafox, G., & García-Rodríguez, G. (2023). Moral reasoning and moral competence as predictors of cooperative behavior in a social dilemma. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in an iterated stranger PDG, the probability of cooperation is strongly influenced by the outcome of previous rounds: negative experiences (sucker, punishment, temptation) reduce cooperation, while positive experiences (reward) increase it. However, individuals with higher levels of moral reasoning (DIT-2 N2-index) and moral competence (MCT C-score) are less likely to reduce cooperation after negative experiences, particularly after sucker outcomes. There is no main effect of moral reasoning or competence on overall cooperation rates, but both moderate the effect of negative experiences. The study does not examine efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff. Its findings are limited to individual-level predictors of cooperation in a repeated PDG without punishment or reward. The study is only indirectly relevant for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, and does not inform how punishment would affect efficiency or payoff outcomes under different game design dimensions.

This association is robust to controls for prior GPA, attendance, social status, and other confounders, and is confirmed using a difference-in-differences approach. The effect is stronger for students in the top 20% of reciprocated cooperation. The experiment does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the game.

- source: Przepiorka, W., & Aksoy, O. (2021). Does Herding Undermine the Trust Enhancing Effect of Reputation? An Empirical Investigation with Online-Auction Data. *Social Forces*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: Using a large dataset of eBay auctions, the study shows that sellers with more positive reputations are more likely to sell their items and at higher prices, while negative reputations reduce these outcomes. Buyers exhibit herding behavior, being more likely to bid on items that already have bids, but this effect peaks and then declines as the number of bidders increases. Importantly, even when herding, buyers still consider seller reputation, and the effect of reputation on sales and price remains significant. There is no evidence that herding undermines the efficiency or trust-enhancing effect of reputation systems in this market context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that reputation mechanisms (a form of informal sanctioning) increase efficiency in online market environments by raising both the probability of sale and the price sellers can obtain. While not a public goods game or direct punishment setting, the findings are highly relevant for predicting the effect of reputation-based sanctions on efficiency in environments where trust and cooperation are important. The results suggest that, even in the presence of herding (social influence), reputation systems continue to enhance efficiency. However, the market context differs from standard PGGs, so direct quantitative transfer should be cautious.

  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish a norm violator (by voting against her) varies by socio-professional category: future teachers are significantly more likely to punish than future police officers or high school students, despite all groups recognizing the immoral behavior. The punishment is costless and anonymous, and the context is a simulated music competition, not an economic or public goods game. The findings challenge the idea of a universal, context-independent motivation to punish norm violators. No efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes are measured or discussed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games or similar economic games. Its findings are about context-specific willingness to punish in a non-economic, real-world scenario, and do not inform the relationship between punishment and efficiency or payoff. It may be relevant for understanding moderators of punishment motivation, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

  findings: Exposure to centralized deterrence institutions (ticket inspections and sanctions) increases subsequent unethical behavior (dishonesty) in a different context, for both previous rule-breakers and compliers. The effect is robust to controls and is stronger with larger inspection teams, suggesting a possible normative or signaling mechanism. The study does not use a public goods game or report any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are about behavioral spillovers of punishment institutions, not about their effect on efficiency or group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not manipulate or report on any of the key PGG design dimensions. Its findings are about behavioral spillovers of deterrence institutions on individual honesty in unrelated contexts. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task, though it may be of conceptual interest for understanding broader effects of punishment institutions.
